*Stage 1 research, 2026-09-26. Written before any lesson is drafted. Five researchers each worked
one part of the subject in parallel, in a fresh context, from a shared brief. Part A covers the
textbooks, the listening-studies field, syllabi, the popular books a learner will meet, and where
the stock listening statistics come from. Part B covers how conversation works: conversation
analysis, turn timing across languages, interruption and gender, questions, the liking gap,
endings, depth and talking to strangers. Part C covers listening research: Rogers and active
listening, the paraphrasing experiments, the Gottman controversy, the high-quality listening
programme, supportive messages and advice, and empathy. Part D covers nonverbal communication:
Mehrabian's 7-38-55, lie detection, faces, mimicry and power posing, culture and gaze, and autism
and the double empathy problem. Part E covers what practitioners teach (crisis services, clinical
communication, motivational interviewing, negotiators and mediators, interviewers, dialogue across
difference, talking with older people and people with hearing loss or a stammer) and the safety
guidance. The five parts are brought together here. The synthesiser re-checked three figures
against the researchers' raw extracts: Mehrabian's two sentences on his own website, the 1.59% in
Mastroianni et al. 2021, and Heritage et al. 2007's 90.3% versus 53.1%. All three agree with the
parts.*

**This course sits in a standards 3.4 domain (psychology) without being flagged as one.**
`course.yaml` has `sensitive_domain: false`, and most of the course is uncontroversial skill
teaching. But four places touch contested or identity-laden questions and each lesson that
touches them should get the neutrality pass whatever the flag says:

- **Gender and interruption** (Part B §3, (c) C3). The popular claim and the meta-analytic
  finding differ, and both sides of the scholarly argument (dominance versus style) have
  proponents who would recognise a strawman at once.
- **Culture** (Part B C4, Part D §5). National labels ("high-context", Hofstede scores) predict
  individuals poorly. A lesson that says "the Japanese do X" fails the specificity test and risks
  a stereotype; say what was measured, in whom, and how large the difference was.
- **Autism and eye contact** (Part D §6). The double empathy account and its 2025 registered
  replication disagree. There is a value dimension in how autism is described; use the language
  the sources use and present both readings.
- **Talking across political difference** (Part E §6). Whether cross-partisan dialogue changes
  minds is contested empirical; what such talk is *for* is partly a value question. The course
  takes no side on any political issue and describes Braver Angels and Essential Partners in their
  own terms.

Stage 2 should decide whether to set `sensitive_domain: true` outright. The synthesiser's view is
that a per-lesson neutrality pass on those four areas is enough, and that is what the outline
should record.

**How to read the quotation marks.** The five parts use one convention:

- **[V]**: copied from text the researcher extracted directly with curl and pdftotext or pandoc,
  or through the PubMed, Europe PMC, OpenAlex, Crossref or ERIC APIs.
- **[V, OCR]** or **[V-OCR]**: from the researcher's own OCR of a scan (Zimmerman and West 1975,
  Pomerantz 1984, Gottman et al. 1998, Rossano, Brown and Levinson 2009). Check against the page
  image before quoting in a lesson.
- **[V-abstract]** or **[V, ERIC abstract]** (Parts C and D): verbatim from an abstract returned by
  an API. The paper itself was not read.
- **[T]**: returned through WebFetch and so not guaranteed verbatim. Used only in Part A (a few
  places) and Part D (the GAO product page). Re-get with curl before quoting.
- **Snippet only**: seen only in a search result. Nothing so marked is in quotation marks, and
  nothing so marked may be stated in a lesson as a finding.

Quoted text in the parts keeps the source's own punctuation, including dashes. A lesson that quotes
such a line keeps the author's dash (courses/CLAUDE.md rule 4) or picks another sentence.

**The raw extracts are not durable.** The parts name files under a scratchpad `cl/A/` to `cl/E/`.
That folder is session scratch and will not survive. The quotations were checked when they were
read. A drafter who needs a quoted line re-reads the live source, not a raw file.

## Access and provenance problems a drafter inherits

1. **The textbooks were read as tables of contents, publisher outlines and prefaces only** (Part
   A §1). Adler, Proctor and Manning, *Looking Out, Looking In*; Wood, *Interpersonal
   Communication: Everyday Encounters*; Adler and Proctor, *Interplay*; DeVito, *The Interpersonal
   Communication Book*; McCornack and Morrison, *Reflect & Relate*. Wood's listening chapter is
   known only from student notes, and Adler's listening chapter outline is from the 13th edition,
   not the current 16th. A lesson may name them as further reading but must not attribute content
   to them beyond what Part A records.
2. **Taylor and Francis, Wiley, SAGE, ResearchGate and Academia returned Cloudflare challenges or
   403s** for every full-text request (Parts A, C, D). This hit the listening journals hardest: the
   *International Journal of Listening* papers (Weger et al. 2010 and 2014) and several PSPB papers
   in the Itzchakov programme are abstract only. Graham Bodie's self-archived papers were readable
   and Part C used them.
3. **PNAS returns 403 to curl** (Part B). Stivers et al. 2009, Mastroianni et al. 2021 and Tamir
   and Mitchell 2012 were read from PubMed Central; Templeton et al. 2022 from an author copy.
4. **The two 1967 Mehrabian papers are paywalled and were not obtained** (Part D §1). Their designs
   are reported from secondary sources and are gated. His own website statement was read in full.
   *Silent Messages* is lending-only at the Internet Archive and was not read.
5. **Bond and DePaulo 2006 is a scanned text with character errors** (Part D). Its figures are
   given as paraphrase, not quotation. Check the publisher PDF before quoting.
6. **Blocked or missing pages:** GAO PDFs (Akamai "Access Denied"), Samaritans' "difficult
   conversation" tips (404), the NHS suicide page (404), the Stuttering Foundation's advice (404),
   the Library of Congress Veterans History Project guidance (403), Smithsonian Folklife's guide
   (403), Quote Investigator, JSTOR (Rankin), and Derber's books (borrow-only). Part E used the
   Smithsonian Institution Archives guide, STAMMA and the other Samaritans pages instead.
7. **Two papers have a Francesca Gino co-authorship.** Huang et al. 2017 was independently audited
   and corrected in 2025 ("The audit confirmed that all the conclusions in the paper are valid"
   [V, PubMed record]); take any statistic from the corrected results at https://osf.io/rymv8/, not
   the 2017 PDF. Yeomans et al. 2020 (conversational receptiveness) has no audit notice that Part B
   could find. Check Retraction Watch and the journal page before a lesson relies on it.
8. **Everything with a date on it moves.** Crisis line numbers and service pages (re-check on
   publication day; reuse the Mental Fitness callout); the status of the double empathy findings
   after the 2025 registered replication; the Itzchakov and Bodie 2026 review and the 2026 four-lab
   preprint (DeMarree et al.), both very recent.

**The `unread:` line is the build's enforcement.** It combines the five parts' UNREAD lists,
deduplicated. The build matches each entry as a whole string, case-sensitively. These changes were
made so that no source that **was** read is blocked:

- **Huang et al 2017** (Part A) is **off the line**. Part B read it in full, with its 2025
  correction.
- **Weger et al. 2010** and **Weger et al. 2014** (Part D) are **off the line**. Part C read both
  abstracts and records what each found; their effect sizes and message content are gated (Part C
  gate 1). Part C's "full text" entries for both stay on.
- Part A's textbook entries are written as "... full text" so that a lesson can still name the
  book as further reading. That limit is enforced by the fact-check, not the build.

## The first things Stage 1 found, and they shape the course

These are the findings a drafter most needs in mind before writing a word. Each is sourced in the
part named.

1. **Two of the most repeated listening statistics have no solid source.** "We spend 45% of our
   communication time listening" traces to Rankin's 1920s self-report diaries of 68 Detroit adults.
   The listening field gives his published figure as 42%; the 45% is Nichols and Stevens' 1957
   retelling, and Nichols himself wrote that the survey "may mean little if harshly analyzed" [V].
   The latest student update (Janusik and Wolvin 2009, abstract) found 24%. "We remember 50% at
   once and 25% after two months" comes from lecture-comprehension tests, not conversation, and
   Nichols' own 1948 study found 68% (via Worthington and Bodie [V]). (Part A §3.) **A lesson may
   say listening has been reported as the most-used communication activity; it may not state any
   single percentage as a fact.**
2. **Mehrabian limits his own formula.** His website gives "Total Liking = 7% Verbal Liking + 38%
   Vocal Liking + 55% Facial Liking" [V] and then says "Unless a communicator is talking about
   their feelings or attitudes, these equations are not applicable." [V] The figures come from two
   small 1967 studies of single spoken words and posed photographs. (Part D §1.) This is the
   cleanest worked example in the course of a real finding stretched into a myth.
3. **Demeanour barely tells you if someone is lying.** Bond and DePaulo 2006 (206 documents,
   24,483 judges): about 54% accuracy, and experts no better than lay people (d = -.025). In
   DePaulo et al. 2003, eye contact d = 0.01 and gaze aversion d = 0.03. Yet "liars avoid eye
   contact" is believed in all 75 countries surveyed (Global Deception Research Team 2006). What
   does help is listening to content and asking good questions (Vrij, Hartwig and Granhag 2019).
   (Part D §2.)
4. **Turn-taking is fast, shared across languages, and meaningful.** In ten languages the mean gap
   between question and answer was +208 ms (Japanese +7 ms, Danish +469 ms), and everywhere a
   slower reply went with "no" or a non-answer (Stivers et al. 2009). Planning even one word takes
   600 ms or more, so listeners plan their reply while the other is still talking (Levinson 2016).
   Gaps over about 700 ms predict an unwelcome answer (Kendrick and Torreira 2015). (Part B §2.)
   This is the mechanism under "waiting for your turn instead of listening", and no textbook Part
   A read teaches it.
5. **Follow-up questions raise liking, modestly, and people don't predict it.** Huang et al. 2017:
   partners of people told to ask more questions liked them more (d about .35 in Study 1, .27 in
   Study 2A), through perceived responsiveness; follow-up questions carried the effect; askers did
   not expect it; outside observers did not share it. (Part B §4.)
6. **People misjudge conversations in consistent directions.** They underestimate how much a new
   partner liked them (Boothby et al. 2018; gap largest in the shy; it lasted several months among
   suitemates but was gone by the last wave). Conversations almost never end when both want: 1.59%
   did, and the mismatch averages about half the conversation's length (Mastroianni et al. 2021).
   People expect deep talk with strangers to be more awkward than it is (Kardas, Kumar and Epley
   2022, twelve experiments), and expect strangers to be less interested than they are (Epley and
   Schroeder 2014; Schroeder, Lyons and Epley 2022). (Part B §§5 to 7.)
7. **"Small talk makes you unhappy" failed to replicate.** Mehl et al. 2010 (79 students) found
   less small talk in happier people. The pooled replication (Milek et al. 2018, N = 486) found
   small talk unrelated to life satisfaction (r = .05), while substantive conversation still went
   with it (r = .23). The abstract: "The failure to replicate the original small-talk effect is
   theoretically and practically important" [V]. (Part B §7.4.)
8. **Paraphrasing on its own does less than the textbooks imply, and its inventor said so.**
   Rogers (1957) wrote that reflecting feelings is "by no means an essential condition" [V], and
   Rogers and Farson (1957) called technique without the attitude "empty and sterile" [V]. In
   Weger et al. 2010 (N = 180, abstract) paraphrase raised the listener's likeability but not the
   speaker's feeling of being understood; in Weger et al. 2014 (N = 115, abstract) active listening
   made people feel more understood than advice did, but advice was as satisfying. Bodie et al.
   2015 found small effects on felt emotional awareness and none on problem-solving. The field's
   current review calls paraphrase "a risky intervention" (Itzchakov and Bodie 2026). But being
   listened to well, as a whole stance of attention rather than a technique, has replicated
   benefits in the Itzchakov and Kluger experiments (lower anxiety, less defensive and clearer
   attitudes), and perceived listening correlates about r = .39 with good outcomes at work (Kluger
   et al. 2024, abstract). (Part C §§1, 2, 4.)
9. **Asking beats guessing.** Across 25 experiments, telling people to imagine another's
   perspective slightly lowered accuracy (d = -0.23), while asking the person raised it sharply
   (Eyal, Steffel and Epley 2018). Speakers overrate how visible their feelings are (Gilovich,
   Savitsky and Medvec 1998: liars expected 48.8% of observers to catch them; 25.6% did). (Part C
   §7.)
10. **Medicine has measured listening directly.** Doctors interrupt the patient's opening after a
    median of 11 seconds (Singh Ospina et al. 2019; older studies 18 to 23 s). Left alone, patients
    talk for a mean of 92 seconds and 78% finish within two minutes (Langewitz et al. 2002: "Doctors
    do not risk being swamped" [V]). Asking "Is there something else?" rather than "Is there
    anything else?" raised the share of patients voicing another concern from 53.1% to 90.3%
    (Heritage et al. 2007). (Part E §2.) These are the course's best worked examples of listening
    behaviour with a number attached.
11. **Asking directly about suicide does not raise suicidal thinking in the settings studied.**
    Dazzi et al. 2014 (full text) and later meta-analyses (Blades 2018, DeCou and Schumann 2018,
    Polihronis 2022, abstracts) agree, and every service Part E read (Samaritans, 988 and
    #BeThe1To, R U OK?, Mental Health First Aid, NHS) says to ask directly. None of the studies
    tests a conversation between friends or family. (Part E §1.)

## Boundaries with other Core courses, settled here so no lesson re-opens them

Checked against `curriculum/TAXONOMY.md` and `curriculum/core-path.yaml` on 2026-09-26. This is the
first course of Term 4, and every other course in the term is still `planned`, so this course draws
the lines and the later courses inherit them.

- **Public Speaking** (T4, planned). Public Speaking owns one-to-many talk: preparing and
  structuring a speech, delivery, voice and gesture on a stage, speech anxiety, slides, and
  evaluating a speech as a listener (critical listening to a speaker). **This course owns one-to-one
  and small-group conversation.** It owns the evidence on body language and the 7-38-55 myth;
  Public Speaking should link back to that lesson rather than re-teach it, and use it only for its
  own claim about delivery.
- **Understanding People: Psychology Fundamentals** (T4, planned). Understanding People owns the
  general psychology: emotion theories in depth (the basic-emotion versus constructionist debate as
  a theory question), personality, social cognition in general, empathy's neuroscience, and the
  replication crisis as a topic. **This course uses those findings only where they bear on a
  conversation**: the face debate as far as "can you read what someone feels from their face?"
  (Part D C1), perspective-taking versus asking (Eyal et al.), the illusion of transparency, and the
  empathy-training evidence as it applies to listening. Duhigg's neural-synchrony claims (Part A
  §6.3) go to Understanding People, gated.
- **Social Skills and Etiquette** (T4, planned). Social Skills owns occasions and manners:
  introductions and names, small talk scripts for events, networking, thanks, compliments,
  apologies, invitations, phone and message etiquette, dinner and table conventions. **This course
  owns the research on how conversation works**, including the stranger, depth, liking-gap and
  endings findings, because they are findings about conversation. Social Skills should build on
  them and not repeat them. Compliments and gratitude (Zhao and Epley 2021; Kumar and Epley 2018)
  get one line here and belong there.
- **Conflict Resolution** (T4, planned). Conflict Resolution owns disagreement as a problem to be
  settled: interests and positions, difficult conversations (Stone, Patton and Heen), de-escalation,
  mediation, and the FBI and negotiator material beyond listening moves. **This course keeps
  listening when you disagree** as a listening skill: acknowledgement is not agreement, the
  receptiveness recipe (Yeomans et al. 2020, gated), and the Braver Angels and Kalla and Broockman
  evidence on conversation across political difference. The Gottman controversy over active
  listening in conflict gets one paragraph here, as the limit of paraphrase, and its detail belongs
  to Conflict Resolution and Relationships and Family.
- **Relationships and Family** (T4, planned). Owns couples and parenting: Gottman's research in
  full, Gordon's *Parent Effectiveness Training*, *The Lost Art of Listening* on listening to
  those close to you. This course may use a family example but teaches no couples' method.
- **Cognitive Biases and Clear Thinking** (T4, planned, school of Foundations). Owns biases in
  general. The conversation-specific miscalibrations (liking gap, endings, undersociality) are
  taught here, and Cognitive Biases links back.
- **Persuasion and Influence** (T14) and **Negotiation** (T17), planned. Changing someone's mind,
  and Chris Voss's *Never Split the Difference*, belong there. **This course does not teach
  listening as a persuasion technique.** Where a source frames it that way (Carnegie; Voss; the FBI
  stairway's end point of behavioural change), the lesson says so and notes that it is a different
  goal from understanding.
- **Mental Fitness** (T2, published). **Crisis belongs to Mental Fitness, and this course points
  there.** Mental Fitness lesson 1 ("Where ordinary ends") carries the crisis callout that every one
  of its lessons repeats, with the emergency numbers and crisis lines by country and
  findahelpline.com. This course teaches only what a friend does in conversation: notice, ask
  directly, listen without fixing, do not promise secrecy, and help the person connect to a crisis
  line or professional. It reuses the Mental Fitness callout text word for word (re-checked on
  publication day) and links to Mental Fitness lesson 1. **It does not teach counselling, risk
  assessment or safety planning**, and says so. Part E §1 has the sources.
- **Writing Clearly** (T1, published). Owns written communication. The receptiveness studies were
  mostly written text; a lesson that uses them says so.
- **Teaching and Mentoring** (T23, planned). Teach-back (AHRQ) is used here only as a way of
  checking you were understood.

## What the field considers essential (the outline's backbone)

In the order the ideas build. The parts' own (a) lists have the detail and the sources.

1. **Conversation is an orderly system, run turn by turn.** One party at a time, transitions at
   points where a turn could be complete, next speaker chosen by the current one or self-selected
   (Sacks, Schegloff and Jefferson 1974). Pairs of actions (question and answer, invitation and
   response) set up what should come next (Schegloff and Sacks 1973). (Part B §1.)
2. **Timing carries meaning.** Gaps of about 200 ms are normal; delay goes with "no", reluctance
   and trouble, and listeners hear it that way. Overlap is common as a share of transitions (30 to
   40%) and rare as a share of time (under 5%). The system is shared across languages with small
   local differences that people notice. (Part B §2; Stivers et al. 2009.)
3. **Preference and repair.** Agreement and acceptance tend to come fast and plain; disagreement
   and refusal come late, hedged and explained (Pomerantz 1984). Misunderstandings are repaired,
   and speakers prefer to fix their own (Schegloff, Jefferson and Sacks 1977). A listener who knows
   this hears the "no" in a slow "well...". (Part B §§1.3, 1.4.)
4. **Hearing is not listening.** Every textbook makes the distinction; listening includes
   attention, understanding, remembering and responding, and has cognitive, emotional and
   behavioural sides (Worthington and Bodie). Scholars do not agree on one definition, and the
   staged models are teaching schemes. (Part A §§1.6, 2.)
5. **Listening has different purposes**, and the right behaviour depends on which: to understand
   information, to judge a claim, to support a person. (Part A (a) 3.)
6. **The named failures**: pseudolistening, self-focus (stage hogging, the shift response),
   selective and defensive listening, ambushing, rehearsing your reply, interrupting. Naming them is
   how learners notice them in themselves. (Part A (a) 4.)
7. **Let them finish, and tolerate silence.** Every practitioner tradition names both (Part E (a)
   1 and 2), and medicine has measured the cost of doing otherwise (Singh Ospina 2019; Langewitz
   2002).
8. **Questions: open, one at a time, then follow up.** Follow-up questions drive responsiveness and
   liking (Huang et al. 2017); wording matters ("some" versus "any", Heritage 2007); and people can
   ask too many without disclosing in return. (Parts B §4, E §§2, 5.)
9. **Say it back and check, as a check, not a performance.** The practitioner consensus (SHUSH's
   "Say it back"; 988's "Summarize What You Hear"; Calgary-Cambridge's summary that "invites
   patient to correct"), with the research qualification that paraphrase alone has small,
   outcome-specific effects and can feel mechanical (Part C (a) 3; Part A (c) C2). Rogers' point:
   the attitude of wanting to understand is the thing, and the restatement is how you test it.
10. **Responding to someone with a problem.** Acknowledge and legitimise feelings before anything
    else (Burleson's person-centred messages; High and Dillard 2012). Advice is not bad; it works
    better when asked for, polite, feasible and placed after support and exploration (MacGeorge,
    Feng). Help that implies incompetence can backfire (Bolger and Amarel 2007). (Part C §6.)
11. **Understanding another mind is hard, and asking is the best tool.** Perspective taking does
    not improve accuracy; asking does (Eyal et al. 2018). People overrate their own transparency
    and their own listening. (Part C §7.)
12. **What body language can and cannot tell you.** Nonverbal signals carry feeling and
    relationship more than content; no gesture has a fixed meaning; demeanour-based lie detection is
    near chance. (Part D (a).)
13. **Difference: culture, age, disability, view.** Culture shapes gaze and pace, but the variation
    is quantitative and within-country variation is large. Adjust to the person and ask how they
    want to be spoken with (RNID); do not over-accommodate older people (elderspeak) or finish a
    stammerer's sentences (STAMMA). Acknowledgement is not agreement. (Parts D §5, E §6.)
14. **When a conversation reveals someone is at risk**, ask directly, listen, do not promise
    secrecy, and connect them to help. (Part E §1; boundary with Mental Fitness above.)
15. **Weighing claims about conversation.** The field's own historians say the classic listening
    measures were weak (Part A §2.1); the stock numbers travelled far beyond their sources; the
    most-cited small-talk finding did not replicate. A Foundation course should teach learners to
    ask where a number came from. (Parts A (a) 8, B (a) 7.)

## Common misconceptions

Each is named by a source in the part cited. The correct understanding follows the arrow.

- **"93% of communication is nonverbal" / "7% words, 38% tone, 55% body."** → Mehrabian: not
  applicable unless someone is talking about feelings or attitudes. (Part D.)
- **"Liars avoid eye contact" / "you can read a liar's body language."** → about 54% accuracy;
  gaze aversion is not a cue (d = 0.03); experts are no better. (Part D.)
- **"Looking up and to the right means lying" (NLP eye cues).** → tested and unsupported (Wiseman
  et al. 2012). (Part D.)
- **"Crossed arms means defensive."** → no gesture has an invariant meaning (Harrigan 2005, via
  Denault et al. 2020, gated). No study tests crossed arms specifically; say the general point.
  (Part D.)
- **"Mirror their body language and they'll like you."** → the mimicry-liking link is fragile; a
  preregistered test found no effect (Hale and Hamilton 2016). (Part D.)
- **"Power poses raise your hormones and confidence."** → no hormonal or behavioural effect; the
  first author disowns it (Carney 2016); a small effect on felt power survives. (Part D.)
- **"We spend 45% of our time listening" and "we forget half of what we hear at once."** → weak
  sources, lecture tests, figures from 24% to 55%. (Part A.)
- **"People have fixed listening styles."** → the scale behind the four styles had
  "uncharacteristically low reliability" (Bodie, Worthington and Gearhart 2013 [V]). The UMN open
  textbook still teaches the old version. (Part A §2.6.)
- **"Active listening means nodding, eye contact and repeating back."** → Rogers and Farson
  rejected technique without attitude; the Program on Negotiation says active listening "does not
  mean sitting patiently" [V]; paraphrase alone has small effects. (Parts C, E.)
- **"Good listeners stay silent."** → the practitioners teach silence as a tool, not a posture;
  Zenger and Folkman 2016 argue good listeners ask and challenge, but their data are colleague
  ratings of 3,492 managers, not peer-reviewed, and the same article repeats the "80% nonverbal"
  myth (Part D §7). Use them as an illustration, not evidence.
- **"Good listeners make eye contact."** → contradicted for autistic listeners by their own
  accounts (Trevisan et al. 2017), for Tzeltal recipients (Rossano et al. 2009), and direct gaze
  reads as less friendly to Japanese viewers (Akechi et al. 2013). No source names this exact
  slogan as a myth; state it as an inference. (Part D.)
- **"Small talk makes you unhappy; only deep talk counts."** → did not replicate (Milek et al.
  2018). (Part B.)
- **"Men interrupt women far more."** → d = .15 overall, .33 for intrusive interruptions, with
  large variation by setting, and a negligible mixed-gender difference (Anderson and Leaper 1998).
  (Part B.)
- **"Overlap is interruption, and interruption is rude."** → much overlap is cooperative (Tannen;
  Schegloff 2000). (Part B.)
- **"Some cultures leave long silences, others talk over each other."** → Japanese were fastest
  (+7 ms) and Italians ordinary in Stivers et al. 2009. (Part B.)
- **"They didn't like me much."** → the liking gap. (Part B.)
- **"The other person will tell you when they want to go."** → they almost never do (Mastroianni et
  al. 2021). (Part B.)
- **"Put yourself in their shoes and you'll know what they think."** → no accuracy gain; ask
  instead (Eyal et al. 2018). (Part C.)
- **"Never give advice."** → timing and whether it was asked for matter; advice after support is
  rated well (Feng; MacGeorge). (Part C.)
- **"Sharing a similar story shows empathy."** → can compete with the speaker (Headlee's own
  example; Derber's shift response, known here only through secondary sources). Contested: see C3
  below. (Part A.)
- **"Reassurance ('It'll be fine') is supportive."** → low in the person-centred hierarchy
  (Burleson); a roadblock in Gordon's list. (Part C.)
- **"Asking about suicide will put the idea in their head."** → no. (Part E.)
- **"'Do you understand?' checks understanding"; "'Any other concerns?' invites more."** → AHRQ
  says those are "NOT teach-back questions" [V]; "some" beats "any" (Heritage). (Part E.)
- **"Finish a stammerer's sentence to help"; "speak louder and slower to someone with hearing
  loss or an older person."** → STAMMA: "Try not to do this." [V]; RNID: "avoid shouting, speaking
  too fast or unnecessarily slow" [V]; elderspeak research on over-accommodation. (Part E.)
- **"One conversation can flip someone's view."** → the famous version (LaCour and Green 2014) was
  retracted; real effects are small and durable (Kalla and Broockman 2020, abstract). (Part E.)
- **Famous quotations about listening are reliable.** → Duhigg's Shaw attribution and Murphy's
  Epictetus attribution are doubtful (Part A gate 14). Check any attributed quotation before use.

## Contested questions and the positions on each

Each question is set out in full, with its proponents' best case, in the part named. This is the
merged list, with the classification under standards 3.1.

1. **Does active listening (paraphrasing and reflecting) help in ordinary conversation?** (Parts A
   C2, C C1.) *Broad benefit:* Itzchakov, Kluger and colleagues, many experiments, some
   preregistered, several outcomes. *Narrow benefit:* Weger 2010 and 2014, Bodie et al. 2015,
   Itzchakov and Bodie 2026, and the preregistered four-lab test (N = 1,407) in which an
   instruction to listen did not change how listened-to partners felt. *Reconciling note:* the
   positive programme manipulates the whole stance of attention; the sceptical studies isolate the
   technique. Both can be true. **Contested empirical.**
2. **Does active listening help in conflict?** (Part C C2.) Gottman et al. 1998 found paraphrase and
   validation "hardly ever occurred" [V-OCR] in happy couples' conflict; Stanley, Bradbury and
   Markman 2000 replied that correlational data cannot test a training method. Gottman's own later
   blueprint uses a restate-and-validate format. **Contested empirical.** Detail belongs to
   Conflict Resolution and Relationships and Family.
3. **Is sharing a similar experience a failure of listening?** (Part A C3.) Derber (secondary) and
   Headlee: yes, prefer support responses. Adler lists agreement and empathising among supporting
   responses, and self-disclosure research treats reciprocal disclosure as how closeness grows; Huang
   et al. note you can ask too many questions without disclosing. The answer probably depends on
   whether the speaker is in distress. **Contested empirical, with a value element.**
4. **Should you give advice when someone shares a problem?** (Part C C4.) Rogerian and practitioner
   tradition: hold back. Communication research: advice helps when solicited, feasible, polite and
   after support; MI's 4th edition says offering advice is "not the opposite of MI" [V]. **Largely
   established that timing and solicitation matter**; whether to advise in a given case is a
   judgement.
5. **Is small talk worthwhile, or should you go deeper?** (Part B C1.) Depth position (Kardas et
   al. 2022; Mehl 2010): people are overly shallow because they misjudge others' interest.
   Small-talk position (Milek et al. 2018; Sandstrom and Dunn 2014; openings as necessary work):
   small talk is not harmful and is how depth is reached. **Contested empirical on effects, partly
   value.** The defensible teaching line: they are not rivals, and people tend to stop short of the
   depth they would enjoy. All the experimental evidence is from the US and UK.
6. **Is interruption rude, or a matter of style?** (Part B C2.) Dominance view (Zimmerman and West);
   style view (Tannen); CA and timing view (overlap frequent, brief, orderly). **Contested empirical
   on how much norms vary; value on how far to accommodate other styles.** Teach interruption as an
   action judged by the people in the conversation.
7. **Do men interrupt more than women?** (Part B C3.) Large asymmetry (Zimmerman and West 1975, a
   tiny sample, and its own text and table disagree: 98% versus 96%); slightly and context-dependent
   (Anderson and Leaper 1998); badly posed (Tannen). **Contested empirical.** The accurate summary: a
   small average difference for floor-taking interruptions that varies strongly with setting.
8. **How universal are turn-taking and gaze norms?** (Parts B C4, D C3.) A shared system with small
   quantitative variation is **largely established** (Stivers et al. 2009; Rossano et al. 2009 on
   speaker gaze). How far variation extends, what it means for misunderstanding, and whether
   national typologies (Hall, Hofstede) help are **contested empirical**; the critics (Kittler et
   al. 2011; Cardon 2008) say national labels predict individuals poorly.
9. **How much can a face tell you about what someone feels?** (Part D C1.) Basic emotion theory
   (Ekman; Keltner et al. 2019; Cowen et al. 2021) versus the constructionist view (Barrett et al.
   2019: expected configurations in about 19% of emotional events). Common ground: faces carry
   social information and context matters. **Contested empirical.** Understanding People owns the
   theory.
10. **Can behaviour ever reveal a lie?** (Part D C2.) Demeanour-only detection is close to
    **established** as poor. Interview-based and content methods (Vrij; Granhag and Hartwig;
    Levine) do better and are **contested empirical** in how much.
11. **Does mirroring help?** (Part D C4.) **Contested empirical**; the practical advice to mirror
    on purpose has no good support.
12. **Is empathy teachable, and is it good?** (Part C C3.) In-person training g = 0.63 (0.51 after
    bias correction, Teding van Berkhout and Malouff 2016, abstract); digital training near zero
    (Williams et al. 2026, abstract). Bloom: emotional empathy is a biased spotlight, prefer
    understanding and compassion. Zaki: empathy is a motivated choice and can be chosen, up or down (see "Gate closures, lesson 07 review").
    **Teachability contested empirical; "is it good" partly value.**
13. **Is communication difficulty in autism one person's deficit or a mismatch?** (Part D C6.)
    Milton 2012 and Crompton et al. 2020 (mismatch) versus the 2025 registered replication (no
    mismatch effect on information transfer, N = 311). **Contested empirical, with a value
    dimension.** Never cite Crompton 2020 alone.
14. **Should you ask directly about suicide?** (Part E C1.) **Established** that asking in research,
    screening and clinical settings does not raise suicidal ideation; **untested** for lay
    conversations, where "ask directly" is the unanimous advice of the services. Teach it as their
    advice, backed by strong evidence of no harm in the settings studied.
15. **Does conversation across political difference reduce hostility or change minds?** (Part E
    C2.) Small, durable effects from non-judgemental exchange of personal stories (Kalla and
    Broockman 2020, d about 0.04 to 0.08, abstract) and a Braver Angels randomised trial (N = 165)
    with mixed significance and some fading; arguments alone did nothing. **Contested empirical on
    size and durability; partly value** on what such talk is for. The organisations themselves say
    they are not trying to change views.
16. **Are practitioner scripts (NURSE, OARS, the FBI stairway) transferable to friends and
    family?** (Part E C3.) **Contested empirical, with a value element** about using influence
    techniques on people you love. Teach the shared moves; flag the scripts as professional tools.
17. **How much of our time is spent listening, and is listening declining?** (Part A C1, C5.) The
    direction (listening is at or near the top) is reasonably established; any percentage is
    **contested empirical**. The decline claims in Murphy and Headlee are unmeasured in what Part A
    read.
18. **Is listening a technique for getting what you want, or attention owed to a person?** (Part A
    C6.) Carnegie frames it as a way to win friends and customers; Rogers and the helping
    traditions as understanding; Derber (secondary) as a question of whose talk gets attention.
    **Value question.** The course describes the positions and says plainly that its own lessons
    aim at understanding, not at persuasion, which belongs to Persuasion and Influence.

## Safety-critical guidance

This is not a safety course, but one situation is safety-critical: a conversation in which someone
reveals they may be at risk of suicide or self-harm.

- **What every service Part E read says**: ask directly and in plain words (Samaritans: "it's okay
  to ask them directly" [V]; #BeThe1To's model question "Are you thinking about suicide?" [V]);
  listen without judging or fixing; do not promise to keep it secret; help them connect to a crisis
  line or professional; follow up. The 988 five steps are Ask, Be there, Help keep them safe, Help
  them connect, Follow up; use the service's exact wording (Part E gate 6).
- **The evidence** that asking does not plant the idea (Dazzi et al. 2014, full text; the three
  meta-analyses, abstracts) concerns research, screening and clinical settings. Samaritans' claim
  that asking "can protect" is attributed to them, not stated as fact (Part E gate 5).
- **The crisis numbers** are the Mental Fitness callout, reused word for word and re-checked on
  publication day: emergency services (911 in the US and Canada, 999 in the UK, 112 across the EU,
  000 in Australia); 988 by call or text in the US and Canada; Samaritans 116 123 in the UK and
  Ireland; Lifeline 13 11 14 in Australia; findahelpline.com elsewhere.
- **The limit**: the course teaches what a friend does in conversation, not counselling, risk
  assessment or safety planning, and points to Mental Fitness lesson 1 for where ordinary distress
  ends and help is needed. It carries the standard 2.5 note once: education, not personalised
  advice.
- **Other care points.** Elderspeak (Part E §6) is linked to resistance to care in dementia
  settings; a lesson on talking with older people says what the study measured and does not
  generalise it. Interpreters: speak to the person, not the interpreter (Part E §6).

## Best existing free resources (to link, not duplicate)

The parts' (e) sections list each with a live check on 2026-09-26. The short list most lessons will
use:

- **Stivers et al. 2009**, turn timing in ten languages: https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC2705608/
- **Levinson and Torreira 2015**, gaps and overlaps with numbers: https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC4464110/
- **ISCA's Schegloff archive**, free PDFs of the founding CA papers: https://www.conversationanalysis.org/schegloff-publication-archive/
- **Mastroianni et al. 2021**, conversation endings: https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC7958231/
- **Boothby et al. 2018**, the liking gap: https://clarkrelationshiplab.yale.edu/sites/default/files/files/BoothbyCooneySandstromClark2018.pdf
- **Milek et al. 2018**, the replication that overturned the small-talk finding: https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC6139582/
- **Epley lab publications**, strangers, depth, compliments, gratitude: https://www.nicholasepley.com/publications
- **Rogers and Farson, *Active Listening*** (1987 excerpt): https://wholebeinginstitute.com/wp-content/uploads/Rogers_Farson_Active-Listening.pdf
- **Itzchakov and Bodie 2026**, the field's current puzzles, open access: https://doi.org/10.1111/spc3.70145
- **Eyal, Steffel and Epley 2018**, ask, don't guess (author copy; see Part C (e) for the URL)
- **Mehrabian's own statement**: http://www.kaaj.com/psych/smorder.html
- **"A world of lies"**, the 58- and 75-country survey: https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC2957901/
- **Wiseman et al. 2012**, the NLP eye-movement test: https://journals.plos.org/plosone/article?id=10.1371/journal.pone.0040259
- **Carney's statement on power poses**: https://faculty.haas.berkeley.edu/dana_carney/pdf_My%20position%20on%20power%20poses.pdf
- **Heritage et al. 2007**, "some" versus "any": https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC2305862/
- **Langewitz et al. 2002**, the 92 seconds: https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC126654/
- **Samaritans, SHUSH listening tips** and **988 Lifeline, Help Someone Else** (URLs in Part E (e))
- **StoryCorps Great Questions**: https://storycorps.org/participate/great-questions/
- **STAMMA** and **RNID** on talking with someone who stammers or has hearing loss (Part E (e))
- **UMN *Communication in the Real World*, chapter 5 "Listening"** (CC BY-NC-SA): https://open.lib.umn.edu/communication/part/chapter-5-listening/ Use it for stages and barriers; skip its statistics and its listening-styles section (Part A §2.6).
- **Headlee's TED talk** "10 ways to have a better conversation", with transcript, as a popular
  account to test against the evidence: https://www.ted.com/talks/celeste_headlee_10_ways_to_have_a_better_conversation/transcript

**Do not link:** the Janusik and Rouillard compilation (errors); third-party uploads of Gilovich
1998 and Bloom 2017 (link the DOI instead); the Columbia Ombuds reprint of Nichols 1957 (permission
unclear).

## Gates: what must be read before a lesson states it

The parts' (d) sections list about 75 gates. The ones most likely to be hit:

- **Any listening percentage** (time spent, retention, speech versus thought rate). Part A gates
  1 to 4.
- **Mehrabian's study designs** (words, speakers, sample, sex of participants, the arithmetic).
  Part D gate 1. Quote only the two website sentences until the 1967 papers or *Silent Messages*
  pp. 75 to 80 are read.
- **Derber**: every quotation and the "1,500 conversations" figure. No primary support found. Part
  A gate 5.
- **Huang et al. 2017**: take statistics from the 2025 corrected results. Part B gate D10.
- **Mastroianni 2021**: use 1.59%, 29.37% and about half the conversation; never "15.6%". Part B
  gate D3.
- **The 200 ms**: say which statistic (mode, median +100 ms, mean +208 ms). Part B gate D7.
- **Zimmerman and West 1975**: 96% in the table, 98% in the text; tiny sample. Part B gate D1.
- **Weger 2010 and 2014** effect sizes and message content. Part C gate 1.
- **Gottman 1998** quotations are OCR; check against the page image. Part C gate 2.
- **Beckman and Frankel 1984 "18 seconds"**: seen only secondhand. Part E gate 1.
- **Yeomans et al. 2020**: integrity check before use. Parts B D11, E 14.
- **Kalla and Broockman 2020** and the Braver Angels trial: read full texts before describing
  scripts or subgroups. Part E gate 13.
- **Quotation attributions** (Shaw, Epictetus, Covey, Coolidge). Part A gate 14.
- **Crisis numbers and service wording**: re-check on publication day. Part E gates 6, 7, 17.

## Open concerns about scope

1. **The course promises five outcomes and one line of description (people who differ in age,
   culture or view) that is really a sixth.** The research supports it well (Parts D §5, E §6), but
   it is the area most likely to sprawl into Social Skills and Conflict Resolution. Keep it to
   listening and conversation across difference; the boundaries above hold the line.
2. **Much of the modern conversation psychology comes from one network of labs** (Epley and
   collaborators; the Harvard and Yale groups around Brooks, Boothby and Mastroianni), mostly with
   US and UK students and online samples. The findings are preregistered and partly replicated, but
   lessons should say who was studied, and not present them as human universals. The CA timing work
   (Stivers et al.) is the cross-cultural exception.
3. **The textbooks are thin exactly where the research is richest** (turn-taking, questions,
   endings), and rich where the research is weakest (listening statistics, listening styles). The
   course will read differently from a standard interpersonal-communication course, and should: it
   is built from the evidence, with the textbooks as a map of what learners have already heard.
4. **Paywalls leave the listening-journal core as abstracts.** The Weger experiments, the Itzchakov
   PSPB papers and the Kluger 2024 meta-analysis (open access but blocked to curl) are cited in
   several places from abstracts. Before drafting the active-listening lesson, a researcher with
   browser or library access should read Weger 2010 and 2014 and Kluger et al. 2024 in full.
5. **Neutrality.** See the note at the top: gender, culture, autism and political dialogue get the
   neutrality pass.
6. **Practitioner lore is abundant and often untested.** Gordon's roadblocks, the FBI stairway,
   NURSE, OARS, SHUSH and LAPP are useful, memorable lists. Each is taught as a practitioner model,
   labelled that way, beside what the research shows, in the way `docs/SOURCE_NOTES.md` treats
   Hormozi.
7. **Length.** Fourteen lessons is the synthesiser's estimate (below). If the outline finds a
   lesson running long, the natural split is the active-listening lesson (the idea, then the
   evidence).

## Suggested lesson sequence (for Stage 2 to confirm)

1. **What a conversation is made of.** Turns, the 200 ms gap, planning while listening, overlap.
   Sacks et al. 1974; Stivers et al. 2009; Levinson 2016. (Part B §§1, 2.)
2. **Why "no" comes late.** Adjacency pairs, preference, repair; hearing the meaning in a pause.
   Schegloff and Sacks 1973; Pomerantz 1984; Kendrick and Torreira 2015. (Part B §§1, 2.3.)
3. **Hearing is not listening.** What listening is, its purposes, and where the 45% and 25% came
   from. Worthington and Bodie; ILA; Nichols and Stevens; Rankin via Part A. (Part A §§2, 3.)
4. **How listening goes wrong.** Pseudolistening, rehearsing, the shift response, one-upping,
   interrupting; overlap versus intrusion; the gender question. (Parts A §7, B §3.)
5. **Let them finish.** Interruption and silence, with medicine's numbers. Singh Ospina 2019;
   Langewitz 2002; practitioner consensus. (Part E §2.)
6. **Asking better questions.** Open and follow-up questions, "some" versus "any", one at a time,
   when not to ask, asking too many. Huang et al. 2017; Heritage 2007; interviewers. (Parts B §4,
   E §§2, 5.)
7. **Saying it back.** Rogers' attitude, paraphrase and summary as a check, what the experiments
   found, the Gottman limit in one paragraph. (Part C §§1 to 5.)
8. **When someone brings you a problem.** Acknowledging feelings, advice and its timing, invisible
   support, reassurance. Burleson; MacGeorge; Feng; Bolger and Amarel. (Part C §6.)
9. **When it's serious.** Noticing, asking directly about suicide, not promising secrecy,
   connecting to help; the Mental Fitness callout. (Part E §1.)
10. **Ask, don't guess.** Perspective taking versus perspective getting, the illusion of
    transparency, empathy and whether it can be learned. Eyal et al. 2018; Gilovich 1998; Ickes;
    the Bloom and Zaki debate. (Part C §7.)
11. **Strangers, small talk and going deeper.** Epley and Schroeder; Kardas et al.; Milek et al.;
    the liking gap. (Part B §§5, 7.)
12. **Ending well.** Mastroianni 2021; pre-closings; kind ways to signal you are ready to go.
    (Part B §§1.2, 6.)
13. **What body language can and cannot tell you.** 7-38-55, lie detection, faces, mimicry, power
    posing, eye contact. (Part D.)
14. **Talking across difference.** Culture and timing, gaze, age, hearing loss and stammering,
    interpreters, and listening to someone who disagrees with you. (Parts B §2, D §§5, 6, E §6.)

The course-end project could be a recorded or written-up real conversation, analysed against the
course (turns, questions, follow-ups, a repair, how it ended), with a rubric; the test draws on all
fourteen. A lesson 15, **Weighing a claim about conversation**, could be the capstone if the
outline wants the evidence thread as its own lesson rather than spread across lessons 3, 11 and 13.

---

The five parts follow in full. Their headings are one level lower than in the researchers' files,
so that each part sits under its own heading here.

## Part A: Textbooks, syllabi, the listening-studies field, popular books, and free resources

Read 2026-09-26. This part covers what a learner, a teacher or a textbook would call "the standard account" of listening and conversation: the five most assigned US interpersonal communication textbooks (editions, chapter lists, how each frames listening); the specialist listening-studies literature (the Worthington and Bodie Sourcebook, Brownell, Worthington, Bodie and Fitch-Hauser, the International Listening Association definition, the Listening Styles Profile and its revision); the origin and strength of the stock statistics ("we spend 45% of communication time listening", "we remember 25% or 50% of what we hear"); the open textbooks usable as free links; four course documents (two interpersonal courses, one listening course outline, one conversation-analysis seminar, plus a conversation-analysis reading list); the popular books a learner will meet (Headlee, Murphy, Duhigg, M. P. Nichols, Carnegie, Stone, Patton and Heen); and Derber's "conversational narcissism". Method: WebSearch to locate, then curl (generic User-Agent) with pdftotext or pandoc to extract text, which I read. Quotations marked [V] were copied from text I extracted myself; [T] came back through WebFetch; "snippet only" means seen only in a search result and never put in quotation marks. Raw extracts are saved in `cl/A/`. Publisher textbooks are paywalled: for those I read publisher tables of contents, publisher chapter outlines, publisher prefaces and free sample chapters, and I say exactly which. I did not read pirate copies (several are indexed on dokumen.pub and Scribd; I did not open them).

---

### 1. The most assigned interpersonal communication textbooks

The five books below dominate US introductory interpersonal courses. All five give listening its own chapter; all five place it in the "messages" or "skills" half of the book, next to language and nonverbal communication and before relationships and conflict. None treats conversation (turn-taking, questions, sequence) as fully as it treats listening, except DeVito, who has a separate "Conversational Messages" chapter.

#### 1.1 Adler, Proctor and Manning, *Looking Out, Looking In* (Cengage)

- **Edition:** 16th edition, ISBN 9780357033944, Adler, Proctor and Manning. Searches found no 17th edition as of today. Published 2022 to 2023 (copyright line not seen; Cengage page does not show it in the text I extracted).
- **What I read:** the Cengage Asia and Cengage Australia product pages (table of contents, feature list) [V], and a Cengage "enrichment module" PDF of chapter outlines for the 13th edition (ISBN 0495569828, c. 2010), which gives the internal structure of the listening chapter [V]. Chapter text: not read.
- **Chapters (16e):** 1 A First Look at Interpersonal Communication; 2 Interpersonal Communication and Social Media; 3 Communication and Identity; 4 Perception; 5 Emotions; 6 Language; 7 Nonverbal Communication; 8 **Listening: More Than Meets the Ear**; 9 Communication and Relational Dynamics; 10 Communication in Close Relationships; 11 Improving Communication Climates; 12 Managing Interpersonal Conflicts. Culture is threaded through (the product page says "diversity and inclusivity appear at the core of all content" [V]) rather than a chapter of its own.
- **Listening chapter structure (13e outline, publisher PDF, [V] headings):** "Hearing vs. listening"; "Mindless listening"; "Mindful listening"; elements of the process: "Hearing - Physiological", "Attending - Psychological", "Understanding – Making Sense", "Responding – Observable Feedback", "Remembering - Recalling". Types of ineffective listening: pseudolistening, stage hogging, selective listening, insulated listening, defensive listening, ambushing, insensitive listening. Why we do not listen better: message overload, preoccupation, rapid thought, effort, external noise, faulty assumptions, lack of apparent advantages, lack of training, hearing problems, media influences. Types of listening responses: prompting, questioning (with a list of "counterfeit questions": questions that trap, tag questions, questions that make statements, carry hidden agendas, seek "correct" answers, rest on unchecked assumptions), paraphrasing ("remain tentative", "use sparingly"), supporting (with "cold comfort" failures such as denying others the right to their feelings and minimising), analysing, advising, judging, and "choosing the best listening response" by gender, situation, the other person and personal style.
- **Why it matters for us:** this is the richest ready-made taxonomy of *responses* (not just of listening failures), and it contains two ideas the course outcomes need directly: counterfeit questions (outcome 3, "judge when not to ask") and "use paraphrasing sparingly", which is a useful corrective to rote active listening. "Stage hogging" is Adler's name for what Derber calls conversational narcissism.
- **What a lesson may say:** that mainstream textbooks distinguish hearing (physiological) from listening (attending, understanding, responding, remembering), and list common faulty-listening patterns. **May not say** that the five-stage model is an empirical finding; it is a teaching scheme (see 2.1 and 2.4).

#### 1.2 Julia T. Wood, *Interpersonal Communication: Everyday Encounters* (Cengage)

- **Edition:** 10th edition, ISBN 9798214157429, copyright 2027, published 14 January 2026 per the Cengage Australia page [V]. The 9th edition (ISBN 9780357032947, 2019/2020) is the one most syllabi still list.
- **What I read:** Cengage product pages for the 9th and 10th editions (TOC) [V]. Chapter text: not read (Internet Archive copies are borrow-only; the full-text download returned 401).
- **Chapters (10e):** Part I Building Blocks: 1 An Introduction to Interpersonal Communication; 2 Communication and Personal Identity; 3 Perception and Communication; 4 The World of Words; 5 The World Beyond Words; 6 **Mindful Listening**. Part II: 7 Emotions; 8 Communication Climate; 9 Managing Conflict; 10 Friendships; 11 Committed Romantic Relationships; 12 Communication in Families. Culture again threaded, not a chapter.
- **Listening content (snippet only; student study notes and flashcards, not the book):** Wood's process begins with "mindfulness" as the first stage, followed by physically receiving, selecting and organising, interpreting, responding and remembering; obstacles are split into external (message overload, complexity, noise) and internal (preoccupation, prejudgment, reacting to emotionally loaded language, lack of effort, failure to adapt listening style); "forms of nonlistening" include pseudolistening, monopolising, selective listening, defensive listening, ambushing and literal listening. This must be checked against the book before any lesson attributes it to Wood.
- **Distinctive choice:** making mindfulness (being present) the first stage of listening. This is the textbook version of Headlee's "don't multitask" and Murphy's "listening as meditation".

#### 1.3 Adler and Proctor, *Interplay: The Process of Interpersonal Communication* (Oxford University Press)

- **Edition:** 16th edition, ISBN 9780197666128, 2024 [T via a bookseller listing; publisher page blocked by a bot challenge]. Earlier editions had Rosenfeld as co-author (Adler, Rosenfeld and Proctor).
- **What I read:** the Library of Congress table of contents for an earlier edition (LCCN 2005034975) [V]; the OUP companion-site chapter outline was blocked (HTTP 202 challenge, then empty to WebFetch). Search results say the 16e listening chapter is still chapter 7 "Listening: Understanding and Supporting Others" (snippet only for 16e).
- **Chapters (earlier edition, [V]):** Part One Foundations: 1 Interpersonal Process; 2 **Culture and Communication**; 3 Communication and the Self; 4 Perceiving Others. Part Two Creating and Responding to Messages: 5 Language; 6 Nonverbal Communication; 7 **Listening: Understanding and Supporting Others**; 8 Emotions. Part Three: 9 Dynamics of Relationships; 10 Intimacy and Distance; 11 Communication Climate; 12 Managing Conflict; appendices on families and work.
- **Distinctive choice:** puts culture early as its own chapter, and titles listening as "understanding and supporting", i.e. frames listening as including the supportive response, which is where Derber's support response sits.

#### 1.4 Joseph A. DeVito, *The Interpersonal Communication Book* (Pearson)

- **Edition:** 16th edition, published 24 March 2021, copyright 2022 [T, Pearson educator page]. 
- **What I read:** the Pearson-hosted front matter PDF of the 15th edition (copyright 2019), which contains the full detailed table of contents and preface [V]. For the 16e, only chapter titles (Ch 6 Listening, Ch 8 Conversational Messages) [T].
- **Chapters (15e, [V]):** 1 Foundations; 2 **Culture and Interpersonal Communication** (individualist/collectivist, high/low context, power distance, etc.); 3 Perception of Self and Others; 4 Verbal Messages; 5 Nonverbal Messages; 6 **Listening**; 7 Emotional Messages; 8 **Conversational Messages**; 9 to 12 relationships, conflict, power.
- **Listening chapter sections (15e, [V]):** The Process and Skills of Listening: Stage One Receiving, Two Understanding, Three Remembering, Four Evaluating, Five Responding. Listening Barriers: physical and mental distractions; biases and prejudices; "Racist, Heterosexist, Ageist, and Sexist Listening"; lack of appropriate focus; premature judgment; hearing impairment. Styles of Effective Listening: Empathic, Polite, Critical, Active. Culture, Gender, and Listening. The preface says the 15e added "a revision and reconceptualization of the styles of listening" and hearing impairment as a barrier [V].
- **Conversation chapter sections (15e, [V]):** Principles of Conversation: process, cooperation, politeness, dialogue, **turn taking**. Conversational Disclosure. Everyday Conversations: small talk, introductions, excuses, apologising, asking for a favour, complimenting, advising.
- **Why it matters:** DeVito is the only one of the five with a conversation chapter built on Grice (cooperation), politeness theory and turn-taking. It is also the book whose "Everyday Conversations" section shows where our course borders Social Skills and Etiquette (introductions, apologies, compliments, excuses are etiquette-course material; turn-taking and advising belong here).

#### 1.5 McCornack and Morrison, *Reflect & Relate* (Macmillan)

- **Edition:** 6th edition, copyright 2022, McCornack and Morrison [T, Macmillan page]. No 7th edition found.
- **What I read:** Macmillan TOC [T]; the Ohio State COMM 3620 syllabus that assigns the 5th edition [V]; and the UMN open textbook, which cites McCornack (2007) for its list of bad listening practices [V]. Chapter text: not read.
- **Chapters (6e):** 1 Introducing IPC; Part 1: 2 Self; 3 Perceiving Others; 4 Emotions; 5 **Understanding Culture**; 6 **Understanding Gender**; Part 2 Skills: 7 **Listening Actively**; 8 Communicating Verbally; 9 Communicating Nonverbally; 10 Conflict and Power; Part 3: romantic, family, friends, workplace.
- **Listening failures attributed to McCornack (2007) via the UMN text [V]:** eavesdropping, aggressive listening ("pay attention in order to attack"), narcissistic listening ("try to make the interaction about them"), pseudo-listening. The UMN text's definitions are close paraphrases of these.

#### 1.6 What the textbooks agree on, and where they are thin

- All five separate hearing from listening and give a staged process model (three of the five use five stages; Wood adds mindfulness; Brownell, below, uses six). The stage names vary (receiving/hearing/sensing; attending; understanding/interpreting; evaluating; remembering/recalling; responding).
- All five list "faulty" or "non-" listening. The overlapping core: **pseudolistening; self-focus (stage hogging, monopolising, narcissistic listening); selective listening; defensive listening; ambushing or aggressive listening; literal or insensitive listening.**
- Response repertoires (Adler's seven responses; DeVito's empathic/polite/critical/active) are the practical heart of the chapters.
- **Thin spots:** none of the five (as far as TOCs and outlines show) teaches the conversation-analytic findings on turn-taking, repair or preference in any depth; DeVito names turn-taking as a "principle". Question-asking is handled as a listening response (Adler's "questioning") rather than as a conversational skill with its own evidence base. Claims about listening statistics in the textbook tradition are poorly sourced (section 3).

---

### 2. The listening-studies field

#### 2.1 Worthington and Bodie, "Defining Listening: A Historical, Theoretical, and Pragmatic Assessment"

- **Citation:** Worthington, D. L., & Bodie, G. D. (2017/2018). Defining listening: A historical, theoretical, and pragmatic assessment. In D. L. Worthington & G. D. Bodie (Eds.), *The Sourcebook of Listening Research: Methodology and Measures* (pp. 3 to 17). Wiley. DOI 10.1002/9781119102991.ch1. Copyright line reads "© 2018 John Wiley & Sons" [V].
- **What I read:** full text of chapter 1 as the publisher's free excerpt PDF (catalogimages.wiley.com), about 6 pages of dense text [V]. The rest of the Sourcebook (measure profiles, including Profile 60 "Time Studies" and Profile 36 LSP-R): not read.
- **Content:**
  - Table 1.1 lists 18 definitions of listening (a "Sample of Listening Definitions") from Tucker (1925) to Bostrom (2011). [Corrected 2026-09-26, lesson 03 review: Stage 1 counted 17; the table has 18 rows, re-counted against the PDF.] Rankin (1926): "The ability to understand spoken language" [V]; Wolvin and Coakley (1988): "The process of receiving, attending to, and assigning meaning to aural stimuli" [V]; ILA (1996): "The process of receiving, constructing meaning from, and responding to spoken and/or nonverbal messages" [V].
  - Listening is treated as having **affective** (motivation, attitudes), **behavioural** (responses, eye contact, questions) and **cognitive** (attending, comprehending, interpreting, evaluating) components, citing Halone, Cunconan, Coakley and Wolvin (1998).
  - On the foundational study: Nichols (1948) "played six 10‐minute audio‐recorded lectures to a sample of undergraduate students", with 10 multiple-choice questions after each; students "recalled an average of 68% of the lecture material" [V]. Note this is **68%, not 50%**, and it is lecture comprehension, not conversation.
  - Kelly (1965, 1967) found early listening tests "were more highly correlated with tests of intelligence than with each other" [V], which undermined the idea of listening as a single measurable skill.
  - The authors say recall of lecture material "is qualitatively different from recall in an interpersonal context" and that even later measures "suffer from insufficient evidence of validity" [V].
  - The behavioural "competency" movement of the 1980s and 1990s shifted attention to observable behaviours; two limitations they name: skills taught may not be the right ones, and "the majority of studies in this area have been atheoretical" [V].
  - They argue against a single definition: "definitions—instead of a single definition—are the goal" [V]. Lay people mean by listening "a state of interpersonal connection with and presence of others" (citing Purdy 2006) [V].
- **What a lesson may say:** listening scholars disagree on a single definition and treat listening as several processes; the early research measured recall of lectures; the field itself says much of its skills training has been weakly theorised. **May not say:** "research shows we remember only half of what we hear" as a fact about conversation.

#### 2.2 The International Listening Association definition

- ILA website page "Listening Definition": "the process of receiving, constructing meaning from, and responding to spoken and/or nonverbal messages" (1996) [V, listen.org, fetched today]. The LibreTexts Usera text dates it to 1995 and cites *Listening Post* 53, p. 4 (April 1995) [V]; Worthington and Bodie give 1996 [V]. Date is inconsistent across sources (gate).
- Note that it is a consensus definition by a professional association; Worthington and Bodie list it alongside others and argue against privileging any one. [2026-09-26, lesson 03 review: "adopted for teaching" is not on the ILA page or in Worthington and Bodie; do not repeat it.]

#### 2.3 Brownell, *Listening: Attitudes, Principles, and Skills* (HURIER)

- **Edition:** 7th edition, Routledge, copyright 2024, 512 pp. [V, Routledge page]. Earlier editions Pearson/Allyn & Bacon; 6th 2018 (Routledge).
- **What I read:** Routledge page, description and TOC [V]. Text not read.
- **TOC [V]:** Part I: 1 Improving Listening Effectiveness; 2 Listening Theory and Research. Part II: 3 The Process of **Hearing**; 4 **Understanding**; 5 **Remembering**; 6 **Interpreting**; 7 **Evaluating**; 8 **Responding**. Part III: 9 Listening Relationships; 10 Listening Challenges. The description calls listening "a process involving six interrelated components" developed "along the parallel dimensions of theory and skill building" [V]. The 7e adds "integrative listening" [V].
- **Use:** HURIER is the best-known named model and gives "interpreting" (reading the speaker's meaning and feeling, including nonverbal) a separate stage from "understanding" (the literal message). That distinction is useful for outcome 2 ("listening that checks understanding").

#### 2.4 Worthington, Bodie and Fitch-Hauser, *Listening: Processes, Functions, and Competency*

- **Edition:** 3rd edition, Routledge, copyright 2024 (1st ed. Worthington and Fitch-Hauser, Pearson 2012; 2nd 2018) [V, Routledge page].
- **TOC (3e) [V]:** Part I Listening as a Cognitive Process: 1 Introduction and Overview; 2 Purposes & Competencies; 3 Information Processing; 4 Individual Differences; 5 Listening in a Mediated World. Part II Listening as a Social Function: 6 **Listening in the Conversational Context**; 7 Family; 8 Friends & Romance. Part III Professional: education, organisations, health, legal. Part IV: 13 Future Directions. New in 3e per the description: neurodiversity, fMRI, "aural architecture" [V].
- **Use:** the only listening textbook with a chapter on listening *in conversation*, and the one closest to current research (Bodie is a co-author). Text not read.

#### 2.5 Wolvin and Coakley

- Their 1988 definition is in Worthington and Bodie's table [V]. The UMN text cites "A Listening Taxonomy" in Wolvin and Coakley (eds.), *Perspectives on Listening* (1993) [V citation]. Their widely taught five listening purposes (discriminative, comprehensive, therapeutic, critical, appreciative) I know only from secondary teaching materials and did not verify in their text (gate). Their book *Listening* (5th ed., 1996) not read.

#### 2.6 Listening styles: LSP-16 and its revision (a live correction the open textbook misses)

- **Citation:** Bodie, G. D., Worthington, D. L., & Gearhart, C. C. (2013). The Listening Styles Profile-Revised (LSP-R): A scale revision and evidence for validity. *Communication Quarterly*, 61(1), 72 to 90. DOI 10.1080/01463373.2012.720343.
- **What I read:** the author-posted published PDF; abstract and opening [V]; the discussion also read at Stage 3 and re-read at Stage 4 of lesson 3 (2026-09-26; see the lesson 03/04 and lesson 03 review gate closures). Results tables not read in detail.
- **Finding:** "The Listening Styles Profile (LSP-16) is the most widely used self-report listening instrument in the communication discipline. Unfortunately, researchers have utilized the instrument despite its uncharacteristically low reliability estimates and unvalidated factor structure." [V] The revision proposes four factors: "relational, analytical, task-oriented, and critical listening" [V].
- **Consequence:** the four styles taught in the UMN open textbook (people-, action-, content-, time-oriented; Watson, Barker and Weaver 1995) rest on the old instrument that its own field says had low reliability. The Janusik compilation (2.8) also records the claim, attributed to Watson et al. 1995, that people with more than one listening style "are typically ineffective communicators" [V], while the UMN text says 40% of people have more than one style and choose by situation [V]. A later Bodie line of work treats styles as habits, not types (Janusik compilation cites Bodie et al. 2019 "Connective, Reflective, Analytical, and Conceptual" [V]; not read).
- **What a lesson may say:** people report different listening preferences and can adapt them; the popular four-type scheme came from a measure later revised because it was unreliable. **May not** present "listening styles" as fixed personality types.

#### 2.7 Vangelisti, Knapp and Daly (1990), "Conversational narcissism"

- **Citation:** Vangelisti, A. L., Knapp, M. L., & Daly, J. A. (1990). Conversational narcissism. *Communication Monographs*, 57(4), 251 to 274. DOI 10.1080/03637759009376202.
- **What I read:** ERIC abstract [V] and a search-result abstract (snippet only). Full text not read.
- **Finding (ERIC [V]):** identifies behavioural referents: "boasting, refocusing the topic of conversation on the self, exaggerating hand and body movements, using a loud tone of voice, and 'glazing over' when others speak." Suggests the phenomenon "is determined interactively". Search-result abstract says six studies (snippet only; number of studies and samples to be checked).
- **Why it matters:** this is the communication-science follow-up to Derber (section 7): it treats conversational narcissism as partly a matter of how *both* people behave, not a trait of one bad listener. Lessons should cite this alongside Derber.

#### 2.8 Janusik and Rouillard, "Research Findings on Listening" (Global Listening Centre)

- A 25-page bullet-point compilation of listening findings with a 15-page reference list [V]. It is **useful as a map and unreliable as a source**. It says itself that it is "meant as a starting point" and asks readers to "refer to the source documents" [V]. Examples of distortion: it says physicians "interrupt 69% of patient interviews within 18 seconds" and that "in 77% of the interviews, the patient's true reason for visiting was never elicited (Beckman and Richard, 1984)" [V]; the 1984 study is usually cited as Beckman and Frankel, and its headline finding is about completion of the opening statement, not the "true reason" never being elicited. Do not cite this compilation as evidence for anything; use it to find primary papers.

---

### 3. Where the stock numbers come from

#### 3.1 "We spend 45% of our communication time listening"

Chain of transmission, as far as I could trace it today:

1. **Rankin (1926 dissertation; 1929 conference; 1930 article).** Paul T. Rankin, Detroit public schools. Janusik's table cites "Rankin, P.T. (1930). Listening Ability: Its Importance, Measurement, and Development. *Chicago Schools Journal*, 12, 177-179" [V] and gives Rankin's percentages as **listening 42%, speaking 32%, reading 15%, writing 11%** [V]. Usera (LibreTexts) also gives 42% and 32% and dates it 1926 [V]. Worthington and Bodie describe Rankin (1926) as asking "adults to chart their waking hours devoted to several communication activities" [V]. I could not open Rankin's own article (JSTOR returned a bot challenge).
2. **Nichols and Stevens (1957), *Are You Listening?*** (McGraw-Hill). Full text on Internet Archive, read [V]. Their retelling: "In 1929 ... Rankin had selected 68 adults of different occupations and asked them to keep tabs, every fifteen minutes"; "the survey was carried on by the 68 people for approximately two months"; they spent "9 per cent in writing, 16 per cent in reading, 30 per cent in talking and 45 per cent in listening" [V]. Nichols then concedes the survey "may mean little if harshly analyzed in terms of scientific research" [V].
   So the **45% figure is Nichols and Stevens's version**; Rankin's published figure, as reported by the listening field's own compilers, is 42%. I have not seen Rankin's text, so I cannot say which is right; the two secondary accounts disagree.
3. **Textbooks.** The UMN open textbook says "research shows that adults spend about 45 percent of their time listening", citing Hargie (2011), p. 177, and adds workers 55% and managers 63% [V]. Hargie's own source was not checked.
4. **Later time studies** (Janusik table [V]): Barker et al. 1980, US college students, 53% listening, but the table marks it as "Total Listening Related Activities combines listening, TV, radio, CD's, tapes, and telephone" [V]; Weinrauch and Swanda 1975 (business personnel) 33%; Werner 1975 55%; Bohlken 1999 53%; Davis 2001 (Australian students) 34.1%; US Department of Labor 1991 55%.
5. **Janusik and Wolvin (2009).** "24 Hours in a Day: A Listening Update to the Time Studies", *International Journal of Listening* 23(2), 104 to 120, DOI 10.1080/10904010903014442. ERIC abstract [V]: "Undergraduate students (N = 680) at a large Eastern university reported that they spend most of their time (48%) communicating with their friends"; "Students spend 24% of their time listening, 20% speaking, 13% using the Internet, 9% writing, and 8% reading." Full text: not obtained (Academia 403; T&F paywall). The Janusik table [V] also reports an earlier Janusik and Wolvin 2006 sample (N = 206) with interpersonal listening 24% but "Total Listening Related Activities" (including TV, radio, phone, recorded music) 50%.

**Assessment.** Every one of these is a self-report or diary estimate from a convenience sample (68 Detroit adults; students at one university). The category "listening" changes from study to study (with or without media; with or without the phone), and once people multitask the percentages stop adding up. The defensible statement is roughly: "time-use surveys going back to the 1920s, all self-reported, have found listening to be the communication activity people report doing most, with estimates from about a quarter to over half of communication time depending on what counts as listening." **The single figure "45%" should not appear as a fact.** Usera's claim that Janusik and Wolvin 2009 found "at least 50% of our day listening" [V] conflates the 2006 total-with-media figure with the 2009 study, whose abstract says 24%.

#### 3.2 "We remember only 50% immediately, 25% after two months"

- **Source:** Nichols and Stevens, "Listening to People", *Harvard Business Review*, September 1957. HBR's page is paywalled; I read a full reprint hosted by Columbia University's Ombuds Office [V]. They write that in testing "several thousand students and ... hundreds of business and professional people", each of whom "listened to short talks by faculty members and was examined for his grasp of the content", they concluded: "immediately after the average person has listened to someone talk, he remembers only about half of what he has heard" [V]; and "two months after listening to a talk, the average listener will remember only about 25% of what was said" [V], citing research at Florida State and Michigan State. They add "we tend to forget from one-half to one-third of it within eight hours" [V].
- **Problems:** (a) the material is short lecture-style talks with a comprehension test, not conversation; (b) no data are given in the article; (c) Nichols's own 1948 study, as summarised by Worthington and Bodie, found **68%** average recall [V]; (d) the listening field's own history says recall of lectures "is qualitatively different from recall in an interpersonal context" [V]. The UMN textbook gives a different set of numbers ("forget about half ... immediately", "35 percent after eight hours", "20 percent after a day", citing Hargie 2011 [V]), which look like a mix of Nichols and the Ebbinghaus forgetting curve; not traced.
- **Verdict:** a lesson may say that early tests of lecture listening found people answered only part of the content questions correctly and forgot more over time. It may not say "we only remember 25% of what people tell us" as a finding about conversation.

#### 3.3 Speech rate versus thought rate

- Nichols and Stevens 1957: "The average rate of speech for most Americans is around 125 words per minute" and thought runs "at speeds much higher than 125 words per minute" [V]; they give no figure for thought. UMN textbook: speech "125 to 175 words per minute", processing "400 and 800 words per minute" (Hargie 2011) [V]. Headlee (TED 2015): "The average person talks at about 225 word per minute, but we can listen at up to 500 words per minute" [V].
- Three popular sources give three different speech rates (125, 125 to 175, 225). "Thought speed" in words per minute is not a well-defined measurement. The **idea** (listening leaves spare capacity that wanders) is Nichols's and is plausible; the **numbers** should not be taught (gate).

#### 3.4 The Conaway (1982) freshman statistic

- UMN textbook: "four hundred first-year students were given a listening test before they started classes. At the end of that year, 49 percent of the students with low scores were on academic probation, while only 4 percent of those who scored high were (Conaway, 1982)" [V]. Janusik compilation repeats it [V]. Primary source (Conaway, M. S., 1982, a chapter in a reading-and-study-skills volume) not found online. Unreplicated single cohort, correlational, listening test confounded with general academic ability (Kelly's finding in 2.1 is the reason to worry). Gate; do not use as a causal claim.

---

### 4. Open textbooks usable as free links

#### 4.1 *Communication in the Real World: An Introduction to Communication Studies*, Chapter 5 "Listening" (University of Minnesota Libraries Publishing, 2016, CC BY-NC-SA 4.0)

- **What I read:** the chapter landing page on open.lib.umn.edu and sections 5.1, 5.2 and 5.3 in full from the Thomas Edison State University mirror (opened.tesu.edu), about 18,000 words of HTML [V]. (The open.lib.umn.edu chapter URLs I tried returned "not found"; the part page works; the TESU mirror is complete.)
- **Content:** 5.1 defines listening as "the learned process of receiving, interpreting, recalling, evaluating, and responding to verbal and nonverbal messages" [V]; hearing is "the physical process" and listening "the cognitive process" [V]; back-channel cues, paraphrasing, clarifying questions; four types (discriminative, informational, critical, empathetic, citing Watson, Barker and Weaver 1995); four styles from the LSP-16. 5.2 barriers: environmental, physiological, speech-thought rate, "response preparation" (rehearsing your reply), multitasking, prejudice; bad listening practices: interrupting (with a good short passage on turn-taking as a "dance"), distorted listening, eavesdropping, aggressive listening, narcissistic listening, pseudo-listening, each with short dialogues. 5.3 competence at each stage (citing Ridge 1993), active listening behaviours, "covert coaching", "mental bracketing", note-taking, critical listening and fallacies, empathetic listening, listening across relationships, professions and cultures.
