- **Quality:** clear, well organised, readable, practical, with good short dialogues (the narcissistic-listening example is ready-made teaching material). **Weaknesses:** it repeats the 45% time claim, the Hargie forgetting percentages, the 125 to 175 versus 400 to 800 wpm numbers, and the Conaway statistic without caveat; it teaches the LSP-16 four styles that the field has since revised; and its active-listening checklist (eye contact, smiling, raised eyebrows, leaning in) is presented as generic without the cultural qualifications it gives elsewhere. Usable as a free link for the stages, barriers and bad-practice sections; not as a source for numbers.

#### 4.2 LibreTexts: Usera, *Communicating to Connect: Interpersonal Communication for Today*, Chapter 7 "Listening"

- **What I read:** section 7.1 "The Fundamentals of Listening" [V] (about 1,500 words of prose).
- **Content:** asks learners to define listening; quotes the ILA and NCA definitions; cites Glenn (1989) content analysis of 50 definitions (five most frequent features: perception, attention, interpretation, remembering, responding) [V]; notes models with five (Adler, DeVito), six (Brownell) or seven (Worthington and Fitch-Hauser 2012) components [V].
- **Quality:** good on the plurality of definitions (it is the only free text I found that frames definitions as contested, in line with Worthington and Bodie). **Errors:** misstates Janusik and Wolvin 2009 as "at least 50% of our day listening" [V] (see 3.1); its memory section uses 9/11 as an example of vivid, accurate long-term memory, which is the classic case where vividness and accuracy come apart (flashbulb-memory research). Link to it for "what counts as listening" only.

#### 4.3 OpenStax

No OpenStax interpersonal communication or listening title found. (OpenStax has psychology and sociology texts whose relevant chapters belong to Understanding People.)

#### 4.4 Other candidates seen but not read

Whatcom *Introduction to Public Communication* 5.5; WTCS Pressbooks *Oral/Interpersonal Communication* ch. 3. Search listings only.

---

### 5. Course documents (syllabi and outlines)

#### 5.1 Ohio State University, COMM 3620 Introduction to Interpersonal Communication (online, Summer 2020, instructor Ott)

- **Read:** full syllabus PDF [V].
- **Sequence (12 weeks):** intro; interpersonal communication and the self (Johari window); perceiving others (in-groups and out-groups); communicating across culture (TED talk on cross-cultural communication); **listening in week 6** (McCornack ch 7, a YouTube video on "Common Ways not to Listen", assignment: reflect on own listening habits and analyse listening in a media example); midterm; verbal and nonverbal (assigns Amy Cuddy's TED talk "Your body language may shape who you are" [V]); conflict; romantic, family, friends.
- **Notable choices:** listening comes after self, perception and culture, i.e. after the learner has the idea that perception filters meaning. The assignment of Cuddy's power-posing talk, whose headline hormonal claim did not replicate, is exactly the kind of popular body-language claim our outcome 5 asks learners to weigh; it shows such material is still assigned uncritically in university courses.

#### 5.2 Portland Community College, COMM 105 Listening (Course Content and Outcomes Guide, dated October 2009)

- **Read:** full CCOG page [V].
- **Content themes [V]:** communication theories; fundamentals of listening; "Types, styles, and motives for listening"; "Process of hearing, remembering, interpreting, evaluating, and responding"; "Role of listening in nonverbal communication"; "Misunderstandings and errors in listening"; listening in academic, business, family, therapy and group contexts; "Constraints of culture, gender, and self-perception in listening". Approved texts: Brownell 5th ed.; Worthington and Fitch-Hauser 1st ed. [V].
- **Notable:** a whole 4-credit course on listening alone exists at community-college level; its competencies include identifying one's own listening strengths and weaknesses, which is a self-assessment step our course could borrow.

#### 5.3 Ohio State University, ARTSCI 113X "What is conversation all about? How people talk and why it matters" (first-year seminar, Taleghani-Nikazm, posted 2024)

- **Read:** full syllabus PDF [V].
- **Core text:** Elizabeth Stokoe (2018), *Talk: The Science of Conversation* [V].
- **Sequence [V]:** 1 Introduction (Stokoe's WIRED talk on controlling a conversation "with a single word"); 2 **Turn-taking**; 3 Action and understanding; 4 to 5 **Preference** (granting and declining, "uhm, I'm tired"); 6 to 7 **Sequence** (adjacency pairs, noticeable absences, pre-announcements like "Guess what happened to me?"); 8 to 9 **Repair**; 10 The anatomy of a turn-at-talk (turn design: request as question, order, or gesture); 11 **Stories**; 12 **Openings and closings**; 13 What are the benefits of analysing conversations?; 14 presentations.
- **Notable choices:** assessment is 40% weekly observation journals (students notice these features in their own conversations), which fits a self-study format well; it teaches on real recorded conversation, not advice. This is the conversation-analysis sequence (it mirrors Sidnell's *Conversation Analysis: An Introduction* chapter order: turn-taking, action, preference, sequence, repair, turn construction, stories, openings and closings, per search description, snippet only).

#### 5.4 EMCA4RJ "Sample Reading Syllabus" (ethnomethodology and conversation analysis for racial justice network)

- **Read:** full page [V]. A reading list, not a course. Core texts suggested: Garcia (2013) *An Introduction to Interaction* ("most suitable for those new to CA"), Sidnell (ed., 2009) *Conversation Analysis: Comparative Perspectives*, Clift (2016) *Conversation Analysis* ("a bit 'tougher'") [V]. Organised as I turns, adjacency and preference; II sequences and actions; III big sequences, alignment and affiliation; IV institutional talk, laughter, knowledge. Notable for its emphasis on cross-linguistic work (Stivers et al. 2009 PNAS on turn-taking universals appears twice) and on non-English data, which bears on our outcome 6.

#### 5.5 Not obtained

LSE PB437 "Conversation Analysis and the Science of Social Interaction" (the 2023/24 course guide URL now redirects to the calendar home page); Loughborough, UCLA, Rutgers modules (no public PDF syllabi found in two searches); MIT OCW (nothing relevant found).

---

### 6. Popular books a learner will meet

#### 6.1 Celeste Headlee, TED talk "10 ways to have a better conversation" (TEDxCreativeCoast 2015) and *We Need to Talk* (Harper Wave, 2017)

- **Read:** full TED transcript (about 1,950 words) [V]; the book excerpt published on TED Ideas, "Why we should all stop saying 'I know exactly how you feel'" (about 1,500 words, "Excerpted with permission" from the book) [V]. Rest of the book not read.
- **Claims (talk):** ten rules: don't multitask; don't pontificate; use open-ended questions (start with "who, what, when, where, why or how"; ask "What was that like?" rather than "Were you terrified?"); go with the flow (let your own thoughts go); if you don't know, say so; "Don't equate your experience with theirs"; don't repeat yourself; stay out of the weeds; listen; be brief [V]. She explicitly rejects performed attention: advice to "look, nod and smile to show that you're paying attention, repeat back what you just heard or summarize it ... It is crap" and "There is no reason to learn how to show you're paying attention if you are in fact paying attention" [V].
- **Evidence leaned on:** Pew research (polarisation; teen texting), an *Atlantic* piece by a teacher, M. Scott Peck, Stephen Covey, quotations from Buddha, Coolidge, Bill Nye, Stephen Hawking. No listening research in the talk.
- **Weaker than it sounds:** (a) "Pew Research did a study of 10,000 American adults, and they found that at this moment, we are more polarized ... than we ever have been in history" [V]; Pew's 2014 report (N = 10,013) says "more divided along ideological lines ... than at any point in the last two decades" [T, pewresearch.org]. Two decades, not all of history. (b) The 225 versus 500 wpm figures (3.3). (c) The categorical "it is crap" about paraphrasing goes beyond any evidence; there is research on paraphrasing and perceived listening (Weger, Castle and Emmett 2010, cited in the Janusik compilation [V]; not read by me; likely covered by another part) that points both ways. (d) "Don't equate your experience with theirs" is presented as a rule; it is a good default in grief and complaint, less obviously right in ordinary talk where matching disclosures is how people build closeness (see contested question C3).
- **Book excerpt:** tells her own story of answering a grieving friend with her own loss ("Okay, Celeste, you win") [V]; introduces Derber's shift and support responses; says "Modern humans are hardwired to talk about themselves more than any other topic" and cites "One study" that "most social conversation time is devoted to statements about the speaker's own emotional experiences and/or relationships, or those of third parties not present" [V] (this is a quotation from a study she does not name in the excerpt; it reads like Dunbar, Marriott and Duncan 1997; gate); gives a loose neuroanatomy (insula, limbic regions); and describes a Max Planck study (Tania Singer's group) on "emotional egocentricity bias" with maggot and puppy stimuli [V] (gate: check the actual stimuli and design). **Strength:** the personal story and the shift/support contrast are vivid and teachable. **Weakness:** "hardwired" and the brain-region narrative are popular-science overreach.

#### 6.2 Kate Murphy, *You're Not Listening: What You're Missing and Why It Matters* (Celadon, 2020)

- **Read:** publisher excerpt (the introduction, about 1,000 words) [V]; Kirkus review [V]. Rest not read.
- **Claims:** listening is undervalued and "we are engaged in a dialogue of the deaf" [V]; schools teach debate and rhetoric but not listening; technology and social media worsen it; "To really listen is to be moved physically, chemically, emotionally, and intellectually by another person's narrative" [V]. Method, in her words: "the better part of two years delving into the academic research" and "hundreds of hours of inter-views" with spies, priests, bartenders, hostage negotiators and others [V]. Kirkus summarises her advice: curiosity, openness, treating listening like meditation (notice distraction, return attention to the speaker), and that familiarity makes us listen worst to those closest to us; and reports her claim that when people with "staunch political views" are challenged, "their brains reacted as if they were being chased by a bear" [V, Kirkus quoting the book].
- **Evidence leaned on:** journalist interviews plus academic research (Kirkus says she "delves into the academic research on listening, which tends to be scantier than that on its noisier cousin, talking" [V]).
- **Weaker than it sounds:** (a) the "chased by a bear" line is a gloss on a small fMRI study of political belief challenge (Kaplan, Gimbel and Harris 2016, about 40 participants, as I recall; not read; gate); (b) the excerpt misattributes "Nature hath given men one tongue but two ears" to Epictetus [V as her text]; the saying is traditionally credited to Zeno of Citium via Diogenes Laertius (gate); (c) the decline-of-listening thesis is asserted, not measured. **Strength:** well written; the "familiarity makes us worse listeners to close others" point has research behind it (the "closeness-communication bias", Savitsky et al. 2011; not read; gate).

#### 6.3 Charles Duhigg, *Supercommunicators: How to Unlock the Secret Language of Connection* (Random House / Cornerstone, 2024)

- **Read:** Penguin UK free sample PDF (prologue and part of chapter 1, about 6,000 words once de-duplicated; the PDF text layer is doubled, so I read it through a de-duplication script) [V]; Kirkus review [V].
- **Claims:** three kinds of conversation, each with its own question: practical ("what is this really about?"), emotional ("how do we feel?"), social ("who are we?") [V, Kirkus]; the "matching principle" (identify which kind of conversation you are in and match it) [V, chapter heading in sample]; "looping for understanding" (ask, summarise, check you got it right) (snippet only, from reviews). Neural entrainment: "When we 'click' with someone, our eyes often start to dilate in tandem; our pulses match; we feel the same emotions and start to complete each other's sentences within our heads. This is known as neural entrainment" [V, from de-duplicated sample text]. He cites Thalia Wheatley's work and a 2010 *PNAS* study (Stephens, Silbert and Hasson): "The more people's brains had synchronized, the better they understood what was said" [V].
- **Weaker than it sounds:** (a) the neural-synchrony research is real but small (fMRI studies with a handful of speakers and listeners) and correlational; "synchrony causes understanding" or "you can train synchrony" is not established; pupil and pulse "matching" are separate literatures bundled together (gate). (b) The sample opens a section with "'The single biggest problem with communication,' said the playwright George Bernard Shaw, 'is the illusion it has taken place.'" [V]. Quote Investigator traces the idea to William H. Whyte, *Fortune*, 1950 ("The great enemy of communication, we find, is the illusion of it"), and finds no evidence Shaw said it (search result summary and snippet only; the Quote Investigator page itself returned a bot challenge; gate). (c) The book is built on narrative case studies (a CIA recruiter, a *Big Bang Theory* writers' room, jurors) with research woven in; reviewers note the anecdotes can feel dramatised (snippet only). **Strength:** the three-kinds-of-conversation frame is a genuinely useful teaching device for "is this person asking for help, sympathy, or recognition?", which maps onto Adler's advice to choose a response by what the other person wants. It should be taught as a heuristic, not a finding.

#### 6.4 Michael P. Nichols and Martha B. Straus, *The Lost Art of Listening: How Learning to Listen Can Improve Relationships*, 3rd ed. (Guilford, 2021)

- **Read:** Guilford's free chapter 1 PDF, "Why Listening Is So Important" (20 pages) [V]; Guilford product page [V]. First edition 1995 (M. P. Nichols alone); "More than 175,000 in print" [V].
- **Claims:** "Few motives in human experience are as powerful as the yearning to be understood" [V]; failure to listen is at the root of relationship distress; the 3rd edition adds texting and social media. The publisher lists skills: hear what people mean, share disagreement without dismissing, get uncommunicative people to open up, "Ask for support without getting unwanted advice" [V].
- **Evidence:** a family therapist's clinical experience and case vignettes; chapter 1 cites a few survey statistics (e.g., "96% of Americans have cell phones" [V]) and says "Studies suggest" about digital communication without detail. **Weaker than it sounds:** little systematic evidence; some gender generalisations in the opening ("Wives complain that their husbands take them for granted. Husbands complain that their wives take forever to get to the point." [V]). **Strength:** the best popular treatment of *why* people fail to listen to those they love (defensiveness, reactivity), which overlaps heavily with Relationships and Family and Conflict Resolution.

#### 6.5 Dale Carnegie, *How to Win Friends and Influence People* (1936), Part Two, chapter 4 "An Easy Way to Become a Good Conversationalist"

- **Read:** the full chapter from a 1943 printing digitised by the Digital Library of India on Internet Archive (djvu text, OCR errors) [V]. Copyright status: Carnegie died 1955; the work is public domain in life-plus-70 countries from 2026, but the 1936 US publication is likely still in copyright in the US (probably to 2032) if renewed; do not link it as a free US resource without checking.
- **Claims:** the principle is "Be a good listener. Encourage others to talk about themselves" [V]. The anchor anecdote: at a dinner Carnegie listened to a botanist for hours, said almost nothing, and was later called "a most interesting conversationalist" [V]; "That kind of listening is one of the highest compliments we can pay to anyone" [V], and he quotes Jack Woodford that "few human beings are proof against the implied flattery of rapt attention" [V]. He adds that he was also "hearty in my approbation and lavish in my praise" [V]. Further anecdotes: a department-store manager who defuses a complaint by listening "from beginning to end without saying a word"; a telephone company "trouble shooter" who listened to an angry customer "for nearly three hours" [V]; Lincoln wanting "merely a friendly, sympathetic listener" [V].
- **Evidence:** anecdotes, many from his own course participants; no research. **Weaker than it sounds:** listening is framed partly as *technique for being liked* ("implied flattery"), which sits uneasily with the course's (and the institute's) view of listening as attention owed to a person. It is also a one-sided model (the listener hardly speaks), which the conversation-analysis tradition would call not a conversation at all. **Strength:** the core observation, that people like being asked about themselves and listened to, is directionally supported by modern research on question-asking and liking (Huang et al. 2017; not read by me; likely in another part).

#### 6.6 Stone, Patton and Heen, *Difficult Conversations: How to Discuss What Matters Most* (Harvard Negotiation Project; 1999; 10th-anniversary ed. 2010)

- **Not read.** Flag as **Conflict Resolution territory**. Its framework (each difficult conversation contains a "what happened" conversation, a feelings conversation and an identity conversation) is widely taught; it overlaps with Duhigg's three kinds and with Adler's climate and conflict chapters. Our course should at most point to it.

---

### 7. Derber, *The Pursuit of Attention*, and "conversational narcissism"

- **Citation:** Derber, C. (1979). *The Pursuit of Attention: Power and Individualism in Everyday Life*. Boston: G. K. Hall (Schenkman); Oxford University Press paperback 1983; 2nd ed. retitled *The Pursuit of Attention: Power and Ego in Everyday Life*, Oxford University Press, 2000, 160 pp. [T, Google Books; Internet Archive metadata V for the 1979 G. K. Hall and 1983 OUP printings].
- **What I read:** not the book (Internet Archive copies are borrow-only). I read a long secondary summary on philosophicalsociety.com that quotes Derber and reproduces his example dialogues [V for that page, not for Derber]; Wikipedia's section [V for the page]; Headlee's excerpt [V]; the Art of Manliness piece as republished on LewRockwell.com [V]. Every Derber sentence below is therefore **quoted by a secondary source** and must be checked against the book.
- **What Derber wrote (as quoted by secondary sources):** "Without attention being exchanged and distributed, there is no social life"; attention is "a unique social resource ... created anew in each encounter" (quoted on philosophicalsociety.com). "Conversational narcissism is the key manifestation of the dominant attention-getting psychology in America. It occurs in informal conversations among friends, family and coworkers" (quoted on the same page, on Wikipedia and in Headlee). "Conversational narcissism involves preferential use of the shift-response and underutilization of the support-response" (same page). Examples (same page): "John: I'm feeling really starved. / Mary: Oh, I just ate." (shift) versus "Mary: When was the last time you ate?" (support). He argues the more common form is subtle: a conversationalist "makes temporary responsive concessions to others' topics before intervening to turn the focus back to himself", mixing shift and support responses (same page). The Jim and Bill car-and-bicycle dialogue shows both speakers shifting in turn.
- **His frame:** sociological, not self-help. The secondary summary reports that Derber ties the competition for attention to weak social support in American individualist culture and to **class and gender**: powerful people command attention; women are expected to give support responses to men; the therapist-client relation institutionalises one-way support. Wikipedia calls him a Marxist sociologist [V for the page]. The 2000 subtitle change from "Individualism" to "Ego" shifts the emphasis toward the personal.
- **Method: unverified.** The Art of Manliness article says "researchers watched 1,500 conversations unfold and recorded how people traded and vied for attention" [V for that page]. A search-result summary adds that he "recorded and transcribed over a hundred informal dinner conversations" and used observers with questionnaires (snippet only). I found no primary description. Gate.
- **How it is popularised versus what he wrote:**
  1. Popular versions (Art of Manliness, Headlee, many blogs) turn it into **a personal bad habit** to fix with one rule: prefer support responses. Derber (as quoted) treats it as a **structural feature** of a competitive, individualist culture, patterned by status and gender.
  2. Popular versions often imply a large quantitative study ("1,500 conversations") and precise findings. What is quoted from Derber is conceptual and illustrated with constructed or selected dialogues; I found no reported rates or effect sizes.
  3. Popular versions treat every shift response as narcissistic. Derber's own quoted point is about **preferential** use, and the Jim and Bill example shows mutual shifting as ordinary. Vangelisti, Knapp and Daly (1990) found conversational narcissism is "determined interactively" [V, ERIC].
  4. In conversation-analysis terms, a shift can also be a *second story*, which is often how people show they understood the first; so the same move can be affiliation or competition depending on design and uptake (this is a claim for Part B or C to support from CA sources; I did not read them).
- **What a lesson may say:** "The sociologist Charles Derber (1979) named a pattern he called conversational narcissism: repeatedly turning the talk back to yourself. He distinguished a shift response from a support response." With the caveat that the empirical base is qualitative and that later communication research treated it as something two people produce together. **May not say:** "a study of 1,500 conversations found...".

---

### (a) What this part says the field considers essential for a Foundation course

From the convergence of the five textbooks, the listening-studies texts, and the syllabi:

1. **Hearing is not listening.** Every source makes this distinction; listening includes attention, understanding, remembering and responding.
2. **Listening is several processes, not one skill.** Cognitive, affective (motivation) and behavioural components (Worthington and Bodie). The staged models (five stages, HURIER's six) are teaching schemes; learners should know that scholars do not agree on one definition.
3. **Purposes of listening differ**, and the right behaviour depends on the purpose: understanding information, evaluating a claim, supporting a person. (Types: discriminative, informational or comprehensive, critical, empathic or relational; appreciative in some schemes.)
4. **The common failures**, with names learners can use to notice them in themselves: pseudolistening, self-focus (stage hogging, monopolising, narcissistic listening, Derber's shift response), selective listening, defensive listening, ambushing, literal or insensitive listening, rehearsing your reply, interrupting.
5. **A repertoire of responses and a way to choose among them**: prompting, questioning (sincere versus counterfeit), paraphrasing (tentatively, sparingly), supporting (avoiding "cold comfort"), analysing, advising, judging; choose by what the other person wants, which is Adler's lesson and Duhigg's "matching".
6. **Conversation has structure**: turn-taking, adjacency pairs, preference, repair, stories, openings and closings (the conversation-analysis syllabus sequence; DeVito's turn-taking principle). The textbooks underweight this; the CA seminar shows it can be taught to first-years through observation journals.
7. **Culture and difference** affect what counts as attentive listening (DeVito's culture-and-listening section; Imhof and Janusik on German versus US conceptions, via the Janusik compilation [V]).
8. **Critical use of evidence**: the field's own historians (Worthington and Bodie) say the classic measures were recall tests with weak validity and much training has been atheoretical. A Foundation course should teach the stock numbers as examples of how claims travel.

### (b) Common misconceptions (each named by a source)

1. **"We spend 45% of our communication time listening" is a measured fact.** Traced in 3.1: a self-report diary of 68 adults (Rankin), whose published figure the listening field gives as 42%, retold as 45% by Nichols and Stevens (1957), who themselves conceded it "may mean little if harshly analyzed" [V]; the latest student update found 24% (Janusik and Wolvin 2009, ERIC abstract [V]).
2. **"We remember only 25% (or 50%) of what we hear."** From lecture-comprehension tests (Nichols and Stevens 1957 [V]); Nichols's own 1948 study found 68% (Worthington and Bodie [V]); the field says lecture recall differs from interpersonal recall [V].
3. **Listening is a single ability you can measure with a recall test.** Named and rejected by Worthington and Bodie, citing Kelly's finding that early listening tests correlated more with intelligence than with each other [V].
4. **People are fixed "listening types" (people-, action-, content-, time-oriented).** Bodie, Worthington and Gearhart (2013): the LSP-16 had "uncharacteristically low reliability estimates and unvalidated factor structure" [V].
5. **Good listening means displaying listening (nodding, eye contact, repeating back).** Named as a misconception by Headlee ("There is no reason to learn how to show you're paying attention if you are in fact paying attention" [V]); tempered by Adler's "use paraphrasing sparingly" [V]. (But the opposite misconception, that visible signals do not matter, is also wrong: Worthington and Bodie say listener responses "are the only signals that listening is taking (or has taken) place" [V].)
6. **Conversational narcissism is a proven finding from a large study.** The "1,500 conversations" figure appears in popularisations (Art of Manliness [V]) with no primary support I could find; Derber's work as quoted is conceptual and structural.
7. **Hearing a relevant story of your own shows empathy.** Named by Headlee from her own experience ("Okay, Celeste, you win" [V]); Derber's shift response.
8. **Famous quotations about communication are reliable.** Duhigg's Shaw attribution [V] and Murphy's Epictetus attribution [V] are both doubtful (gates).

### (c) Contested questions

**C1. How much of our time is spent listening, and does it matter for the argument?**
- *Positions:* The time-study tradition (Rankin; Nichols and Stevens; Barker et al. 1980; Janusik and Wolvin 2009) holds that listening is the most-used communication activity, which justifies teaching it. Critics of the method (Nichols and Stevens themselves concede weakness [V]; the variation 24% to 55% across studies [V]) hold the numbers are not meaningful as point estimates.
- *Best case for each:* for the tradition, every study for a century points the same direction (listening is at or near the top). Against, definitions and self-report make the percentages artefacts of the categories.
- *What would settle it:* experience-sampling or passive audio recording (e.g., wearable recorders) with a fixed definition of listening, across ages and cultures.
- *Classification:* contested empirical (magnitude); the direction is reasonably established.

**C2. Does "active listening" (paraphrasing and reflecting) make people feel heard, or is it mechanical?**
- *Positions:* textbooks (UMN "Active Listening", DeVito "Active Listening") teach it as a core skill. Adler's outline qualifies it ("remain tentative", "use sparingly" [V]). Headlee rejects the performed version outright [V]. Duhigg promotes "looping for understanding" (snippet only).
- *Best case for each:* for, paraphrase is the only way the speaker can check you understood (it serves outcome 2 directly). Against, formulaic paraphrase is felt as technique and can irritate.
- *What would settle it:* experiments comparing paraphrase, simple acknowledgement and questions on the speaker's felt understanding and accuracy of the listener's understanding, in natural conversation, across relationships and cultures. (Primary studies such as Weger et al. 2010 are for another part.)
- *Classification:* contested empirical.

**C3. Is sharing a similar experience (a shift response) a failure of listening?**
- *Positions:* Derber (as quoted) and Headlee: shift responses are the mark of conversational narcissism; prefer support responses. Adler lists "empathizing" and "agreement" within supporting responses [V], and self-disclosure research (DeVito's disclosure section) treats reciprocal disclosure as how closeness is built.
- *Best case for each:* in grief and distress, a matched story can compete with the speaker (Headlee's example). In ordinary talk, a second story can show understanding and invite more.
- *What would settle it:* studies coding second stories versus support responses and measuring the first speaker's felt understanding, separated by context (distress versus sociable talk).
- *Classification:* contested empirical, with a value element (what we owe a person in distress).

**C4. Are listening styles stable traits or situational habits?**
- *Positions:* Watson, Barker and Weaver (1995) as reported: people rely on one predominant style and multiple styles confuse others [V via Janusik]; Imhof (2004) and Bodie: styles are flexible habits activated by goals [V via Janusik and LSP-R].
- *What would settle it:* longitudinal and situational designs with validated measures (LSP-R is a step).
- *Classification:* contested empirical, leaning toward "habits".

**C5. Is listening (and conversation) declining because of technology?**
- *Positions:* Murphy, Headlee, Nichols and Straus assert decline; none of the excerpts I read measures it.
- *What would settle it:* comparable measures over time. Out of scope for this part; flag for others.
- *Classification:* contested empirical.

**C6. Is listening a technique for getting what you want or attention owed to a person?**
- *Positions:* Carnegie frames it as flattery that wins friends and customers [V]; Derber frames the distribution of attention as a question of social justice and status [quoted by secondary]; Nichols and Straus frame it as care in relationships.
- *Classification:* value question. The course should state its own standpoint openly.

### (d) Gates: claims a drafter must re-check against a primary text before a lesson states them

1. Any percentage of time spent listening (Rankin's 42% versus Nichols's 45%; Janusik and Wolvin's 24%). Re-check Rankin 1929/1930 and the Janusik and Wolvin 2009 full text.
2. Any retention figure (50%, 25%, 68%, the 35%/20% "Hargie" figures). Re-check Nichols 1948 (*Speech Monographs*) and Hargie's source.
3. Speech-rate and "thought-rate" words per minute (125, 150, 225; 400 to 800; 500).
4. Conaway (1982) freshman probation statistic.
5. Derber: every quotation, the method ("1,500 conversations", "a hundred dinner conversations"), and the class and gender argument. Read the 2000 OUP edition.
6. Vangelisti, Knapp and Daly (1990): number of studies, samples, findings beyond the abstract.
7. Wood's definition, stages and "forms of nonlistening" (my source is student notes).
8. Adler 16e listening chapter contents (my outline is from the 13th edition).
9. Wolvin and Coakley's five listening purposes.
10. The ILA definition's date (1995 versus 1996).
11. Headlee's "One study" on conversation topics (probably Dunbar et al. 1997) and the Max Planck egocentricity-bias study (Silani, Singer et al.): stimuli, sample, result.
12. Murphy's "chased by a bear" study (Kaplan et al. 2016?): design and sample.
13. Duhigg's neural-synchrony claims (Stephens, Silbert and Hasson 2010: sample size and what was correlated; Wheatley's work); "pupils dilate in tandem", "pulses match".
14. Quote attributions: "illusion that it has taken place" (Shaw versus Whyte 1950); "two ears and one mouth" (Epictetus versus Zeno); Covey's "listen with the intent to reply"; Coolidge's "No man ever listened himself out of a job".
15. The Beckman and Frankel (1984) physician-interruption figures, if used (the Janusik compilation's version is garbled).
16. Anything taken from the Janusik and Rouillard compilation: re-check the primary.

### (e) Best free resources to link (all fetched today, 2026-09-26, HTTP 200)

- UMN, *Communication in the Real World*, Chapter 5 Listening, sections 5.1 to 5.3 (full text mirror): https://opened.tesu.edu/umncommunication/chapter/5-1-understanding-how-and-why-we-listen/ (and 5-2, 5-3). Clear stages, barriers and bad-practice dialogues; skip its numbers. Canonical landing page: https://open.lib.umn.edu/communication/part/chapter-5-listening/
- Worthington and Bodie, "Defining Listening" (publisher's free chapter): https://catalogimages.wiley.com/images/db/pdf/9781119103073.excerpt.pdf. The best short history of what listening research measured and why the classic numbers are weak.
- ILA definition: https://www.listen.org/listening-definition. The field's consensus definition, one line.
- Nichols and Stevens, *Are You Listening?* (1957), Internet Archive: https://archive.org/details/areyoulistening00ralp. Primary source for the Rankin retelling and the 25% claim, with Nichols's own caveat. (The 1957 HBR article "Listening to People" is paywalled at https://hbr.org/1957/09/listening-to-people; a reprint is hosted by Columbia's Ombuds Office at https://ombuds.columbia.edu/sites/default/files/content/pics/30%20Anniv/Listening%20to%20People.pdf, whose permission status I cannot confirm.)
- Bodie, Worthington and Gearhart (2013), LSP-R, author-posted PDF: https://static1.squarespace.com/static/5633a3ade4b02b1547969346/t/572d1bdb40261d2ef97f3eb2/1462574044157/LSPR+CQ+FINAL+PUBLISHED.pdf. For the correction to "listening styles".
- Headlee TED talk and transcript: https://www.ted.com/talks/celeste_headlee_10_ways_to_have_a_better_conversation/transcript. The popular talk learners will know; good to critique.
- Headlee book excerpt on conversational narcissism: https://ideas.ted.com/why-we-should-all-stop-saying-i-know-exactly-how-you-feel/. A vivid shift-response story.
- OSU first-year seminar "What is conversation all about?": https://firstyearseminars.osu.edu/sites/default/files/2024-06/What%20is%20Conversation%20All%20About.pdf. A model sequence for teaching conversation structure by observation.
- Guilford free chapter, *The Lost Art of Listening* ch 1: https://www.guilford.com/excerpts/nichols_ch1.pdf?t=1. For why people fail to listen to those close to them.
- Penguin sample, *Supercommunicators*: https://cdn.penguin.co.uk/dam-assets/books/9781847943644/9781847943644-sample.pdf. Useful for the three-kinds frame; text layer is doubled.
- Celadon excerpt, *You're Not Listening*: https://celadonbooks.com/excerpt-youre-not-listening/. Introduction only.
- Not recommended as a learner link: Janusik and Rouillard compilation (map only, errors); LibreTexts Usera 7.1 (errors noted in 4.2), though acceptable for "what counts as listening".

### (f) Not read

Full texts of all five textbooks (read only TOCs, publisher outlines, a publisher preface and secondary references); Adler 16e listening chapter; Wood's listening chapter; Interplay 16e; DeVito 16e internal headings; McCornack's listening chapter; Hargie's *Skilled Interpersonal Communication* beyond the 7th-edition TOC (front-matter preview read; chapter 7 "the skill of listening" and chapter 5 "questioning" not read); Brownell 7e text; Worthington, Bodie and Fitch-Hauser 3e text; Wolvin and Coakley; Rankin's own article and dissertation; Janusik and Wolvin 2009 full text; Barker et al. 1980; Nichols 1948; Conaway 1982; Vangelisti, Knapp and Daly 1990 full text; Derber (any edition); Headlee's and Murphy's and Duhigg's books beyond the excerpts; Stone, Patton and Heen; Carnegie beyond Part Two chapter 4; Stokoe's *Talk*; Sidnell's *Conversation Analysis*; Weger, Castle and Emmett 2010; Huang et al. 2017; Kaplan et al. 2016; Stephens, Silbert and Hasson 2010; Dunbar et al. 1997; Beckman and Frankel 1984; Savitsky et al. 2011; the LSE, Loughborough, UCLA and Rutgers syllabi (not obtained).

UNREAD: Looking Out Looking In 16th edition full text, Everyday Encounters full text, Interplay 16th edition full text, DeVito 16th edition full text, Reflect and Relate full text, Hargie Skilled Interpersonal Communication chapters, Brownell Listening 7th edition text, Worthington Bodie Fitch-Hauser Listening 3rd edition text, Wolvin and Coakley Listening, Rankin 1930, Rankin 1926 dissertation, Janusik and Wolvin 2009 full text, Barker et al 1980, Nichols 1948, Conaway 1982, Vangelisti Knapp Daly 1990 full text, Derber 1979, Derber 2000, We Need to Talk full book, You're Not Listening full book, Supercommunicators full book, Difficult Conversations, Stokoe Talk, Sidnell Conversation Analysis, Weger Castle Emmett 2010, Huang et al 2017, Kaplan Gimbel Harris 2016, Stephens Silbert Hasson 2010, Dunbar Marriott Duncan 1997, Beckman Frankel 1984, Savitsky et al 2011, Silani Singer egocentricity bias study, Watson Barker Weaver 1995, Glenn 1989, Whyte 1950 Fortune

---

## Part B: How conversation works (conversation analysis and the modern psychology of conversation)

This part covers the machinery of talk as conversation analysis (CA) describes it (turn-taking, adjacency pairs, preference, repair, overlap), the timing research that tested CA's claims with numbers across languages, the interruption and gender literature and its critiques, and the post-2014 social psychology of conversation (questions, the liking gap, endings, deep versus shallow talk, talking to strangers, self-disclosure). Read on 2026-09-26. Method: primary texts fetched with curl from author sites, PubMed Central, university repositories and the ISCA Schegloff archive, converted with pdftotext or pandoc; two scanned book chapters (Zimmerman and West 1975; Pomerantz 1984) were OCR'd with tesseract and the key figures checked against the page images. Raw extracts are in `cl/B/`. Quotations marked [V] were copied from text I extracted myself (OCR'd texts are marked [V, OCR]). Where I read only an abstract or a secondary account, the entry says so.

---

### 1. The turn-taking system (conversation analysis)

#### 1.1 Sacks, Schegloff and Jefferson 1974

**Citation.** Sacks, H., Schegloff, E. A., & Jefferson, G. (1974). A simplest systematics for the organization of turn-taking for conversation. *Language*, 50(4), 696–735. DOI 10.2307/412243. Read: full text (ISCA Schegloff archive PDF, https://www.conversationanalysis.org/mp-files/simplest-systematics-for-the-organization-of-turn-taking-for-conversation-language.pdf/).

**Design.** Qualitative analysis of audio-recorded, transcribed American English conversation (group therapy sessions, telephone calls, everyday talk). No statistics. The paper proposes a model and checks it against a list of observable facts.

**The "grossly apparent facts".** The authors list 14 facts any model must account for. The first four are the ones lessons usually cite:
- (1) speaker change recurs, or at least occurs;
- (2) "Overwhelmingly, one party talks at a time" [V];
- (3) "Occurrences of more than one speaker at a time are common, but brief" [V];
- (4) transitions with no gap and no overlap are common; together with transitions with slight gap or slight overlap they make up the vast majority.
The rest: turn order, turn size, conversation length, content and relative distribution of turns are not fixed in advance; number of parties can vary; talk can be continuous or discontinuous; allocation techniques exist (current speaker selects next, or self-selection); turn-constructional units range from one word to sentences; repair mechanisms exist for turn-taking errors (if two start at once, one stops early).

**The rule set (section 3.3).** Paraphrase: turns are built from turn-constructional units (TCUs), and the end of each possible unit is a "transition-relevance place" (TRP). At the first TRP: (1a) if the current speaker has selected a next speaker (for example by addressing a question to them), that party has the right and obligation to speak next; (1b) if not, any party may self-select, and the first starter gets the turn; (1c) if not, the current speaker may, but need not, continue. (2) If the current speaker continues, the rules reapply at the next TRP, recursively. The ordering matters: 1a outranks 1b, which outranks 1c. The authors sum up the model as locally managed, "party-administered, interactionally controlled, and sensitive to recipient design" [V].

**On universality, from the paper itself.** A footnote says cross-cultural validity "can, of course, be settled only empirically" and reports consistency with Thai and New Guinea creole materials. So the 1974 paper claimed a general system but explicitly left cross-cultural validation open. That was later tested (Stivers et al. 2009, section 2).

**What a lesson may say.** That conversation runs on a locally managed turn-taking system in which people project when a turn will end and time their entry to it; that overlap is common but brief; that most transitions are close to seamless. That this was an observational, qualitative analysis of English talk. **May not say** that Sacks et al. measured gap lengths or percentages (they did not; the numbers came decades later).

#### 1.2 Schegloff and Sacks 1973: adjacency pairs and closings

**Citation.** Schegloff, E. A., & Sacks, H. (1973). Opening up closings. *Semiotica*, 8(4), 289–327. Read: full text (ISCA archive PDF).

**Findings.** Defines the adjacency pair: sequences with "(1) two utterance length, (2) adjacent positioning of component utterances, (3) different speakers producing each utterance" [V], plus relative ordering (first pair part before second) and "discriminative relations" (the first part constrains what counts as a fitting second). Examples: question-answer, greeting-greeting, offer-acceptance/refusal. The basic rule: once a recognisable first pair part is produced, at its first possible completion the speaker should stop and the next speaker should produce a second pair part of the same type. The paper's main topic is how conversations end: a conversation does not simply stop but is brought to a close through a "terminal exchange" (bye/bye) usually prepared by "pre-closings" (for example "well", "okay" offered at the end of a topic), which give the other person a chance to raise a new topic before the close.

**Use in a lesson.** Adjacency pairs explain why an unanswered question feels like an event (the answer is "noticeably absent"), and pre-closings give a concrete, research-based way to end conversations gracefully. This pairs well with Mastroianni et al. 2021 (section 6). Qualitative; no quantities.

#### 1.3 Preference organisation: Pomerantz 1984

**Citation.** Pomerantz, A. (1984). Agreeing and disagreeing with assessments: Some features of preferred/dispreferred turn shapes. In J. M. Atkinson & J. Heritage (Eds.), *Structures of Social Action* (pp. 57–101). Cambridge University Press. Read: full chapter (scanned PDF on the author's site, https://www.apomerantz.com/wp-content/uploads/2015/04/agreeingdisag23d.pdf, OCR'd).

**Findings.** After an assessment that invites agreement, agreements and disagreements come in different "turn shapes". Pomerantz's summary points (paraphrased from section 3): agreements fill the whole turn, disagreements are often prefaced; agreements are stated outright, disagreements range from unstated to stated and are often "partial agreements/partial disagreements"; agreements come with minimal gap, while "disagreement components are frequently delayed within a turn or over a series of turns" [V, OCR]; and silence, requests for clarification and similar are heard as not-yet-stated disagreement. Typical delay devices: no immediate talk, repair initiators ("what?"), "uh" and "well" prefaces, and agreement prefaces ("yes, but"). Crucially, preference is not the same as personal wish: after a self-deprecation, agreement becomes the dispreferred response, so disagreeing ("no, you're not") comes quickly and plainly.

**What a lesson may say.** That in English conversation, awkward or unwelcome answers tend to arrive late and wrapped (delay, "well", partial agreement first), and listeners read the delay itself. That this is a qualitative CA finding, later quantified (Kendrick and Torreira 2015; Stivers et al. 2009). **May not say** "people always" or give a threshold from this paper.

#### 1.4 Repair: Schegloff, Jefferson and Sacks 1977

**Citation.** Schegloff, E. A., Jefferson, G., & Sacks, H. (1977). The preference for self-correction in the organization of repair in conversation. *Language*, 53(2), 361–382. DOI 10.2307/413107. Read: full text (ISCA archive PDF).

**Findings.** "Repair" covers any dealing with trouble in speaking, hearing or understanding (not only errors). The paper distinguishes who starts the repair (self or other) from who completes it, and shows "a strong empirical skewing in which self-repair predominates over other-repair" [V]. The system gives the speaker the first opportunities (within the same turn, at its end, in the third turn), so others usually only initiate (for example "what?", "who?") and leave the fix to the speaker. When others do correct, "When other-corrections are done, they are frequently modulated in form" [V]: hedged with uncertainty ("Quail, I think"), put as a question, or in the "Y'mean X?" format, which also serves as a check of understanding. The authors note an exception: adult-child talk, where other-correction is more common and seems to serve socialisation.

**Use in a lesson.** Two practical, well-grounded points: (1) the respectful default is to let the speaker fix their own trouble, and correct others tentatively; (2) "You mean X?" is a naturally occurring understanding-check, which links directly to listening that checks understanding. No quantities in the paper; the "preference" is shown through distribution and sequential analysis of English data.

#### 1.5 Overlap, and its resolution: Schegloff 2000

**Citation.** Schegloff, E. A. (2000). Overlapping talk and the organization of turn-taking for conversation. *Language in Society*, 29(1), 1–63. Read: abstract, the sections on unproblematic overlap and on resolution (ISCA archive PDF).

**Findings.** Many overlaps are not treated as problems at all: "terminal overlaps" (starting as the other finishes), "continuers" ("uh huh", "mm hm"), conditional access (helping someone find a word) and "choral" talk (laughing, greeting together). For competitive overlaps Schegloff describes an "overlap resolution device" operating beat by beat (roughly syllable by syllable): he reports that "the vast majority of overlaps are resolved to a single speaker by the third beat" [V].

**Use.** Supports the distinction between overlap and interruption that the gender literature later fought over (section 3).

---

### 2. Timing: the numbers behind turn-taking

#### 2.1 Stivers et al. 2009 (PNAS)

**Citation.** Stivers, T., Enfield, N. J., Brown, P., Englert, C., Hayashi, M., Heinemann, T., Hoymann, G., Rossano, F., de Ruiter, J. P., Yoon, K.-E., & Levinson, S. C. (2009). Universals and cultural variation in turn-taking in conversation. *PNAS*, 106(26), 10587–10592. DOI 10.1073/pnas.0903616106. Read: full text (PMC2705608).

**Design.** Video recordings of informal conversation in 10 languages from 5 continents (Danish, Dutch, English, Italian, Japanese, Korean, Lao, Tzeltal, Yélî Dnye, ‡Ākhoe Hai‖om). Each contributor identified 350 consecutive questions across 5 to 17 interactions (101 conversations in total), and the analysis was restricted to polar (yes/no) questions and their responses. Response offset measured in ELAN in 10 ms steps rounded to 100 ms. The authors checked a Dutch corpus and found no difference in timing after questions versus other turns, so question-response pairs were used as a proxy for turn-taking generally.

**Findings.**
- All 10 languages show a unimodal distribution with a mode between 0 and +200 ms; overall mode 0 ms; medians 0 ms (English, Japanese, Tzeltal, Yélî Dnye) to +300 ms (Danish, ‡Ākhoe Hai‖om, Lao); overall median +100 ms.
- Means vary more: "Danish has the slowest response time on average (+469 ms) and Japanese has the fastest (+7 ms)" [V]. Cross-language mean +208 ms; every language within about 250 ms of it, roughly one English syllable.
- The same factors slow responses in every language: non-answers ("I don't know") are slower than answers; confirmations are 100 to 500 ms faster than disconfirmations (significant in 7 of 10); responses with a visible component (nod) are faster; responses to questions asked with the speaker gazing at the addressee are faster in 9 of 10 (significant in 5; Danish trended the other way).
- Ethnographic reports did not hold up well: Italians, reputed to tolerate overlap, averaged +310 ms with only 17% of responses in overlap; Japanese, reputed to leave long silences, were the fastest. Danish fitted reports of Nordic slowness, but its mode was still about 100 ms.
- Coders' judgements of what counted as "on time" differed: subjectively on-time responses averaged 203 ms in Danish and 202 ms in Lao versus 36 ms in Japanese and 83 ms in Tzeltal, so each community has its own local calibration of what counts as a delay.

Abstract summary: all languages show "a general avoidance of overlapping talk and a minimization of silence between conversational turns" [V].

**Limits.** Only polar questions; one corpus per language, often one community; offsets rounded to 100 ms; informal talk only (institutional talk has its own rules). The mean differences (for example Danish versus Japanese) are real but small in absolute terms (under half a second).

**What a lesson may say.** Across very different languages, people aim to start their reply almost as soon as the other stops, typically within a fraction of a second; cultures differ in the average by at most a few hundred milliseconds, but those small differences are felt; slow replies everywhere tend to go with "no" or non-answers. **May not say** that some cultures have "no rules" about overlap, or that Japanese speakers leave long silences (the data contradict that for this sample), or that the study proves universality for all turn types.

#### 2.2 Levinson 2016 (Trends in Cognitive Sciences) and Levinson and Torreira 2015

**Citations.** Levinson, S. C. (2016). Turn-taking in human communication: Origins and implications for language processing. *Trends in Cognitive Sciences*, 20(1), 6–14. DOI 10.1016/j.tics.2015.10.010. Read: full text (MPG PuRe author PDF). Levinson, S. C., & Torreira, F. (2015). Timing in turn-taking and its implications for processing models of language. *Frontiers in Psychology*, 6, 731. DOI 10.3389/fpsyg.2015.00731. Read: full text (PMC4464110).

**Findings (Levinson 2016, a review).** Less than 5% of the speech stream involves two or more speakers at once; the modal overlap is under 100 ms; the modal gap is about 200 ms; the system works as well without visual contact (telephone). Language production is slow: "preparation before output begins takes 600 ms for a single word if primed" [V], about 1000 ms if not, about 1500 ms for a short clause. Therefore "one must plan while still listening and predicting what the rest of the incoming turn will contain" [V]. EEG evidence suggests speech-act recognition within the first 400 ms of a turn and response planning beginning within about 500 ms of enough information being available. Levinson also argues turn-taking is phylogenetically old: proto-conversation in infants (timing near adult in the first months, then responses slow and overlap reduces from about 9 months) and vocal turn-taking in other primates (marmosets, gibbons and others), though whether that is homology or analogy is open.

**Findings (Levinson and Torreira 2015, a review plus new Switchboard analysis).** In 348 English telephone conversations (about 38 hours), 77% of the signal was one speaker, 19.2% silence, and only 3.8% simultaneous speech. Yet between-speaker overlaps were 30.1% of all floor transfers, with a modal duration of 96 ms and median 205 ms, and 73% of overlaps involved a backchannel. They also report other corpora: roughly 40% of transitions involve some overlap (over 10 ms) in Heldner and Edlund's data, and 51 to 55% of transitions take place in under 200 ms.

**What a lesson may say.** The "200 ms gap versus 600+ ms to plan a word" argument is the standard explanation for why listeners must plan their reply while the other person is still talking. A lesson can make the practical point carefully: good listening is not waiting silently to plan, since everyone plans during the other's turn; the question is what the planning is aimed at. **Gate:** do not say "overlap is rare". Overlap is frequent as a share of transitions (30 to 40% in some corpora) but tiny as a share of speaking time (under 5%). Do not say the 200 ms figure is a hard rule; it is a mode in a wide distribution.

#### 2.3 Long gaps signal trouble: Kendrick and Torreira 2015; Roberts, Francis and Morgan 2006; Roberts and Francis 2013

**Kendrick, K. H., & Torreira, F. (2015). The timing and construction of preference: A quantitative study. *Discourse Processes*, 52(4), 255–289. DOI 10.1080/0163853X.2014.955997.** Read: full text (submitted version, White Rose repository).
Design: 195 preferred and dispreferred responding actions (for example acceptances and rejections of invitations, offers, requests) from English telephone corpora. Findings: the timing of the most frequent cases did not differ systematically, but "after approximately 700 ms, the proportion of dispreferreds was greater than that of preferreds" [V]; and departures beyond a normal gap (over 300 ms) reduce the chance of an unqualified acceptance and raise the chance of a "dispreferred" format (for example "yes, but"). They reproduce Pomerantz's example in which a 1.7 s silence after "D'they have a good cook there?" leads the questioner to revise the question to "Nothing special?", showing speakers act on silence.
*Lesson use:* a long pause before a reply is a probabilistic signal, not a certainty; most short delays mean nothing. This is a useful correction to "any pause means no".

**Roberts, F., Francis, A. L., & Morgan, M. (2006). The interaction of inter-turn silence with prosodic cues in listener perceptions of "trouble" in conversation. *Speech Communication*, 48(9), 1079–1093. DOI 10.1016/j.specom.2006.02.001.** Read: not read directly; described in Kendrick and Torreira 2015 and Roberts and Francis 2013. Listeners heard simulated phone calls in which a request ("Can you give me a ride over there?") was accepted ("Sure!") after 0, 600 or 1200 ms; rated willingness fell as the gap grew. A cross-linguistic replication (Roberts, Margutti & Takano 2011, Italian and Japanese) is cited but I did not read it.

**Roberts, F., & Francis, A. L. (2013). Identifying a temporal threshold of tolerance for silent gaps after requests. *JASA Express Letters*, 133(6), EL471–EL477. DOI 10.1121/1.4802900.** Read: full text (author PDF). Between-groups design, n = 380, gaps from 200 to 1200 ms in 100 ms steps before an identical "sure" (*corrected 2026-09-26, Stage 4 note, lesson 02: this entry said "yes"; the token was "sure", see "Gate closures, lesson 02 review"*). "There was a notable drop-off in ratings at 600 ms and a statistically significant difference in ratings between 700 and 800 ms" [V]; after about 900 ms, a floor.

**What a lesson may say.** Listeners hear delay before a "yes" as reluctance, and the effect appears at well under a second (roughly 600 to 800 ms in these English experiments). **Keep to English-language, lab-rated, request-acceptance findings**; do not generalise the threshold to all cultures or contexts.

#### 2.4 Fast responses and connection: Templeton et al. 2022

**Citation.** Templeton, E. M., Chang, L. J., Reynolds, E. A., Cone LeBeaumont, M. D., & Wheatley, T. (2022). Fast response times signal social connection in conversation. *PNAS*, 119(4), e2116915119. DOI 10.1073/pnas.2116915119. Read: full text (PDF hosted on a Columbia workshop site; PMC8794835 also exists). Note the brief's author list gives "Cone LeBeaux"; the paper and its Crossref record give "Cone LeBeaumont" (Templeton, Chang, Reynolds, Cone LeBeaumont, Wheatley).

**Design and samples.**
- Study 1: 66 Dartmouth undergraduates (33 female) in six same-gender round-robin groups of 11; each had 10-minute unstructured conversations with every other member; 322 conversations recorded. Mostly strangers (prior acquaintance rating mean 8.98 on 0 to 100). After each, participants rated enjoyment, then watched the video and rated moment-to-moment connection.
- Study 2: 22 of those participants returned with three close friends each (65 conversations).
- Study 3 (preregistered, OSF u2brn): 450 MTurk listeners heard six ~10-turn audio clips (mean 23 s) from Study 1 in original (mean gap 278.55 ms), fast (one fifth, 55.68 ms) or slow (double, 557.14 ms) versions.

**Findings.** Faster partner responses predicted higher felt connection across conversations and moment to moment, for strangers and for friends (friends: b = 0.07, SE = 0.02). Partners' response speed mattered more than one's own. Third-party listeners rated the same conversation as more connected and more enjoyed when gaps were shortened. The authors argue that since very short response times (<250 ms) cannot be consciously controlled, they are an "honest signal": "Because extremely short response times (<250 ms) preclude conscious control, they provide an honest signal that even eavesdroppers use to judge how well two people "click."" [V]

**Limits.** Undergraduates at one US college, same-gender dyads only, 10-minute get-acquainted talk; correlational in Studies 1 and 2 (connection could cause speed as much as the reverse); Study 3 manipulates only perceptions of outside listeners. No independent replication found in my search.

**What a lesson may say.** In one set of US studies, quick replies went with feeling "in sync", and outsiders used speed as a cue. **Must not** turn this into advice to "reply faster". The authors' own point is that the fastest responses are not under conscious control, and the cross-cultural data (Stivers) show "fast" is locally calibrated. Also sits in tension with the advice to pause and think before answering; a lesson should present both honestly.

---

### 3. Conversational style, interruption and gender

#### 3.1 Tannen: high-involvement and high-considerateness styles

**Primary source read.** Tannen, D. (1989). Interpreting interruption in conversation. In B. Music, R. Graczyk & C. Wiltshire (Eds.), *Papers from the 25th Annual Regional Meeting of the Chicago Linguistic Society, Part Two: Parasession on Language in Context* (pp. 266–287). Chicago Linguistic Society. Read: full text (scanned PDF from the author's site, text layer). **Not read:** *Conversational Style: Analyzing Talk Among Friends* (1984; 2nd ed. 2005) and *That's Not What I Meant!* (1986); what follows about the 1984 book comes from Tannen's own 1989 summary of it and from search-result descriptions.

**Design (the 1984 study, as Tannen describes it).** Microanalysis of about two and a half hours of taped Thanksgiving dinner conversation among six friends (1978), including Tannen herself. Qualitative; no statistics.

**Claims.** Some speakers leave little or no pause between turns and often start while another is talking. Tannen calls this "high-involvement" style (priority on showing interest and enthusiasm); others, "high-considerateness", prioritise not imposing. Among like-style speakers, overlap was "cooperative overlapping", in her words "supportive rather than obstructive, evidence not of domination but of participation" [V]. When the same moves met high-considerateness speakers, those speakers "hesitated, faltered, or stopped, feeling interrupted and, more to the point, dominated" [V]. In her data the high-involvement speakers were of East European Jewish background from New York and the high-considerateness speakers were Christian Californians; she later stresses the style is "not so much Jewish as East European" [V] and not shared by all Jewish Americans. She cites Edelsky (1981): in faculty meetings men talked more in "singly developed" floors, women as much as men in "collaboratively developed" floors. Her political argument: if overlap equals dominance, then high-involvement groups (she names women of many ethnic backgrounds) are stigmatised as pushy, so the interpretation of interruption must look at intentions and effects in context, not counts.

**Evidence status.** Rich qualitative case analysis of a handful of speakers; influential and plausible, but not quantitatively tested in that form. Stivers et al. (2009) cut against strong versions of "some cultures overlap as a norm" (Italian overlap was ordinary, 17%), while leaving room for small, felt differences. Tannen's own account is best taught as a lens on misunderstanding, not as a measured cultural map.

**What a lesson may say.** Overlap can be cooperative (showing enthusiasm, finishing a sentence, a quick "yes!") or competitive (taking the floor, changing topic); people raised with different habits can misread each other, and one person's engaged overlap can feel like interruption to another. **May not say** that an ethnic group "interrupts" or that Tannen measured this in a population.

#### 3.2 Zimmerman and West 1975

**Citation.** Zimmerman, D. H., & West, C. (1975). Sex roles, interruptions and silences in conversation. In B. Thorne & N. Henley (Eds.), *Language and Sex: Difference and Dominance* (pp. 105–129). Newbury House. Read: full chapter (scanned PDF, Stanford site, OCR'd; figures checked against page images).

**Design.** 31 two-party conversational segments; three quarters recorded in coffee shops, drug stores and other public places in a university community, the rest in private homes. 10 male-male, 10 female-female and 11 male-female segments; participants white, apparently middle class, mostly university students. Some were recorded without prior consent (consent sought afterwards where possible). Definitions follow Sacks et al.: an overlap begins at or very close to a possible transition point (within the last word); an interruption penetrates the turn earlier and is treated as a violation.

**Findings.** Same-sex: 7 interruptions and 22 overlaps, roughly evenly split. Cross-sex: 48 interruptions and 9 overlaps; Table 2 gives males 96% (46) of interruptions and 100% (9) of overlaps. **The text on the facing page says "98% and 100%, respectively" [V, OCR, checked against page image], which does not match the table's 46 of 48 (95.8%).** One cross-sex segment (a female teaching assistant repeatedly interrupted by a male undergraduate) held 13 of the 48 interruptions (27%). They also report longer silences by women after being interrupted.

**Critiques.** Tannen (1989) and Murray (1985, cited there) argue no purely structural criterion can identify interruption, since completion rights depend on context; some of West and Zimmerman's own examples look justified ("Don't touch that"). Tannen also notes West and Zimmerman's follow-up (1983) with unacquainted undergraduates found 75% (21 of 28) of interruptions were men interrupting women, a smaller skew. Methodologically: tiny sample, one coder team, no inferential statistics, one highly influential segment, covert recording.

**What a lesson may say.** This was a small, pioneering study that launched a large literature; its striking percentages rest on 48 interruptions in 11 conversations. **Gate:** do not cite "96%" or "98%" as a general fact about men and women.

#### 3.3 Anderson and Leaper 1998 (meta-analysis)

**Citation.** Anderson, K. J., & Leaper, C. (1998). Meta-analyses of gender effects on conversational interruption: Who, what, when, where, and how. *Sex Roles*, 39(3–4), 225–252. DOI 10.1023/A:1018802521676. Read: full text (eScholarship, UC Santa Cruz).

**Design.** Meta-analyses of 43 published studies of adult women's and men's interruptions. Interruption definitions coded into three levels: (1) undefined or broad (may include backchannels, affiliative overlaps, unsuccessful interruptions); (2) excluding backchannels; (3) intrusive (aimed at taking the floor, for example "successful" interruptions). Moderators: author gender, year, gender composition, group size, familiarity, setting, activity, observation length.

**Findings.** Overall, men interrupted significantly more, but the effect was negligible: d = .15. For intrusive interruptions (17 studies), d = .33. Moderators for intrusive interruptions (weighted d): women first authors d = .54 versus men first authors d = −.21 (non-significant); strangers d = .38 versus familiar d = .19; naturalistic settings d = .76 (only 3 of the 17 studies) versus lab d = .31; unstructured activities d = .73 versus instrumental tasks d = .05; groups of three or more versus dyads d = .13 (groups reported as d = .31 in the results and d = .63 in the discussion, an internal inconsistency). Gender composition did not moderate: same-gender d = .24, mixed-gender d = .11. Samples were mostly middle-class European American; ethnicity and class could not be tested.

**What a lesson may say.** On average across studies, men interrupt slightly more than women; the difference is small overall and larger for intrusive interruptions, in groups, in unstructured talk and in natural settings, and it depends on how interruption is defined and even on who did the study. **May not say** "men interrupt women far more" as a settled general fact. Note the finding that the mixed-gender difference was negligible, which cuts against the specific cross-sex claim of Zimmerman and West.

#### 3.4 Related context (abstracts only)

- Leaper, C., & Ayres, M. M. (2007). A meta-analytic review of gender variations in adults' language use: Talkativeness, affiliative speech, and assertive speech. *Personality and Social Psychology Review*, 11(4), 328–363. DOI 10.1177/1088868307302221. Abstract only (Europe PMC): negligible average effects; men slightly more talkative (d = −.14 in their sign convention, meaning men higher), men slightly more assertive speech (d = .09), women slightly more affiliative speech (d = .12); some moderators produce d > .2 and some reverse direction.
- Mehl, M. R., Vazire, S., Ramírez-Esparza, N., Slatcher, R. B., & Pennebaker, J. W. (2007). Are women really more talkative than men? *Science*, 317(5834), 82. DOI 10.1126/science.1139940. Abstract only: 396 participants wore a sampling recorder; women and men both spoke about 16,000 words a day.

---

### 4. Questions

#### 4.1 Huang, Yeomans, Brooks, Minson and Gino 2017

**Citation.** Huang, K., Yeomans, M., Brooks, A. W., Minson, J., & Gino, F. (2017). It doesn't hurt to ask: Question-asking increases liking. *Journal of Personality and Social Psychology*, 113(3), 430–452. DOI 10.1037/pspi0000097. Correction: *JPSP* (2025), 128(3), 669, DOI 10.1037/pspi0000491. Read: full text (HBS PDF) and the correction notice (PubMed 40111841).

**Design and samples.**
- Study 1: 430 recruited, 398 analysed (199 dyads), 15-minute online text chats to "get to know each other". One partner per dyad told to ask "at least nine questions" or "at most four questions" (values from a pilot, N = 193).
- Study 2A: both partners assigned high or low question-asking (2 × 2); Study 2B: outside observers (at least three per conversation, 169 conversations) rated the transcripts.
- Question typology from hand coding (4,545 question turns): follow-up 40.51%, full-switch 27.55%, mirror 19.03%, introductory 5.52%, partial-switch 5.48%, rhetorical 1.91%.
- Study 3: an existing speed-dating dataset (110 people, three sessions, 4-minute dates; about 2,000 dates) run through an NLP "follow-up question detector".

**Findings.**
- Study 1: partners of high question-askers liked them more (5.79 vs 5.31 on 1 to 7; Cohen's d = .35). High askers were rated more responsive (d = .30), and responsiveness mediated the effect.
- Study 2A replicated (d = .27).
- Askers did not anticipate the effect: predicted liking did not differ by condition, and question-asking was uncorrelated with predicted liking (r = −.02).
- Study 2B: outside observers did **not** like high question-askers more; they liked those who were asked many questions more.
- Follow-up questions specifically drove perceived responsiveness; full-switch questions went with lower responsiveness.
- Study 3: average date had 4.51 follow-up questions per person; a higher follow-up rate predicted partners wanting a second date. The authors' illustration: an 8% difference in follow-up question rate goes with about one additional second-date "yes" over 20 dates. Correlational.
- The authors themselves flag a boundary: one can ask too many questions without reciprocating disclosure.

**Integrity note.** Francesca Gino is a co-author; several of her other papers were retracted in 2023 after data concerns. For this paper, the 2025 correction reports an independent audit: "The audit confirmed that all the conclusions in the paper are valid" [V, from PubMed record], with minor reporting errors corrected (materials at https://osf.io/rymv8/). The data for Study 3 came from an earlier, independent speed-dating study (Jurafsky, Ranganath & McFarland 2009).

**What a lesson may say.** In experiments with strangers, people who asked more questions, especially follow-ups on what the other just said, were liked more by their partners, and people did not expect this. The effect is modest (d around .3). Liking comes from the person being asked, not from onlookers. **May not say** that more questions are always better, or state the speed-dating result as causal.

#### 4.2 Brooks and John 2018 (practitioner summary)

**Citation.** Brooks, A. W., & John, L. K. (2018). The surprising power of questions. *Harvard Business Review*, 96(3), 60–67. Read: full text (reprint PDF hosted by CEBMA).

**Content.** Practitioner summary of Huang et al. and related work: favour follow-up questions; know when to keep questions open-ended; get the sequence right (in tense situations asking tough questions first can make people more willing to open up, citing John's work on decreasing intrusiveness); pay attention to tone and group dynamics (people follow each other's lead in disclosure). It also notes that "too many questions can also create a stilted dynamic" [V]. Cites Minson and colleagues: pessimistic-assumption questions ("This business will need some new equipment soon, correct?") reduce lying in negotiation. **Use:** accessible summary to link; its claims beyond Huang et al. should be traced to their own primary papers before a lesson states them.

#### 4.3 Yeomans, Schweitzer and Brooks 2022 and the practical guide

- Yeomans, M., Schweitzer, M. E., & Brooks, A. W. (2022). The conversational circumplex: Identifying, prioritizing, and pursuing informational and relational motives in conversation. *Current Opinion in Psychology*, 44, 293–302. DOI 10.1016/j.copsyc.2021.10.001. Read: full text (open access). A theoretical review: conversation goals vary on two dimensions, informational (giving or getting accurate information) and relational (building the relationship), and people often hold conflicting goals within themselves and across partners. Useful framing for "what is this conversation for?" No new data.
- Yeomans, M., Boland, F. K., Collins, H. K., Abi-Esber, N., & Brooks, A. W. (2023). A practical guide to conversation research: How to study what people say to each other. *Advances in Methods and Practices in Psychological Science*, 6(4). Read: abstract and introduction. Methods guide; confirms the field now studies conversation with recorded, transcribed, turn-level data at scale. Not a source of findings for a lesson.

#### 4.4 Conversational receptiveness: Yeomans et al. 2020

**Citation.** Yeomans, M., Minson, J., Collins, H., Chen, F., & Gino, F. (2020). Conversational receptiveness: Improving engagement with opposing views. *Organizational Behavior and Human Decision Processes*, 160, 131–148. DOI 10.1016/j.obhdp.2020.03.011. Read: full text (author PDF).

**Findings.** An interpretable machine-learning model identifies the language of "receptiveness" (willingness to engage thoughtfully with opposing views). Government executives rated as more receptive by the algorithm and by partners (not by themselves) were seen as better teammates and advisers. Wikipedia editors whose opening posts were more receptive were less likely to receive personal attacks later. In Study 4 a "receptiveness recipe" of four markers, "positive statements, rather than negations; explicit acknowledgement of understanding; finding points of agreement; and hedging to soften claims" [V], raised rated receptiveness (standardised β = 0.57), collaboration intentions (β = 0.28) and persuasiveness to opponents (β = 0.24). Writers reported the recipe felt difficult.

**Integrity note.** Gino is a co-author. I found no correction or audit notice for this paper in my search (unlike Huang et al.). The Wikipedia data were independent (Zhang et al. 2018). A drafter should check Retraction Watch and the journal page before relying on it.

**Use.** Belongs mainly to talking with people who differ in view (this course's last outcome) and to Conflict Resolution later in the term; the four-marker recipe is concrete and teachable, with the caveat that the studies are mostly written, not spoken, disagreement.

---

### 5. The liking gap

#### 5.1 Boothby, Cooney, Sandstrom and Clark 2018

**Citation.** Boothby, E. J., Cooney, G., Sandstrom, G. M., & Clark, M. S. (2018). The liking gap in conversations: Do people like us more than we think? *Psychological Science*, 29(11), 1742–1756. DOI 10.1177/0956797618783714. Read: full text (Yale lab PDF).

**Studies.**
- Study 1a: 36 community members (18 dyads), 5-minute conversation with ice-breaker questions; people rated how much they liked the partner and how much they thought the partner liked them. Liking gap found. Shyness moderated it: high-shyness participants' gap was 1.14 points (6.16 actual vs 5.02 perceived, 1 to 7), average-shyness 0.55, low-shyness 0.25 (not significant).
- Study 1b: coders watching the videos could detect liking signals, which participants apparently neglected.
- Study 2: 84 Yale students and recent graduates, free 5-minute conversation; rating type predicted liking, b = −0.57 (95% CI −0.71 to −0.44). Thoughts about one's own performance were more negative than about the partner's, and this mediated the gap.
- Study 3: 102 people (Harvard Decision Science Lab) talked as long as they liked; the gap held for short, medium and long conversations, for liking and enjoyment (enjoyment b = −0.52).
- Study 4: 118 people at "How to Talk to Strangers" workshops in the UK; partners were found more interesting (4.30) than people thought they themselves were found (3.61).
- Study 5: 102 first-year Yale suitemates surveyed five times (September 2016 to May 2017); people underestimated how much their suitemates liked them "at all time points except for the final one" [V]. The authors suggest the gap closed by May because people knew each other well or because decisions about rooming together forced liking into the open.

**What a lesson may say.** After a first conversation, most people underestimate how much the other person liked them and enjoyed it; the gap is larger for shy people and can last for months in new relationships. **Gate:** the popular "persisted over months" summary is right, but the gap had disappeared by the last wave in May; say "for several months".

#### 5.2 Replications and extensions

- **Mastroianni, A. M., Cooney, G., Boothby, E. J., & Reece, A. G. (2021). The liking gap in groups and teams. *Organizational Behavior and Human Decision Processes*, 162, 109–122. DOI 10.1016/j.obhdp.2020.10.013.** Read: abstract and introduction (PDF). The gap appears in small-group conversations (Study 1), persists in teams working on a project (Study 2), and in working adults is linked to how well people think teams can work together (Study 3); largest for peer relationships; partly driven by focus on the negative aspects of one's own impression. Same research group, so a conceptual extension rather than independent replication.
- **Wolf, W., Nafe, A., & Tomasello, M. (2021). The development of the liking gap: Children older than 5 years think that partners evaluate them less positively than they evaluate their partners. *Psychological Science*, 32(5), 789–798. DOI 10.1177/0956797620980754.** Abstract only (Europe PMC): N = 241 US children aged 4 to 11; the gap appears from age 5 and grows with age. An independent lab.
- **Sprecher, S. (2025). Liking gap(s) in getting-acquainted interactions. *Journal of Social Psychology*, 166(5), 650–655. DOI 10.1080/00224545.2025.2576765** (author and pages from Crossref). Abstract only: in a compiled sample of stranger pairs across several of Sprecher's studies, both the perceived and the actual liking gap were found and were robust across studies and conditions. An independent lab.
- I found no registered or many-labs replication of the liking gap.

---

### 6. Conversation endings: Mastroianni, Gilbert, Cooney and Wilson 2021

**Citation.** Mastroianni, A. M., Gilbert, D. T., Cooney, G., & Wilson, T. D. (2021). Do conversations end when people want them to? *PNAS*, 118(10), e2011809118. DOI 10.1073/pnas.2011809118. Read: full text (PMC7958231).

**Design.**
- Study 1: online survey (MTurk). 1,000 recruited, 806 analysed (mean age 36.8), each recalling their most recent in-person, one-to-one conversation of 1 to 45 minutes. Mostly intimates: friend 27.9%, spouse or partner 27.2%, other family 24.1%, stranger 2.1%; 56% talked with the person daily or almost daily.
- Study 2: lab. 366 Harvard pool participants paired as strangers, told to talk "for as little time or as much time as you like" between 1 and 45 minutes; 252 in 126 dyads analysed (57 of 183 dyads ran the full 45 minutes and were stopped, and were excluded from the key analyses).

**Findings.**
- Study 1: 66.51% said there was a point when they felt ready to end; of these, 90.57% kept feeling that way for the rest of the conversation. On average people wished the conversation had been 24.08% longer, but the absolute gap between desired and actual length averaged 56.01% of the conversation.
- Study 2: 68.65% felt ready to end at some point; absolute discrepancy 46.03% of conversation length. Both partners wanted to end at the same time and actually did so in only 1.59% of conversations; 29.37% ended when one partner wanted. In 46.83% both wanted to end sooner than they did; in 9.52% both wanted to go on longer.
