People were poor at guessing when the partner wanted to stop: the estimate was off by 63.84% of the conversation's duration on average.
- The authors frame ending as a "coordination problem" people cannot solve because they hide the needed information.

**Gate.** A figure of "15.6%" circulates in secondary coverage; I did not find it in the paper and it should not be used. Use the figures above.

**What a lesson may say.** Conversations rarely end when both people want; mismatches run to about half the conversation's length; many conversations go on longer than one or both would like, while some end too soon. People are bad at telling when the other wants to leave. This joins naturally with pre-closings (Schegloff and Sacks 1973): a lesson can teach honest, kind ways to signal readiness to end. **May not say** people "always" want out early; in Study 1 the average person wanted it longer, and about 1 in 10 Study 2 pairs both wanted more.

---

### 7. Depth, strangers and wellbeing

#### 7.1 Kardas, Kumar and Epley 2022: "Overly shallow?"

**Citation.** Kardas, M., Kumar, A., & Epley, N. (2022). Overly shallow?: Miscalibrated expectations create a barrier to deeper conversation. *JPSP*, 122(3), 367–398. DOI 10.1037/pspa0000281. Read: full text (author PDF via Epley's site).

**Design.** Twelve experiments (1a, 1b, 1c, 2, 3, 4a, 4b, 5, 6a, 6b, 7a, 7b) plus pilots. Samples include 25 pairs of financial-services executives at a conference (1a), 89 pairs of master's students (2), 103 pairs from university and community pools (3), 109 pairs at a master's orientation (4a), 204 pairs recruited in public parks, including a close-other condition (5), groups of four for within-person comparison (6a, 6b; N = 120 each), and MTurk samples (7a N = 93; 7b N = 130). Participants discussed shallow or deep question sets (in Experiment 3 they wrote their own), and predicted then reported awkwardness, connection, enjoyment and happiness.

**Findings.** Across experiments people overestimated how awkward conversations with strangers would be and underestimated connection and happiness, and the miscalibration was larger for deep conversations (Experiment 2: deep condition overestimation d = 2.23 vs shallow d = 0.55). The mechanism tested: people expected others to care less about their disclosures than they did. With close others predictions were more accurate. After having both a shallow and a deep conversation (6a, 6b), people felt more connected to the deep partner, and preferences shifted toward deep talk (d = .60). Deep conversations felt only marginally more awkward in experience. Changing expectations about others' interest increased choice of deeper topics (7a, 7b).

**Limits the authors state.** All conversations were in experiments where both people knew they were to talk; only single, short-term conversations; US samples over a narrow time period. Prior literature on intimacy and connection is mixed (they cite null or mixed results from Kashdan and others).

**What a lesson may say.** People in these US experiments expected deep conversations with strangers to be more awkward and less rewarding than they turned out to be; the reason seems to be underestimating how interested others are. **May not say** that small talk is worthless or that depth is always right (see section 7.4 and (c)).

#### 7.2 Talking to strangers: Epley and Schroeder 2014; Schroeder, Lyons and Epley 2022; Sandstrom and Dunn 2014

**Epley, N., & Schroeder, J. (2014). Mistakenly seeking solitude. *Journal of Experimental Psychology: General*, 143(5), 1980–1999. DOI 10.1037/a0037323.** Read: full text. Nine experiments on Chicago-area trains and buses, in taxis, and in a lab waiting room. Commuters told to talk to a stranger reported a more positive and no less productive commute than those told to sit in solitude (Experiment 1a, final N = 97 train commuters); separate commuters predicted the opposite. The mistaken preference "stems partly from underestimating others' interest in connecting" [V]. Cab riders who routinely talked to drivers had calibrated predictions. In a waiting room, those who were talked to were as positive as those told to talk.

**Schroeder, J., Lyons, D., & Epley, N. (2022). Hello, stranger? Pleasant conversations are preceded by concerns about starting one. *Journal of Experimental Psychology: General*, 151(5), 1141–1153. DOI 10.1037/xge0001118.** Read: full text. A conceptual replication with London-area rail commuters: 466 recruited at four stations, 383 completed (82% response). Those assigned to talk reported a significantly more positive commute and learning more than solitude or control; 99 of 127 in the talk condition reported actually doing so. **Unlike 2014, these commuters expected talking to be more positive.** Experiment 2 (148 commuters) located the barrier in starting: people predicted that trying to start a conversation would be less pleasant than having one, because they expected others to be uninterested.

**Sandstrom, G. M., & Dunn, E. W. (2014). Is efficiency overrated? Minimal social interactions lead to belonging and positive affect. *Social Psychological and Personality Science*, 5(4), 437–442. DOI 10.1177/1948550613502990.** Abstract only (Essex repository record). People told to have a genuine interaction with a barista (smile, eye contact, brief conversation) had more positive affect than those told to be efficient, with initial evidence of mediation by belonging. Sample size not seen.

**What a lesson may say.** Several experiments, in the US and UK, found that brief conversations with strangers were more pleasant than people expected and did not cost productivity; the main barrier seems to be worry about starting and about the other's interest. Keep "several experiments by one research group plus a small barista study" in view; these are not population-wide effects.

#### 7.3 Related miscalibration findings (Epley lab and others)

- **Kardas, M., Schroeder, J., & O'Brien, E. (2022). Keep talking: (Mis)understanding the hedonic trajectory of conversation. *JPSP*, 123(4), 717–740. DOI 10.1037/pspi0000379.** Read: abstract and introduction. Five preregistered experiments, N = 1,093 (966 spoken conversations): people expected enjoyment to decline as a conversation with a new acquaintance went on, but it stayed stable or rose; they underestimated how much they would have to talk about.
- **Atir, S., Wald, K. A., & Epley, N. (2022). Talking with strangers is surprisingly informative. *PNAS*, 119(34), e2206992119.** Read: abstract (author manuscript). Seven experiments: people expected to learn less from a conversation than they reported learning.
- **Kumar, A., & Epley, N. (2018). Undervaluing gratitude: Expressers misunderstand the consequences of showing appreciation. *Psychological Science*, 29(9), 1423–1435. DOI 10.1177/0956797618772506.** Read: abstract and methods. Three experiments (Experiment 1: 107 MBA students writing gratitude letters; Experiment 2 a direct replication; preregistered): writers underestimated recipients' surprise and positive feelings and overestimated awkwardness.
- **Zhao, X., & Epley, N. (2021). Insufficiently complimentary?: Underestimating the positive impact of compliments creates a barrier to expressing them. *JPSP*, 121(2), 239–256. DOI 10.1037/pspa0000277.** Read: abstract and parts of methods. Nine experiments: expressers underestimated how positive and overestimated how awkward recipients would feel.
- **Liu, P. J., Rim, S., Min, L., & Min, K. E. (2023). The surprise of reaching out: Appreciated more than we think. *JPSP*, 124(4), 754–771. DOI 10.1037/pspi0000402.** Not read directly (APA PDF blocked); read an independent replication report: Transparent Replications (Clearer Thinking), Report #7 (2023), which replicated Study 4a (initiators underestimate how much a weak-tie acquaintance would appreciate being contacted) and rated the original five stars for transparency. The paper is described in press coverage as having more than 5,900 participants (search snippet only).
- **Epley, N., Kardas, M., Zhao, X., Atir, S., & Schroeder, J. (2022). Undersociality: Miscalibrated social cognition can inhibit social connection. *Trends in Cognitive Sciences*, 26(5), 406–418. DOI 10.1016/j.tics.2022.02.007.** Read: first pages. The lab's own synthesis of this programme.

**Status.** A coherent, mostly preregistered programme, largely from one network of labs (Epley and collaborators), with at least one independent direct replication (Liu et al. Study 4a) and conceptual replications across countries (London). The general pattern, "people underestimate how positively others will respond to social approaches", is well supported within these designs; generalisation beyond WEIRD samples is not established.

#### 7.4 Substantive conversation and wellbeing: Mehl et al. 2010 and Milek et al. 2018

**Mehl, M. R., Vazire, S., Holleran, S. E., & Clark, C. S. (2010). Eavesdropping on happiness: Well-being is related to having less small talk and more substantive conversations. *Psychological Science*, 21(4), 539–541. DOI 10.1177/0956797610362675.** Read: full text (Vazire site PDF). 79 undergraduates wore the Electronically Activated Recorder (EAR) for 4 days, recording 30 s every 12.5 min (23,689 waking recordings). Small talk was 17.9% and substantive conversation 35.5% of conversations. Well-being correlated with less time alone (r = −.35), more time talking (r = .31), less small talk (r = −.33) and more substantive conversation (r = .28). The happiest versus unhappiest had roughly one third as much small talk (10.2% vs 28.3%) and twice as many substantive conversations (45.9% vs 21.8%). Correlational; the authors raise both directions of causation.

**Milek, A., Butler, E. A., Tackman, A. M., Kaplan, D. M., Raison, C. L., Sbarra, D. A., Vazire, S., & Mehl, M. R. (2018). "Eavesdropping on happiness" revisited: A pooled, multisample replication of the association between life satisfaction and observed daily conversation quantity and quality. *Psychological Science*, 29(9), 1451–1462. DOI 10.1177/0956797618774252.** Read: full text (PMC6139582). Pooled four samples (the original plus three adult samples), N = 486. **What held:** life satisfaction went with less time alone (r = −.19), more time talking (r = .22) and more substantive conversation (r = .23; .15 as a share of conversations). **What shrank or failed:** small talk was unrelated to life satisfaction (pooled r = .05, 95% CI −.04 to .14; normalised −.08). The abstract states: "The failure to replicate the original small-talk effect is theoretically and practically important" [V]. Personality (including extraversion) did not substantially moderate.

**What a lesson may say.** People who have more substantive conversations tend to be more satisfied with life (correlation around .2); having more small talk is not linked to lower satisfaction. **Must not** say "small talk makes you unhappy" (that was the part that did not replicate), and must not present any of this as causal.

#### 7.5 The 36 questions: Aron et al. 1997

**Citation.** Aron, A., Melinat, E., Aron, E. N., Vallone, R. D., & Bator, R. J. (1997). The experimental generation of interpersonal closeness: A procedure and some preliminary findings. *Personality and Social Psychology Bulletin*, 23(4), 363–377. DOI 10.1177/0146167297234003. Read: **abstract only** (the free copies I found were landing pages; Sage paywall). The Greater Good in Action page reproduces the 36 questions.

**From the abstract.** Pairs spend 45 minutes on self-disclosure and relationship-building tasks that "gradually escalate in intensity"; Study 1 found greater post-interaction closeness than comparable small-talk tasks; Studies 2 and 3 found no closeness effects of matching on attitudes, expecting mutual liking, or making getting close an explicit goal.

**Replication.** Sprecher (2021, *Journal of Social and Personal Relationships*, DOI 10.1177/0265407521996055) compared the Fast Friends procedure with small talk and unstructured self-disclosure; search snippets indicate stronger affiliative outcomes for Fast Friends. **Snippet only; not read.** Kardas et al. (2022) cite mixed results in the wider literature on intimacy and connectedness.

**What a lesson may say.** Structured, gradually deepening mutual disclosure produced more closeness than small talk in a lab. **May not say** it makes people fall in love (a popular framing from a 2015 newspaper essay, not the study's finding).

---

### 8. Talking about ourselves and about others

#### 8.1 Tamir and Mitchell 2012, and the source of "30 to 40%"

**Citation.** Tamir, D. I., & Mitchell, J. P. (2012). Disclosing information about the self is intrinsically rewarding. *PNAS*, 109(21), 8038–8043. DOI 10.1073/pnas.1202129109. Read: full text (PMC3361411).

**Design and findings.** Five studies. Studies 1a (n = 78) and 1b (n = 117): fMRI; disclosing one's own opinions or traits activated the mesolimbic dopamine system (nucleus accumbens, VTA) more than judging others. Study 2 (n = 37): on 195 trials people chose between answering about self, other or facts, each tied to $0.01 to $0.04; they gave up money to talk about themselves, an average loss of 17% of potential earnings. Studies 3 (n = 17, fMRI) and 4 (n = 41): self-disclosure was more rewarding when shared with another person than when private.

**The 30 to 40% figure.** The abstract opens: "Humans devote 30–40% of speech output solely to informing others of their own subjective experiences" [V]. The body attributes it to "Studies of human conversation" citing refs 1 to 4: Dunbar, Marriott and Duncan 1997; Emler 1990; Emler 1994; Landis and Burtt 1924. Tamir and Mitchell did not measure it. I could not read Dunbar 1997 in full (paywalled; ProQuest and Scribd copies not retrievable), so I cannot confirm which of the four sources contains a 30 to 40% figure. **Gate:** a lesson must not attribute "30 to 40%" to Tamir and Mitchell's data; if used, it must be traced to Dunbar 1997 or Emler.

**What a lesson may say.** People find talking about themselves rewarding (brain reward regions, willingness to give up small sums), which helps explain why good listening takes effort. The fMRI samples are small (17 to 117), and "rewarding" here means activation plus choice behaviour, not a claim that people are selfish.

#### 8.2 What people talk about: Dunbar et al. 1997 and its 2025 revisit

**Dunbar, R. I. M., Marriott, A., & Duncan, N. D. C. (1997). Human conversational behavior. *Human Nature*, 8(3), 231–246. DOI 10.1007/BF02912493.** **Not read** (paywalled). Described in Szala et al. 2025 as 45 casual conversations in public settings, over three quarters by students in two university cafeterias (Liverpool and London), topic scan-sampled every 30 seconds by eavesdropping coders, with social and personal topics about two thirds of conversation time.

**Szala, A., Wacewicz, S., Placiński, M., et al. (2025). How much conversation content is actually social: Human conversational behaviour revisited. *Language and Cognition*, 17. Published online 9 January 2025.** Read: full text (open access, Cambridge). Criticises the original's sample (45 conversations, young urban academics, public places), single-coder classification and lack of an explicit definition of "social". Using the Spokes corpus of Polish informal conversation (535 conversations, 274,954 lines after exclusions), they classify about 85% of content as social. They also cite Dahmardeh and Dunbar 2017: 76% social in Iranian conversations.

**What a lesson may say.** Most everyday conversation is about people: ourselves, others and relationships (estimates from about two thirds to 85%, depending on sample and definition of "social"). **May not say** "two thirds of conversation is gossip" in the pejorative sense; "gossip" in Dunbar's usage means social and personal topics broadly.

---

### (a) What this part says the field considers essential

1. **Conversation is an orderly, locally managed system.** Turn-taking (TCUs, transition-relevance places, current-selects-next, self-selection), adjacency pairs, preference and repair are the core concepts of conversation analysis and the foundation for everything quantitative that followed (Sacks et al. 1974; Schegloff and Sacks 1973; Schegloff et al. 1977; Pomerantz 1984).
2. **Timing is tight and meaningful.** Typical gaps are around 200 ms; producing even a word takes 600 ms or more, so listeners plan while listening. Overlap is common but brief. Delays carry meaning: slower replies tend to go with "no", non-answers and reluctance, and listeners read them that way (Stivers et al. 2009; Levinson 2016; Kendrick and Torreira 2015; Roberts and Francis 2013).
3. **The system is broadly universal with small, felt local differences** (Stivers et al. 2009).
4. **Responsiveness is the active ingredient in being liked**: follow-up questions that show you heard, understood and care (Huang et al. 2017; the responsiveness construct of Reis and colleagues, cited there).
5. **People are systematically miscalibrated about conversation**: they underestimate how much partners like them (liking gap), how much strangers are interested in them and in deeper talk, how enjoyable and informative conversations will be, and how welcome compliments, thanks and outreach are; they cannot tell when the other wants to end (Boothby et al. 2018; Kardas et al. 2022; Epley and Schroeder 2014; Schroeder et al. 2022; Mastroianni et al. 2021; Liu et al. 2023).
6. **Substantive conversation goes with wellbeing; small talk is not harmful** (Milek et al. 2018 correcting Mehl et al. 2010).
7. **Methods matter.** Conversation research has moved from small hand-analysed samples to recorded, turn-level data at scale, preregistration and replication (Yeomans et al. 2023; Milek et al. 2018; the 2025 audit of Huang et al.).

### (b) Common misconceptions (each named by a source)

1. **"Small talk makes you unhappy; only deep talk counts."** Named by Milek et al. 2018: the small-talk association in Mehl et al. 2010 "did not replicate" and the authors say the original "has garnered considerable scientific and lay interest". Substantive conversation still correlates with life satisfaction.
2. **"Men interrupt women far more than women interrupt men."** Anderson and Leaper 1998: overall effect negligible (d = .15), larger only for intrusive interruptions and in certain settings; mixed-gender difference negligible (d = .11). Tannen 1989 criticises the counting of overlaps as dominance.
3. **"Overlap means interruption, and interruption is always rude."** Tannen 1989 (cooperative overlapping); Schegloff 2000 (continuers, terminal overlaps and choral talk are unproblematic); Levinson and Torreira 2015 (73% of overlaps in Switchboard involved a backchannel).
4. **"Some cultures (for example Japanese) leave long silences; others (for example Italian) talk over each other as a norm."** Stivers et al. 2009 tested the ethnographic reports: Japanese were fastest (+7 ms mean), Italians ordinary (+310 ms, 17% overlap); differences were within about 250 ms of the mean.
5. **"Women talk far more than men"** (the "20,000 versus 7,000 words" claim). Mehl et al. 2007 (abstract): both about 16,000 words a day; Leaper and Ayres 2007: men slightly more talkative on average, negligible.
6. **"If you want to be liked, talk about interesting things about yourself."** Huang et al. 2017: askers, especially of follow-up questions, are liked more, and people fail to predict this; Tamir and Mitchell 2012 show the pull to talk about oneself.
7. **"They didn't like me much."** Boothby et al. 2018 (liking gap): people typically underestimate how much a new partner liked them.
8. **"Strangers don't want to be talked to" / "deep questions will be awkward."** Epley and Schroeder 2014; Schroeder et al. 2022; Kardas et al. 2022.
9. **"The other person will tell you when they want to go."** Mastroianni et al. 2021: people barely know when the partner wants to end.
10. **"Tamir and Mitchell found people spend 30 to 40% of speech on themselves."** Their own paper attributes the figure to prior conversation studies (refs 1 to 4), not their data (see gate D5).

### (c) Contested questions

**C1. Is small talk worthwhile, or should you go deep?**
- *Depth position* (Kardas, Kumar and Epley 2022; Mehl et al. 2010): people are "overly shallow" because they wrongly expect deep talk to be awkward and unwelcome; deep conversations produce more connection, and substantive conversation goes with happiness. Best case: twelve experiments, several with real strangers in the field, consistent miscalibration, within-person comparisons favouring depth (6a, 6b).
- *Small-talk-has-value position* (Milek et al. 2018; Sandstrom and Dunn 2014; Schegloff and Sacks 1973 on openings and closings as necessary work): small talk is not associated with lower wellbeing; brief social exchanges with weak ties raise positive affect and belonging; small talk is how conversations are opened, trust is tested and depth is reached. Best case: the pooled N = 486 naturalistic data and the null small-talk association; Aron et al.'s procedure itself escalates gradually rather than starting deep.
- *What would settle it:* naturalistic experience-sampling or EAR studies with within-person variation (days with more versus less substantive talk), field experiments assigning depth over weeks, and cross-cultural samples (all current experimental evidence is US or UK).
- *Classification:* **contested empirical** on effects; partly **value** (what one wants from a conversation, and norms about privacy that differ by culture and person). The defensible teaching position: small talk and depth are not rivals; people tend to stop short of the depth they would enjoy.

**C2. Is interruption rude, or culturally variable?**
- *Dominance view* (Zimmerman and West 1975; West and Zimmerman 1983): interruptions violate the speaker's turn and are "a device for exercising power and control in conversation" (as quoted by Tannen 1989 [V]).
- *Style view* (Tannen 1984, 1989; Edelsky 1981): much overlap is cooperative; what counts as interruption depends on the participants' styles and intentions; judging by counts stigmatises high-involvement speakers.
- *CA and timing view* (Schegloff 2000; Levinson and Torreira 2015; Stivers et al. 2009): overlap is frequent, mostly brief and orderly; all languages sampled avoid overlap and minimise gaps; the differences are small but noticed. Intrusive interruption (taking the floor mid-unit, changing topic) and supportive overlap are different actions.
- *What would settle it:* studies that code intrusive versus cooperative overlap and measure participants' own experience of being interrupted, across communities.
- *Classification:* **contested empirical** on how much overlap norms vary (evidence favours small quantitative variation); **value** on how much to accommodate other styles. A lesson can present "interruption" as an action judged by the participants, with both over-sensitivity and floor-grabbing as real ways conversations go wrong.

**C3. Do men interrupt more than women?**
- *Yes, meaningfully* (Zimmerman and West 1975; the dominance tradition): large asymmetry in cross-sex talk.
- *Slightly, and it depends* (Anderson and Leaper 1998): d = .15 overall, .33 for intrusive interruptions, larger in groups, among strangers, in naturalistic and unstructured settings, and in studies with women first authors; negligible in mixed-gender pairs.
- *The question is badly posed* (Tannen 1989; Murray 1985 as cited): counting simultaneous speech does not measure dominance.
- *What would settle it:* large, preregistered, naturalistic studies with agreed definitions of intrusive interruption, diverse samples, and blind coding. (Post-1998 studies exist, for example on court and meeting settings, but I did not review them.)
- *Classification:* **contested empirical**. The accurate summary for a lesson: a small average difference for floor-taking interruptions that varies strongly with context.

**C4. How universal are turn-taking norms?**
- *Universal system* (Sacks et al. 1974 as a hypothesis; Stivers et al. 2009; Levinson 2016): same underlying system across 10 diverse languages; same factors predict delay; developmental and primate evidence suggest a biological base.
- *Cultural variability* (the ethnographic reports Stivers et al. tested, for example on Nordic silences, Antiguan overlap; Tannen on styles): communities differ in pacing and overlap norms in ways that matter for understanding.
- *Reconciliation offered by Stivers et al.:* a universal system with local calibration; differences of up to a few hundred ms are real and noticed by insiders and outsiders.
- *What would settle it:* more languages, more turn types than polar questions, multi-party talk, and institutional settings.
- *Classification:* largely **established** that there is a shared system with small quantitative variation; **contested empirical** on how far the variation extends beyond question-answer pairs and what it means for cross-cultural misunderstanding.

### (d) Gates: claims a drafter must re-check against a primary text before a lesson states them

- **D1.** Zimmerman and West 1975's cross-sex percentage: the table says 96% (46 of 48) of interruptions by men; the text says 98%. If cited at all, cite the table and the tiny sample.
- **D2.** Anderson and Leaper 1998 group-size effect: d = .31 (results) versus d = .63 (discussion) for groups. Do not quote either without the page and the caveat.
- **D3.** Mastroianni et al. 2021: use 1.59% (both wanted to end and did), 29.37% (one did), discrepancy about half the conversation (46 to 56%). Do not use "15.6%", which I did not find in the paper.
- **D4.** Boothby et al. 2018: the gap persisted "for several months" but was gone at the final (May) wave.
- **D5.** "30 to 40% of speech is about oneself": trace to Dunbar et al. 1997 or Emler, not Tamir and Mitchell's data. I could not verify the primary source.
- **D6.** "Two thirds of conversation is social": from Dunbar et al. 1997 (not read directly); Szala et al. 2025 found about 85% in Polish data with a different definition. State as a range with its sources.
- **D7.** The 200 ms figure is a modal gap; Stivers reports an overall mode of 0 ms, median +100 ms and mean +208 ms. Levinson says "modal gap ... around 200 ms". Pick the statistic and cite it precisely.
- **D8.** "Overlap is rare" is false as a share of transitions (30 to 40%) and true as a share of speaking time (under 5%). State which.
- **D9.** Templeton et al. 2022: the fourth author is Cone LeBeaumont (paper and Crossref), not "Cone LeBeaux". The "<250 ms is beyond conscious control" claim is the authors' interpretation, not something they tested; present it as their argument.
- **D10.** Huang et al. 2017 numbers were corrected in 2025 after an audit (conclusions unchanged). Take any specific statistic from the corrected results at https://osf.io/rymv8/, not from the 2017 PDF.
- **D11.** Yeomans et al. 2020 (Gino co-author): check for any correction or retraction notice before relying on it.
- **D12.** Aron et al. 1997: read beyond the abstract before stating effect sizes; do not repeat the "fall in love" framing.
- **D13.** Roberts, Francis and Morgan 2006 and Roberts et al. 2011: I read only descriptions; check the 600 ms and cross-language claims in the originals.
- **D14.** Tannen 1984 (*Conversational Style*): I read only her 1989 summary; check the book before describing its data in detail (six speakers, dinner, 1978).
- **D15.** Sandstrom and Dunn 2014: abstract only; check N and effect size before citing numbers.

### (e) Best free resources to link (checked live 2026-09-26)

- https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC2705608/ (200): Stivers et al. 2009, full text; the cross-language timing study, readable with good figures.
- https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC4464110/ (200): Levinson and Torreira 2015, full text; the clearest numbers on gaps and overlaps.
- https://www.conversationanalysis.org/schegloff-publication-archive/ (200): ISCA's archive with free PDFs of Sacks et al. 1974, Schegloff and Sacks 1973, Schegloff et al. 1977 and Schegloff 2000.
- https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC7958231/ (200): Mastroianni et al. 2021, full text on conversation endings.
- https://www.adammastroianni.com/research/2021/1/31/do-conversations-end-when-people-want-them-to (200): the lead author's plain-language page on the same study.
- https://clarkrelationshiplab.yale.edu/sites/default/files/files/BoothbyCooneySandstromClark2018.pdf (200): the liking gap paper, free PDF.
- https://www.hbs.edu/ris/Publication%20Files/Huang%20et%20al%202017_6945bc5e-3b3e-4c0a-addd-254c9e603c60.pdf (200): Huang et al. 2017 full text (note the 2025 correction).
- https://hbr.org/2018/05/the-surprising-power-of-questions (200): Brooks and John's practitioner summary (HBR may meter access).
- https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC6139582/ (200): Milek et al. 2018, the replication that overturned the small-talk finding; a model of honest reporting.
- https://www.nicholasepley.com/publications (200): free PDFs of the Epley lab papers (strangers, depth, compliments, gratitude).
- https://ggia.berkeley.edu/practice/36_questions_for_increasing_closeness (200): Greater Good's page with the 36 questions and how to use them.
- https://replications.clearerthinking.org/replication-2023jpsp124-4/ (200): an independent replication of Liu et al. 2023, useful to show students what replication looks like.
- https://www.mikeyeomans.info/papers/receptiveness.pdf (200): conversational receptiveness paper, with the four-marker recipe.
- https://www.cambridge.org/core/journals/language-and-cognition/article/how-much-conversation-content-is-actually-social-human-conversational-behaviour-revisited/61BF5C91583F04053BE9471ED96FAE1D (200): open-access 2025 revisit of "what people talk about".
- https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC3361411/ (200): Tamir and Mitchell 2012, full text.
- Levinson 2016 author PDF: https://pure.mpg.de/rest/items/item_2193297_10/component/file_2240007/content (downloaded 200 today; a later status check of the PuRe view page returned 403 to curl, probably bot filtering; re-check in a browser before linking).

### (f) Not read

- Tannen, *Conversational Style* (1984/2005) and *That's Not What I Meant!* (1986): not read; described only via Tannen 1989 and search results.
- West and Zimmerman 1983 and Murray 1985: known only as cited in Tannen 1989.
- Roberts, Francis and Morgan 2006 and Roberts, Margutti and Takano 2011: known only via Kendrick and Torreira 2015 and Roberts and Francis 2013.
- Heldner and Edlund 2010: known only via Levinson and Torreira 2015.
- Dunbar, Marriott and Duncan 1997: not read (paywall); described via Szala et al. 2025 and Tamir and Mitchell 2012.
- Emler 1990, 1994; Landis and Burtt 1924: not read.
- Aron et al. 1997: abstract only. Sprecher 2021: search snippet only.
- Sandstrom and Dunn 2014 (SPPS, barista): abstract only. Sandstrom and Dunn 2014 (PSPB, weak ties): not read.
- Wolf, Nafe and Tomasello 2021: abstract only. Sprecher 2025, "Liking gap(s) in getting-acquainted interactions": abstract only.
- Liu et al. 2023: not read directly (APA PDF blocked); read the independent replication report.
- Mehl et al. 2007 (Science, talkativeness) and Leaper and Ayres 2007: abstracts only.
- Edelsky 1981: known only via Tannen 1989.
- Dahmardeh and Dunbar 2017: known only via Szala et al. 2025.

UNREAD: Conversational Style 1984, That's Not What I Meant 1986, West and Zimmerman 1983, Murray 1985, Roberts Francis Morgan 2006, Roberts Margutti Takano 2011, Heldner and Edlund 2010, Dunbar Marriott Duncan 1997, Emler 1990, Emler 1994, Landis and Burtt 1924, Aron et al 1997 full text, Sprecher 2021, Sandstrom and Dunn 2014 barista, Sandstrom and Dunn 2014 weak ties, Wolf Nafe Tomasello 2021, Sprecher 2025 liking gaps, Liu et al 2023, Mehl et al 2007, Leaper and Ayres 2007, Edelsky 1981, Dahmardeh and Dunbar 2017
