## Part C: Listening research, active listening and its critics, supportive responses, and empathy

Researcher C, read 2026-09-26. Method: primary texts fetched with curl (generic research User-Agent) and extracted with pdftotext; one scanned paper (Gottman et al. 1998) was OCR'd with tesseract, so its quotations are marked [V-OCR] and should be spot-checked against the page image before publication. Abstracts not otherwise obtainable were pulled from the OpenAlex and Europe PMC APIs (the abstract text is the publisher's, reconstructed by me from the API; marked [V-abstract]). Raw extracts are saved under `cl/C/` (PDFs, .txt extractions, and `cl/C/bodie/` for Graham Bodie's self-archived papers). Taylor and Francis, Wiley, SAGE and ResearchGate returned Cloudflare challenges or 403s for every full-text request, so several papers named in the brief are abstract only; each entry says so. Tags: [V] verbatim from my own extraction (URL given); [V-OCR] verbatim from OCR; [V-abstract] verbatim abstract from API; [T] came through WebFetch or a search summary and is not guaranteed verbatim. No quotation here comes from a search snippet.

Coverage: the Rogers and Farson origin of "active listening" and Rogers' 1957 conditions; the Weger experiments on paraphrasing; the Gottman 1998 challenge to active listening in marital conflict and the Stanley, Bradbury and Markman reply, plus Gottman's later clinical protocol; Itzchakov and Kluger's high-quality listening programme and the 2024 meta-analysis; Bodie's measurement work and the 2015 helping-conversation experiment; a 2026 open-access review of contradictions in listening research (Itzchakov and Bodie); person-centred supportive messages, advice response theory, sequencing of advice, invisible support; Gordon's roadblocks; empathy training meta-analyses, the Bloom and Zaki debate, perspective taking versus perspective getting, empathic accuracy, the illusion of transparency; motivational interviewing's reflective listening and its evidence base.

---

### 1. Origins: Rogers and Farson (1957), and Rogers' conditions (1957)

#### 1.1 Rogers, C. R., and Farson, R. E. (1957). *Active Listening*. Chicago: Industrial Relations Center, University of Chicago (25 pp.). Reprinted as an excerpt in R. G. Newman, M. A. Danziger and M. Cohen (eds), *Communicating in Business Today*, D. C. Heath, 1987.

- **What I read:** two full reprints of the excerpt: (a) https://wholebeinginstitute.com/wp-content/uploads/Rogers_Farson_Active-Listening.pdf (5 pp., header "Excerpt from Communicating in Business Today ... 1987"); (b) the Gordon Training International 2007 reprint at http://www.ask-force.org/web/Discourse/Rogers-Farson-ActiveListening-1957.pdf (8 pp.). Both are the 1987 excerpt, not the full 25-page 1957 booklet. The 1957 original I did not see.
- **Design:** an essay for managers, not a study. It cites "clinical and research evidence" without references.
- **What it says (paraphrase with short quotes):**
  - Active listening is framed as a workplace practice for "the usual day-to-day events of any job", grounded in attitudes, not a technique. If the attitudes are absent the behaviour "will be empty and sterile" [V, (a)].
  - What to avoid: trying to change the other person's way of seeing things; passing judgment. "Passing judgment, whether critical or favorable, makes free expression difficult." [V, (a)] It groups advice and information with this: "advice is seldom taken, and information hardly ever utilized." [V, (a)] It also warns that praise and reassurance ("I'm sure everything will work out O.K.") can block.
  - What to do, three headings: **Listen for Total Meaning**: "Any message a person tries to get across usually has two components: the content of the message and the feeling or attitude underlying this content." [V, (a)] Example: a machinist saying he is "finally finished with that damned lathe setup" versus simply "finished". **Respond to Feelings**: sometimes the content matters less than the feeling underneath. **Note All Cues**: hesitations, inflection, facial expression, posture, breathing.
  - A test of understanding: "reflecting in your own words what the speaker seems to mean", with the rule of thumb that you do not understand until you can communicate the understanding "to the others satisfaction" [V, (b), sic]. It proposes the discussion ground rule that each person must restate the other's point to the other's satisfaction before making their own. (This rule also appears in Rogers' 1952 "Communication: its blocking and its facilitation"; I did not read that paper, see UNREAD.)
  - Warns against listening that only waits: if one's attitude is to "wait out" the speaker rather than really listen, it will fail [V, (a) short phrase].
  - Also notes that sincere interest is required and that listening "carries a strong element of personal risk" because the listener may be changed.
- **May say:** Rogers and Farson coined the phrase "active listening" in a 1957 booklet for managers; they defined it as listening for content plus feeling ("total meaning"), responding to feelings, noticing non-verbal cues, and checking understanding by restating; they cautioned against judgment, advice, and reassurance; they insisted it depends on genuine respect rather than technique.
- **May not say:** that the essay presents evidence (it asserts outcomes such as people becoming "more democratic, and less authoritarian" without data). Do not attribute a specific list of "active listening skills" (e.g., "nodding, eye contact, paraphrase") to them as a checklist; they explicitly rejected technique without attitude.

#### 1.2 Rogers, C. R. (1957). The necessary and sufficient conditions of therapeutic personality change. *Journal of Consulting Psychology*, 21(2), 95–103. (Reprinted 1992, *Journal of Consulting and Clinical Psychology*, 60(6), 827–832.)

- **What I read:** full text of the reprint at https://counselling-tutor.s3.eu-west-2.amazonaws.com/CSR+Documents/The+Necessary+and+Sufficient+Conditions+of+Therapeutic+Personality+Change.pdf (9 pp., with editor's note naming the 1957 citation).
- **Design:** theoretical paper stating testable hypotheses.
- **Findings / claims:** six conditions: psychological contact; client incongruence; therapist congruence; unconditional positive regard; empathic understanding of the client's internal frame of reference, communicated; the client perceives this "to a minimal degree". "No other conditions are necessary." [V]
  - Definition of empathy: "To sense the client's private world as if it were your own, but without ever losing the “as if” quality—this is empathy" [V].
  - Crucially for this course, Rogers says that the technique of "reflecting feelings" "is by no means an essential condition of therapy" [V]; it matters only as a channel for empathy and regard, and "Feeling may be “reflected” in a way which communicates the therapist's lack of empathy." [V]
  - He presents the statement "not ... with any assurance as to its correctness" but as hypotheses "open to proof or disproof" [V], and says the hypotheses "hold in any situation whether it is or is not labeled “psychotherapy.”" [V]
- **Replication status (from my other reading, not re-checked here):** the "necessary and sufficient" claim is generally regarded as not supported in its strong form; empathy, positive regard and congruence correlate with therapy outcome in later meta-analyses (e.g., Elliott et al. 2018 on empathy), but I did not read those. See UNREAD.
- **May say:** Rogers himself separated the attitude (empathy, regard) from the technique (reflecting feelings) and warned that mechanical reflection can communicate the opposite of empathy. This is the historical root of the modern critique that "active listening" became a checklist.
- **May not say:** that Rogers claimed the conditions were proven; that Rogers "invented paraphrasing".

---

### 2. Does paraphrasing help? The Weger experiments and Bodie et al. (2015)

#### 2.1 Weger, H., Jr., Castle, G. R., and Emmett, M. C. (2010). Active listening in peer interviews: The influence of message paraphrasing on perceptions of listening skill. *International Journal of Listening*, 24(1), 34–49. DOI 10.1080/10904010903466311

- **What I read:** abstract only [V-abstract, OpenAlex]. Unpaywall lists a free "bronze" copy at Taylor and Francis, but curl and WebFetch both received a Cloudflare challenge or 403; ResearchGate copy blocked likewise.
- **Design / sample:** experiment; 180 undergraduates in peer interviews about comprehensive examinations; interviewer responded to the participant's opinions with either a paraphrased reflection or a simple acknowledgement.
- **Findings:** paraphrases "were associated with the social attractiveness of the listener but were not associated with participants' conversational satisfaction or perceptions of feeling understood by the listener." [V-abstract]
- **Effect sizes:** not obtained.
- **May say:** in one experiment with students, paraphrasing made the listener seem more likeable but did not make speakers feel more understood or more satisfied.
- **May not say:** any effect size; anything about emotional topics (the topic was an academic policy opinion).

#### 2.2 Weger, H., Jr., Castle Bell, G., Minei, E. M., and Robinson, M. C. (2014). The relative effectiveness of active listening in initial interactions. *International Journal of Listening*, 28(1), 13–31. DOI 10.1080/10904018.2013.813234

- **What I read:** abstract [V-abstract, OpenAlex] and a two-page practitioner summary by the University of Minnesota REACH centre (https://reachfamilies.umn.edu/sites/default/files/prtw/2021-09/Weger_2014__2021091010606.pdf). Full text blocked (Cloudflare) at Taylor and Francis and academia.edu.
- **Design / sample:** 115 participants, 10 trained confederates who responded with one of three message types: active listening, advice, or simple acknowledgements. Initial interactions (strangers).
- **Findings (abstract):** active listening recipients "felt more understood than participants who received either advice or simple acknowledgements." Recipients of either active listening or advice were more satisfied and rated the confederate as more socially attractive than those receiving simple acknowledgements, "although the effect sizes for these differences were small." Satisfaction and social attractiveness "did not differ between participants receiving active listening responses and participants receiving advice" [V-abstract].
- **Note:** the REACH summary's first bullet says active listening produced higher satisfaction than advice, then its third bullet says there was no difference. The abstract supports the latter. Trust the abstract.
- **What "active listening" meant here:** not verified from the full text. Bodie et al. (2015) describe the Weger definition of paraphrase as repeating what was said "in the listener's own words the way he or she understood it" (cited in Bodie et al. 2015, p. 153, [V]). Whether the 2014 active listening messages also included questions or feeling reflections is a gate.
- **May say:** in a study of strangers, paraphrase-based active listening made people feel more understood than advice or bare acknowledgements, but advice was just as satisfying and made the adviser just as likeable; the differences were small.
- **May not say:** "active listening beats advice" without the qualification; any number.

#### 2.3 Bodie, G. D., Vickery, A. J., Cannava, K., and Jones, S. M. (2015). The role of "active listening" in informal helping conversations: Impact on perceptions of listener helpfulness, sensitivity, and supportiveness and discloser emotional improvement. *Western Journal of Communication*, 79(2), 151–173. DOI 10.1080/10570314.2014.943429

- **What I read:** full text, author's self-archived publisher PDF: http://www.grahambodie.com/s/The-Role-of-Active-Listening-WJC_PUBLISHED.pdf
- **Design / sample:** undergraduates disclosed a recent upsetting problem (mean distress 5.08 on a 1 to 7 scale; half were academic problems) to either a trained active listener (n = 41) or an untrained listener (n = 130). Trained listeners asked open questions, paraphrased content, reflected feelings, checked assumptions, and were non-verbally immediate. Behaviours were coded; outcomes rated by disclosers.
- **Findings:** the set of verbal and non-verbal behaviours predicted ratings of the listener's emotional awareness (model R² = .14) and the discloser's affect improvement (R² = .13), but not problem-solving utility or relational assurance. For emotional awareness, paraphrasing carried almost half of the explained variance. For affect improvement, no single behaviour accounted for more than about 3%. Verbal behaviours mattered more than non-verbal ones, partly because untrained listeners were already non-verbally immediate. Authors: "the magnitude of effects was relatively small, suggesting that the enactment of active listening behaviors is not a panacea." [V]
- **Correction to the brief:** the brief asks whether this paper found that "verbal person-centered message quality mattered more than paraphrasing". It did not test that. It found that verbal active-listening behaviours (paraphrasing chief among them) mattered more than non-verbal immediacy. The person-centredness contrast belongs to Burleson's line of work (section 6), which Bodie et al. note was "partially derived from the active listening paradigm of Carl Rogers" [V].
- **Limits stated by the authors:** strangers only; seven of eight confederates female; coded broad categories of behaviour rather than how well each paraphrase was done.
- **May say:** in informal troubles talk between students, active listening behaviours helped the discloser feel emotionally understood and a little better, did nothing for solving the problem, and the effects were small.
- **May not say:** that active listening improves problem-solving or friendship; that the effect is large.

#### 2.4 Bodie's broader point that "active listening" is ill-defined

- **Bodie, G. D. (2011).** The Active-Empathic Listening Scale (AELS): Conceptualization and evidence of validity within the interpersonal domain. *Communication Quarterly*, 59(3), 277–295. DOI 10.1080/01463373.2011.583495. **Read:** full text at http://www.grahambodie.com/s/7-The-Measurement-of-Active-Empathic-Listening_Final-PUBLISHED.pdf. Two studies of college students (N = 416 self-report; N = 217 other-report). 11 items in three dimensions (sensing, processing, responding), adapted from a salesperson scale (Drollinger et al. 2006). Example items: "I am aware of what others imply but do not say." "I ask questions that show my understanding of others’ positions." [V] Bodie opens by saying scholarly work on listening is "vastly underdeveloped" and notes "Definitions of listening are as varied as researchers studying the phenomenon" [V]. **May say:** the most used research measure of active listening is a short self-report scale; it measures what people say they do. **Caution:** self-report validity is weak (next item).
- **Self-rating versus partner rating:** the 2026 review below reports (citing Bodie et al. 2014, a multitrait-multimethod study I did not read) that listeners' self-ratings "correlated negatively with speakers' assessments" [V, quoted from Itzchakov and Bodie 2026]. Gate this against Bodie et al. 2014 before a lesson states it.

#### 2.5 Itzchakov, G., and Bodie, G. D. (2026). Puzzles of interpersonal listening: Conflicting findings, theories, and future research. *Social and Personality Psychology Compass*, 20. DOI 10.1111/spc3.70145. Open access (CC BY).

- **What I read:** full text: http://www.grahambodie.com/s/ItzchakovBodie2026.pdf (Puzzles 1 to 8 in detail, skimmed 9, 10 and discussion).
- **Nature:** narrative review by the two leading figures in, respectively, the psychology and the communication strands of listening research. It is the best single current source on what is uncertain.
- **Key points for this course (paraphrased; the review's claims about other studies are secondary and are gated in (d)):**
  - Puzzle 1: "good listening" is not one thing. Empathy and non-judgement are the most cited markers but both are ambiguous; a simplified active listening "checklist" (paraphrase, reflect, nod, warmth) can feel disingenuous; much guidance reflects "neurotypical and often Western relational ideals" [V].
  - Puzzle 2: speakers often cannot tell when a listener is distracted, yet distraction still harms the speaker's memory for their own story, attitude clarity, and creativity (secondary, citing Pasupathi, Bavelas, Itzchakov).
  - Puzzle 3: disagreement lowers how well people feel listened to even when listening behaviour is held constant (secondary, citing Ren and Schaumberg 2024), yet high-quality listening during disagreement still has benefits.
  - Puzzle 6: listeners overrate themselves; the review cites a study where 71% rated their own listening above average (Welker et al. 2023, secondary).
  - Puzzle 7: follow-up questions predict liking (citing Huang et al. 2017) but mechanical or interrogating questions can backfire.
  - Puzzle 8: "paraphrasing should not be treated as a standalone marker of listening quality, but as a risky intervention that can either deepen or derail understanding." [V] Every paraphrase conveys a stance; therapist formulations transform what clients said in five ways (citing Korman et al. 2013).
- **May say:** as of 2026 the field's own leaders describe listening research as "conceptually fragmented" [V] and paraphrasing as double-edged.
- **May not say:** any secondary statistic in this review as fact without checking the cited primary (gates list).

---

### 3. Active listening in conflict: Gottman versus its defenders

#### 3.1 Gottman, J. M., Coan, J., Carrère, S., and Swanson, C. (1998). Predicting marital happiness and stability from newlywed interactions. *Journal of Marriage and the Family*, 60(1), 5–22. DOI 10.2307/353438

- **What I read:** full text, scanned PDF on Gottman's site, OCR'd: https://www.johngottman.net/wp-content/uploads/2011/05/Predicting-Marital-Happiness-and-Stability-from-Newlywed-Interactions.pdf
- **Design / sample:** 130 newlywed couples from the Seattle area (first marriage within 6 months, childless), recruited 1989 to 1992 via newspaper adverts in two stages; 15-minute conflict discussions coded with SPAFF; physiology recorded. Followed 3 to 6 years; 17 divorces. Analyses compared the 17 divorced couples with the 20 most happily and 20 most unhappily married stable couples (an extreme-groups design; stability F tests on df 1, 55).
- **What "active listening" meant:** they "cast a wider net" than paraphrase-and-validate, counting any sequence where one spouse's negative affect was followed by the partner's interest, affection, humour, or validation [V-OCR, paraphrased].
- **Findings:** those sequences totalled about 4 seconds out of 900; none predicted stability or satisfaction (F ratios .00 to 3.92, all non-significant). "These active listening exchanges hardly ever occurred, and they were not predictive of differential marital outcomes." [V-OCR] Qualitative review of every happy stable couple's tape found they rarely paraphrased, summarised feelings, or validated; the SPAFF "validation" code was mostly capturing listener backchannels (nods, "mm-hm"). What did predict divorce: harsh start-up by the wife, husband's refusal to accept influence, lack of de-escalation, and lack of physiological soothing of the husband. Prediction: 83% accuracy for divorce/stability, 80% for satisfaction.
- **Their conclusion:** they were "shocked and surprised" because they had recommended the intervention themselves. The model "may be expecting a form of emotional gymnastics from people who, at that moment in that relationship, are somewhat emotionally disabled by conflict." [V-OCR] They also note the "conceptual leap": in client-centred therapy the client usually complains about a third party, whereas in marital conflict the listener is the target of the complaint. Therapy "needs to abandon the active listening model" [V-OCR] in favour of gentleness, soothing and de-escalation.
- **Replication:** Kim, Capaldi and Crosby (2007, *JMF* 69, 55–72; full text read, PMC1828692) tested Gottman's models in 85 at-risk couples and report "The major findings of Gottman et al. failed to replicate" [V-abstract]; they did not test the active listening model but report the same pattern: such sequences "occurred very rarely" [V]. Heyman and Slep (2001) criticised lack of cross-validation (abstract not read; UNREAD).
- **May say:** in Gottman's study, happy couples almost never paraphrased or validated during conflict, and doing so did not predict who stayed married or happy.
- **May not say:** that active listening "causes divorce" or "does not work" (the study observed natural behaviour; it did not test training). Do not say the finding replicated; the active listening component was not independently re-tested in the sources I read.

#### 3.2 Stanley, S. M., Bradbury, T. N., and Markman, H. J. (2000). Structural flaws in the bridge from basic research on marriage to interventions for couples. *Journal of Marriage and the Family*, 62(1), 256–264. DOI 10.1111/j.1741-3737.2000.00256.x

- **What I read:** abstract only [V-abstract, OpenAlex]. Unpaywall lists a free publisher PDF; curl and WebFetch received Cloudflare/403.
- **Argument:** Gottman et al.'s recommendations "appear to us to be premature at best" [V-abstract]. Methodological concerns: non-random selection, failure to control for initial differences, observational and physiological procedure, ambiguous statistics; conceptual concerns about labelling patterns and drawing causal inferences from correlational data. The secondary summaries (Hafen and Crane 2003; Kim et al. 2007) add that PREP's speaker-listener technique is a structured tool for when conflict escalates, not a description of what happy couples do naturally, so its absence in happy couples' natural talk does not test it. Kim et al. note Stanley et al. also argued wives' topics were over-represented and that the extreme-groups design limits generalisation [V, Kim et al. 2007].
- **May say:** the authors of the most widely used couples-education programme argued that Gottman's data could not test whether teaching structured listening helps, because the study was correlational and looked at unassisted conversation.

#### 3.3 Gottman, J., Carrère, S., Swanson, C., and Coan, J. A. (2000). Reply to "From basic research to interventions". *JMF*, 62(1), 265–273. DOI 10.1111/j.1741-3737.2000.00265.x

- **Read:** abstract only [V-abstract, OpenAlex]. They restate the critique's main themes (results stated too strongly, analyses not addressing hypotheses, non-replicated findings) and note five conclusions Stanley et al. did not challenge. Whether they conceded anything on active listening I could not see.

#### 3.4 Hafen, M., Jr., and Crane, D. R. (2003). When marital interaction and intervention researchers arrive at different points of view: The active listening controversy. *Journal of Family Therapy*, 25(1), 4–14.

- **Read:** abstract only. An account of the controversy and "the remaining unanswered questions" [V-abstract].

#### 3.5 Gottman's later position: the Gottman-Rapoport Conflict Blueprint (The Gottman Institute, 2016)

- **Read:** full handout https://www.gottman.com/wp-content/uploads/2016/09/Money-Conflict-Blueprint.pdf
- **Content:** "Postpone persuasion until each person can state their partner’s position to their partner’s satisfaction. Take turns as Speaker & Listener." [V] The listener is told to "Hear and repeat the content of the speaker’s needs and perspective", name the speaker's feelings, and "Validate the speaker by completing a sentence like “It makes sense to me that you would feel that way ...”" [V]. It adds self-soothing breaks when "flooded".
- **Significance:** this is, in structure, a speaker-listener exercise with paraphrase and validation, the kind of thing the 1998 paper said therapy should "abandon". The Gottman approach now places it inside a framework of soothing and de-escalation. A lesson may note the apparent convergence; it should not claim Gottman publicly recanted, since I found no statement to that effect.

---

### 4. The high-quality listening programme (Itzchakov, Kluger and colleagues)

#### 4.1 Itzchakov, G., Kluger, A. N., and Castro, D. R. (2017). I am aware of my inconsistencies but can tolerate them: The effect of high quality listening on speakers' attitude ambivalence. *Personality and Social Psychology Bulletin*, 43(1), 105–120. DOI 10.1177/0146167216675339

- **Read:** abstract only [V-abstract]; SAGE paywalled; description in the authors' HBR piece (4.4).
- **Design:** four laboratory experiments manipulating listening quality on various attitude topics.
- **Findings:** high-quality listening reduced speakers' social anxiety, increased awareness of contradictions in their attitudes (objective ambivalence), reduced extremity, and weakened the link between objective and felt ambivalence ("tolerate"). Effects stronger for socially anxious speakers (Study 4). Effect sizes not obtained.

#### 4.2 Itzchakov, G., DeMarree, K. G., Kluger, A. N., and Turjeman-Levi, Y. (2018). The listener sets the tone: High-quality listening increases attitude clarity and behavior-intention consequences. *PSPB*, 44(5), 762–778. DOI 10.1177/0146167217747874

- **Read:** abstract only [V-abstract]. Five experiments (one preregistered). High-quality listening (empathic, attentive, non-judgmental) reduced social anxiety, increased self-awareness and attitude clarity (not attitude correctness), and increased intentions to express the attitude but not to persuade others.

#### 4.3 Kluger, A. N., and Itzchakov, G. (2022). The power of listening at work. *Annual Review of Organizational Psychology and Organizational Behavior*, 9, 121–146. DOI 10.1146/annurev-orgpsych-012420-091013

- **Read:** abstract only [V-abstract]. Proposes "episodic listening theory" (a fleeting state of togetherness). Links listening to performance, leadership, trust, well-being.

#### 4.4 Itzchakov, G., and Kluger, A. N. (2018, 17 May). The power of listening in helping people change. *Harvard Business Review* (digital article H04C0H).

- **Read:** full text, author-hosted reprint: https://cdn.prod.website-files.com/63f7b57cd262df24d969df5b/640cb15a2e2b1682051dba3b_Itzchakov&Kluger_2018_HBR.PDF
- **Content:** Kluger's earlier meta-analysis of 607 feedback experiments found feedback lowered performance in 38% of cases [V]. Lab study: 112 undergraduates as speaker/listener pairs; half the listeners were distracted by text messages; speakers with undistracted listeners "felt less anxious, more self-aware, and reported higher clarity" [V]. Second study: 114 business undergraduates talked 12 minutes about their fitness to be managers to a good (trained), moderate, or poor (acted-distracted) listener; good listening produced more two-sided self-assessments; poor listening produced the most confident one-sided ones. Three field studies (180 workers) with "listening circles" replicated the pattern against controls. Tips: full attention or none, do not interrupt, and so on.
- **Caution:** which PSPB paper each lab study belongs to is not stated in the HBR text; gate.

#### 4.5 Kluger, A. N., Lehmann, M., Aguinis, H., Itzchakov, G., and others (2024). A meta-analytic systematic review and theory of the effects of perceived listening on work outcomes. *Journal of Business and Psychology*, 39(2), 295–344. DOI 10.1007/s10869-023-09897-5. Open access.

- **Read:** abstract only [V-abstract]; Springer returned a JavaScript challenge to curl. This is the meta-analysis the brief asks about; it is in *JBP*, not *Psychological Bulletin*.
- **Findings:** registered review; three-level meta-analysis of 664 effect sizes, 400,020 observations. Perceived listening correlated with work outcomes r̄ = .39, 95% CI [.36, .43], ρ̄ = .44; with relationship quality r̄ = .51; with performance r̄ = .36. "75% of the literature relied on self-reports raising concerns about discriminant validity" [V-abstract]; removing self-report-only data, listening and job performance r̄ = .21 [.13, .29].
- **May say:** people who feel listened to at work report better relationships and performance; the association is robust but mostly correlational and inflated by same-source self-report.
- **May not say:** that listening "causes" a 39% improvement, or that r = .39 is an experimental effect.

#### 4.6 Other programme results (abstracts only unless stated)

- Castro, Anseel, Kluger, Lloyd and Turjeman-Levi (2018), *Psychology of Aesthetics, Creativity, and the Arts*, 12, 489–502: five studies, internal meta-analysis of listening and creativity, N = 744, k = 5, r̄ = .39 [V-abstract].
- Itzchakov, Weinstein, Saluk and Amar (2023), *PSPB* 49, 1273–1294: five experiments, total N = 1,643; high-quality listening reduced state loneliness after disclosing social rejection [V-abstract].
- Itzchakov, Weinstein, Leary, Saluk and Amar (2024), *JPSP* 126(2), 213–239: four experiments; high-quality listening during disagreement reduced attitude polarisation via "positivity resonance" and non-defensive self-reflection. Read: abstract page of the article (1 page) at https://perc.wa.gov/wp-content/uploads/2024/07/Listening_Depolirization.pdf [V].
- **An important partial failure:** DeMarree, Itzchakov, and others (2026, PsyArXiv, DOI 10.31234/osf.io/b9e2s_v2), "Learning goals and listening in polarized conversations: A test across four labs". Preregistered, four sites (Israel, Singapore, UK, US), N = 1,407, live conversations between disagreeing strangers. Instructing one person to adopt a goal of learning about the partner (versus persuading) "did not influence perceived listening quality or polarization outcomes in any sample" [V-abstract]. The correlational path (perceived listening, then psychological safety, then open-mindedness) held in all four labs. Read: abstract only (Europe PMC); preprint PDF not downloadable by curl.
- **Replication status overall:** the programme's experiments are internally consistent, several are preregistered, and many are from one group. Most manipulations contrast a trained or instructed "good" listener with a deliberately distracted or "moderate" one, which is a strong contrast. The four-lab study suggests that telling people to listen better may not change how listened-to their partner feels.

---

### 5. Measurement and definitions (summary)

- Listening is measured three ways: self-report scales (AELS), partner perception ("I felt listened to"), and coded behaviour. The sources agree these converge weakly (Bodie 2011; Itzchakov and Bodie 2026, Puzzle 6; Ickes 1993 on empathic accuracy and self-report, section 7.4). Practically: people are poor judges of their own listening.
- "Active listening" in research has meant: paraphrase only (Weger 2010); paraphrase plus questions plus feeling reflection plus assumption checks plus immediacy (Bodie 2015); any warm response to a partner's negativity (Gottman 1998); "attentive, empathic, non-judgmental" listening as perceived by the speaker (Itzchakov and Kluger). Results do not transfer automatically between these definitions.

---

### 6. Supportive communication: person-centred messages, advice, and invisible support

#### 6.1 Burleson's person-centredness hierarchy

- **Burleson, B. R. (2003).** Emotional support skills. In J. O. Greene and B. R. Burleson (eds), *Handbook of Communication and Social Interaction Skills* (pp. 551–594). Erlbaum. **Not read** (no free copy found).
- **What I read instead:** Jones, S. M., Bodie, G. D., Youngvorst, L., Navarro, M., and Danielson, C. (2018). Mapping the terrain of person-centered supportive conversations. *Communication Monographs*. DOI 10.1080/03637751.2018.1501503. Full text: http://www.grahambodie.com/s/58-Mapping-the-terrain-of-person-centered-supportive-conversations_Jones-Bodie-et-al-Monographs-2018.pdf
  - Definition: person-centred support is "the extent to which a supportive message explicitly acknowledges and validates how a distressed person feels and thinks about a difficult event" [V, citing Burleson 1987].
  - The nine-level hierarchy (Burleson 1982), as described there: **low** (1 condemning the feelings, 2 challenging their legitimacy, 3 ignoring them); **moderate** (4 distracting, 5 condolences not centred on feeling, 6 explanations of the event not centred on feeling); **high** (7 explicitly acknowledging the feelings, 8 elaborated acknowledgement and explanation of them, 9 helping the person see how the feelings fit a broader context) [V, paraphrased from the definitions].
  - Their study: 223 conversations with listeners trained to give low, moderate, or high person-centred comfort. In "high" conversations only 24% of turns were coded high; in "low" conversations almost half were low; over 95% of turns in "moderate" conversations were moderate [V-abstract of that paper].
- **May say:** a large research tradition grades comforting messages by how far they acknowledge and legitimise the other person's feelings, with "you shouldn't feel that way" at the bottom and helping someone understand their feelings in context at the top.

#### 6.2 High, A. C., and Dillard, J. P. (2012). A review and meta-analysis of person-centered messages and social support outcomes. *Communication Studies*, 63(1), 99–118. DOI 10.1080/10510974.2011.598208

- **Read:** abstract only [V-abstract]. 23 studies. Positive linear association between person-centredness and "actual effectiveness", stronger association with "perceived effectiveness"; the association was "attenuated in confederate-based methodologies"; the nine levels need better conceptual separation.
- **Effect sizes:** not obtained. Gate.
- **May say:** across 23 studies, more person-centred messages are judged more helpful and do more good, with stronger effects on ratings of messages than on measured outcomes, and weaker effects when a trained stranger delivers them.

#### 6.3 Advice: advice response theory and sequencing

- **MacGeorge, E. L., Feng, B., and Guntzviller, L. M. (2016).** Advice: Expanding the communication paradigm. *Communication Yearbook*, 40. **Read:** full accepted manuscript at http://www.lisaguntzviller.com/wp-content/uploads/2012/04/MacGeorge-Feng-Guntzviller-Communication-Yearbook-in-press.pdf (sections 1 to 3 closely).
  - Advice threatens "face": negative face when it feels like "butting in", positive face when it implies incompetence (Goldsmith). "Face concerns become particularly salient when advice is unsolicited ... which predicts poorer evaluations" [V]. Polite, mitigated advice is received better than blunt advice.
  - Content features: advice is rated better and more likely to be used when the recommended action seems effective, feasible, and without big drawbacks, and when it confirms what the person already intended [V, paraphrased].
  - Advice response theory (Feng and MacGeorge 2010): message features matter more than who the adviser is; adviser characteristics act mainly through how the message is perceived.
  - Feng's integrated model of advice: advice offered after emotional support and after problem inquiry and analysis is rated higher in quality, more helpful for coping, and more likely to be implemented [V, paraphrased].
- **MacGeorge, E. L., Guntzviller, L. M., Hanasono, L. K., and Feng, B. (2016).** Testing advice response theory in interactions with friends. *Communication Research*, 43(2), 211–231. DOI 10.1177/0093650213510938. **Read:** full text, https://escholarship.org/content/qt0824p98f/qt0824p98f.pdf. N = 244 students discussing a real current problem with a friend in the lab. Adviser characteristics had no significant direct effect on outcomes; intention to implement was driven by perceived content (efficacy, feasibility), while perceived quality and coping help were driven by politeness. The authors note an "unexpectedly large percentage" of participants reported receiving no advice at all [V].
- **Feng, B. (2014).** When should advice be given? Assessing the role of sequential placement of advice in supportive interactions in two cultures. *Communication Research*, 41(7), 913–934. DOI 10.1177/0093650212456203. **Read:** full text, https://escholarship.org/content/qt5q97d0hj/qt5q97d0hj.pdf. 572 US and 540 mainland Chinese participants read a hypothetical scenario. Advice that followed emotional support and then problem inquiry (the "EPA" sequence) was judged higher in quality and more likely to be implemented, in both cultures; Americans valued the preliminary moves more [V-abstract, paraphrased]. **Limit:** hypothetical vignettes, not live talk.
- **Feng, B., and Magen, E. (2016).** Relationship closeness predicts unsolicited advice giving in supportive interactions. *Journal of Social and Personal Relationships*, 33(6), 751–767. **Read:** abstract only [V-abstract]. People responding to hypothetical statements of discontent from friends who were not asking for advice gave unsolicited advice "at a very early stage of a supportive interaction in approximately 70% of cases", more so to closer friends.
- **May say:** advice is not bad in itself; how and when it comes matters. Advice lands better when it is asked for, polite, feasible, and comes after the person feels understood and the problem has been explored. People jump to advice early most of the time, especially with close friends.
- **May not say:** "never give advice" (Weger 2014 found advice as satisfying as active listening; ART finds good advice helps coping).

#### 6.4 Invisible support

- **Bolger, N., Zuckerman, A., and Kessler, R. C. (2000).** Invisible support and adjustment to stress. *JPSP*, 79(6), 953–961. DOI 10.1037//0022-3514.79.6.953. **Read:** full text, http://www.columbia.edu/~nb2229/docs/bolger-zuckerman-kessler-jpsp-2000.pdf. Daily diaries from 68 couples (of 99 recruited) in which one partner was taking the New York State Bar Examination; 32 days before the exam. When the partner reported giving emotional support but the examinee did not report receiving it ("invisible" support), examinee depression fell; the examinee's own report of receiving support was associated with worse adjustment. For anxiety, receipt increased anxiety but provision did not reduce it [V, paraphrased].
- **Bolger, N., and Amarel, D. (2007).** Effects of social support visibility on adjustment to stress: Experimental evidence. *JPSP*, 92(3), 458–475. DOI 10.1037/0022-3514.92.3.458. **Read:** full text, http://www.columbia.edu/~nb2229/docs/bolger-amarel-support-experiments-2006.pdf. Three experiments, female students only (N = 257 total; 35, 96 and 167 recruited), anticipating a stressful speech; a confederate peer gave a public-speaking tip either directly to the participant (visible) or as a question to the experimenter (invisible). Invisible support reduced distress relative to visible and no support; visible support was "either ineffective or it exacerbated reactivity" [V-abstract]. Study 3 found "invisibility per se is not the essence of what makes invisible support effective; rather, it is (at least in part) avoiding the communication that the support recipient is inefficacious." [V]
- **Later nuance (abstract only):** Marini et al. (2019, *J Gerontology B*) found invisible support can cost the provider: spouses felt worse when their help to a partner with osteoarthritis went unrecognised [V-abstract]. The Zee and Bolger review and Girme et al. studies on when visible support helps were not read (UNREAD).
- **May say:** help that makes a stressed person feel incompetent can backfire; the best support often does not announce itself as help.

#### 6.5 Gordon's twelve roadblocks (practitioner lore)

- **Source read:** SAMHSA (2019). *TIP 35: Enhancing Motivation for Change in Substance Use Disorder Treatment*, Exhibit 3.5 "Gordon's 12 Roadblocks to Active Listening", source "Gordon, 1970". Full PDF: https://library.samhsa.gov/sites/default/files/tip-35-pep19-02-01-003.pdf (public domain).
- **The list (as given in TIP 35):** 1 ordering, directing, commanding; 2 warning, cautioning, threatening; 3 giving advice, making suggestions, or providing solutions prematurely or when unsolicited; 4 persuading with logic, arguing, lecturing; 5 moralising, preaching, telling people what they should do; 6 judging, criticising, disagreeing, blaming; 7 agreeing, approving, praising; 8 shaming, ridiculing, labelling; 9 interpreting, analysing; 10 reassuring, sympathising, consoling; 11 questioning, probing (*corrected 2026-09-26, Stage 4 note, lesson 06: the exhibit's item reads "Questioning or probing", and its gloss objects to intensive, interrogating questioning; see "Gate closures, lesson 06 review"*); 12 withdrawing, distracting, humouring, changing the subject. TIP 35: "If you engage in any of these 12 activities, you are talking and not listening." [V]
- **Caution:** item 3's qualifier "prematurely or when unsolicited" appears to be SAMHSA's adaptation; Gordon's original *Parent Effectiveness Training* (1970) wording not checked. Gordon Training International says the list dates to Gordon's 1950s leadership courses; I did not verify that page's text.
- **Evidence status:** no study I found tests the twelve as a set. Several items conflict with evidence: praise and reassurance are low-level person-centred responses in Burleson's hierarchy (consistent), but questioning is a roadblock here while follow-up questions predict liking elsewhere (Puzzle 7), and advice is sometimes as welcome as reflection (Weger 2014). Present as a useful checklist of habits, not as findings.

---

### 7. Empathy: teachable, and good?

#### 7.1 Teding van Berkhout, E., and Malouff, J. M. (2016). The efficacy of empathy training: A meta-analysis of randomized controlled trials. *Journal of Counseling Psychology*, 63(1), 32–41. DOI 10.1037/cou0000093 (erratum for running head only).

- **Read:** abstract only [V-abstract]; APA paywalled.
- **Findings:** 18 RCTs, 1,018 participants (after excluding one outlier). g = 0.63, "adjusted to 0.51 after trim-and-fill evaluation for estimated publication bias" [V-abstract]. Larger effects for health professionals and university students, when trainees were paid, for measures focused on understanding, feeling, or accurately commenting on emotions, and for objective rather than self-report measures. Hours of training not associated with effect. Longest follow-up 6 months. A search summary [T] says programmes using all four components of behavioural skills training (instruction, modelling, practice, feedback) had larger effects; gate.
- **Newer contrast (abstract only):** Williams, Xie, Muratoglu and Goldberg (2026), *Current Psychology*, meta-analysis of digital empathy interventions: 24 studies, n = 3,137, g = 0.19, falling to g = 0.07 (non-significant) after trim-and-fill [V-abstract].
- **May say:** in-person empathy training, especially with practice and feedback, produces moderate short-term gains on empathy measures; app-based training shows little once publication bias is considered.
- **May not say:** that training changes empathy for life, or that the gains show in everyday relationships (not measured).

#### 7.2 Motivated empathy: Zaki and Weisz

- **Zaki, J. (2014).** Empathy: A motivated account. *Psychological Bulletin*, 140(6), 1608–1647. **Read:** abstract [V-abstract]: people avoid empathy when it brings suffering, material cost, or interferes with competition, and approach it for positive affect, affiliation, and social desirability.
- **Weisz, E., and Zaki, J. (2018).** Motivated empathy: A social neuroscience perspective. *Current Opinion in Psychology*, 24, 67–71. **Read:** nothing beyond citation (author PDF returned 403). UNREAD.
- **Weisz, E., Ong, D. C., Carlson, R. W., and Zaki, J. (2021).** Building empathy through motivation-based interventions. *Emotion*, 21(5), 990–999. DOI 10.1037/emo0000929. **Read:** abstract [V-abstract]. 292 first-year students; malleable-mindset and/or social-norms interventions versus control. Eight weeks later: stronger belief that empathy can change, greater empathic accuracy for others' positive (not negative) emotions, and more friends reported. No effect on intergroup outcomes.
- **Zaki, J. (2016?).** "Choosing empathy", Edge.org conversation with written responses from Paul Bloom and others. **Read:** full page https://www.edge.org/conversation/jamil_zaki-choosing-empathy. Zaki: think of empathy "not as something that happens to us, but rather as a choice that we make" [V]. He cites Thomas and Maio: men told that women find sensitive men attractive performed as well as women on an empathic accuracy task (secondary; gate).
- **Cameron, Hutcherson, Ferguson, Scheffer, Hadjiandreou and Inzlicht (2019).** Empathy is hard work. *JEP: General*, 148(6), 962–976. Abstract only (truncated in the API): a robust preference to avoid empathy, linked to its perceived effort; increasing perceived efficacy removed the avoidance.

#### 7.3 Bloom: against (emotional) empathy

- **Bloom, P. (2016).** *Against Empathy: The Case for Rational Compassion*. Ecco. **Not read** (book).
- **Bloom, P. (2017).** Empathy and its discontents. *Trends in Cognitive Sciences*, 21(1), 24–31. DOI 10.1016/j.tics.2016.11.004. **Read:** full text via a course reading copy, http://nwkpsych.rutgers.edu/~kharber/selectedtopicsinsocialpsychology/READINGS/Bloom%202017%20Empathy%20and%20its%20discontents.pdf
  - Distinguishes four senses: cognitive empathy (understanding), emotional contagion, emotional/affective empathy (feeling what you think another feels; Bloom's target), and compassion/concern (wanting another's good without sharing the feeling).
  - Argument: emotional empathy is a "spotlight", hence "innumerate and subject to bias" and can lead to burnout; compassion is better [V-abstract/text]. Cites Singer and Klimecki: empathy training produced empathic distress, compassion training promoted prosocial behaviour and resilience.
  - **Directly relevant to listening (Box 3):** when you go to a loved one anxious or sad, "Here, it is understanding and compassion we need, not empathy" [V]; he concedes that people sometimes do want others to feel what they feel, and that this can be part of intimacy.
  - In the Edge responses Bloom agrees empathy can be chosen but says that flexibility makes it easy to manipulate [V, paraphrased].
- **May say:** Bloom's case is mainly about moral and policy decisions; for personal support he argues for understanding plus compassion over sharing the distress.
- **May not say:** that Bloom is "against caring" or against cognitive empathy.

#### 7.4 Batson and whether perspective-taking instructions raise concern

- **Batson** (empathy-altruism hypothesis): not read directly (UNREAD). Summarised in Bloom 2017: when subjects are encouraged to feel empathy they help more [V, paraphrase of Bloom].
- **McAuliffe, W. H. B., Carter, E. C., Berhane, J., Snihur, A. C., and McCullough, M. E. (2020).** Is empathy the default response to suffering? A meta-analytic evaluation of perspective taking's effect on empathic concern. *Personality and Social Psychology Review*, 24(2), 141–162. **Read:** full text, https://escholarship.org/content/qt5j86s6m6/qt5j86s6m6.pdf (results section). "Remain objective" instructions reduced empathic concern relative to no instructions (g = 0.45, 95% CI [0.35, 0.55]), but "imagine" instructions did not significantly raise it above no instructions (g = .08, 95% CI [−0.02, 0.17]) [V]. Interpretation: people empathise by default; instructions mainly work by suppressing it.

#### 7.5 Eyal, T., Steffel, M., and Epley, N. (2018). Perspective mistaking: Accurately understanding the mind of another requires getting perspective, not taking perspective. *JPSP*, 114(4), 547–571. DOI 10.1037/pspa0000115

- **Read:** full text, author-hosted: https://uploads-ssl.webflow.com/5c484e0f4aa6f839dc553c45/5c955937e29104f659c11a83_Eyal_Steffel_Epley_2018_Perspective_Mistaking%20(1).pdf
- **Design:** 25 experiments. Experiments 1 to 15: accuracy tasks (Reading the Mind in the Eyes, facial expressions, body postures, fake versus genuine smiles, lie detection). Experiments 16 to 24: predicting a partner's or spouse's preferences and opinions (N = 1,132 in 16 to 24). Experiment 25: 104 heterosexual couples.
- **Findings:** manipulation check worked (d = 0.49). Across 1 to 15, perspective taking made people slightly *less* accurate, d = −0.26, 95% CI [−0.40, −0.12], though significant in only 4 of 17 comparisons and not reliable across replications. Across 16 to 24, no gain (one measure slightly worse). Across all 25, d = −0.23, 95% CI [−0.32, −0.13]. Perspective taking reduced egocentric projection but "the information used in its place was not systematically more accurate" [V]. Pretest participants overwhelmingly expected it to help. Experiment 25: asking the partner directly ("perspective getting") improved accuracy substantially (e.g., d = 1.85 for the full-information condition on absolute difference). "If anything, perspective taking decreased accuracy overall while occasionally increasing confidence in judgment." [V] "Increasing interpersonal accuracy seems to require gaining new information rather than utilizing existing knowledge about another person." [V]
- **Limits stated:** cannot prove perspective taking never helps; the negative effect was small and not robust per experiment.
- **May say:** imagining yourself in someone's shoes did not make people better at knowing what that person thought or felt, across 25 experiments; asking them did.
- **May not say:** that perspective taking is useless for kindness or reducing bias (the paper measured accuracy only).

#### 7.6 Empathic accuracy: Ickes

- **Ickes, W. (1993).** Empathic accuracy. *Journal of Personality*, 61(4), 587–610. **Read:** full text, https://greatergood.berkeley.edu/images/uploads/Empathic_Accuracy.pdf
  - Method: two people are videotaped unaware; each lists their own thoughts and feelings at each moment; then each infers the partner's; independent raters score similarity.
  - Stinson and Ickes (1992, reported here): male friends' accuracy was "about 50% higher than those of male strangers (Ms 29.8 vs. 19.9, p < .02)" [V]; the advantage came from shared knowledge of each other's lives, not more interaction or personality similarity.
  - Marangoni et al. (1993, reported here): 80 undergraduates inferring clients' thoughts in three taped simulated psychotherapy sessions improved with exposure, and feedback about the client's actual thoughts "accelerated the rate at which the perceivers' empathic accuracy improved" [V].
  - People "lack metaknowledge" of their own empathic accuracy; self-report empathy scales did not predict actual accuracy, and most correlations were negative [V, paraphrased].
- **May say:** even friends read each other's moment-to-moment thoughts only partly; accuracy improves with feedback, which is what checking your understanding out loud provides.
- **Caution:** the review gives the global score as .00 to 1.00 and does not state the scale of the 29.8 and 19.9 means (lesson 07 review gate closure). The index is a rater-similarity score, not "percent of thoughts read correctly"; do not present 30 as "30% of thoughts".

#### 7.7 Gilovich, T., Savitsky, K., and Medvec, V. H. (1998). The illusion of transparency: Biased assessments of others' ability to read one's emotional states. *JPSP*, 75(2), 332–346. DOI 10.1037/0022-3514.75.2.332

- **Read:** full text via a third-party upload, http://www.communicationcache.com/uploads/1/0/8/8/10887248/the_illusion_of_transparency-_biased_assessments_of_others_ability_to_read_ones_emotional_states.pdf (do not link this in a lesson; link PubMed 9731312).
- **Study 1a:** 39 Cornell undergraduates in groups of five, each lying once in five rounds; chance detection 25%. Liars estimated that "48.8% of the participants would correctly peg them as the liar when, in fact, only 25.6% did so" [V], about chance. Studies 2a/2b: tasters of foul drinks estimated 4.91 of 10 observers would spot their disgust; 3.56 did [V].
- **Relevance:** speakers overestimate how much their inner state shows, so listeners should not assume they have seen it, and speakers should say it. Together with Eyal et al. and Ickes this supports asking and checking rather than assuming.
- **May not say:** that people are "unreadable" when not trying to hide a feeling (every study tested concealment). Corrected: observers were at chance on disgust, not "somewhat better" (see "Gate closures, lesson 07").

---

### 8. Motivational interviewing: reflective listening, OARS, and the fixing reflex

- **Miller, W. R., and Rollnick, S. (2023).** *Motivational Interviewing: Helping People Change and Grow* (4th ed.). Guilford. **Not read.** **Read instead:** Miller and Rollnick, "What's new (and not new) in the 4th edition", https://motivationalinterviewing.org/sites/default/files/what_new_in_mi-4.pdf (full, 2 pp.), and SAMHSA TIP 35 (2019), chapter 3, full.
  - The 4th edition renames terms: "Righting Reflex" becomes "Fixing Reflex"; "Elicit-Provide-Elicit" becomes "Ask-Offer-Ask" [V, table]. OARS is unchanged. New emphasis on "Offering information and advice in an MI-consistent way, recognizing that these are not the opposite of MI" [V].
  - TIP 35 definitions: OARS is Open questions, Affirmations, Reflective listening, Summaries. Reflective listening "requires you to make a mental hypothesis about the underlying meaning or feeling of client statements and then reflect that back to the client with your best guess" [V]. Simple reflections repeat or rephrase; complex reflections use different words to reflect underlying meaning or feeling [V, paraphrased]. Example given: client "My wife is nagging me about my drinking." Simple: "Your wife is nagging you about your drinking." Complex: "Your wife is pressuring you about your drinking." [V] The righting reflex is "the natural response to “fix” a person's problems from a desire to help" [V, TIP 35 citing Miller and Rollnick 2013].
- **Evidence for MI as a clinical method:**
  - Lundahl, B. W., Kunz, C., Brownell, C., Tollefson, D., and Burke, B. L. (2010). A meta-analysis of motivational interviewing: Twenty-five years of empirical studies. *Research on Social Work Practice*, 20(2), 137–160. **Abstract only** [V-abstract]: 119 studies; against weak comparisons g = 0.28, durable, small; against specific treatments g = 0.09, non-significant.
  - Schwenker, R., and others (2023). Motivational interviewing for substance use reduction. *Cochrane Database of Systematic Reviews*, CD008063.pub3. **Abstract read in full** (Europe PMC). 93 studies, 22,776 participants; versus no intervention SMD 0.48 post-intervention (low certainty), fading to 0.20 short term and 0.12 medium term; versus treatment as usual or another active intervention little or no difference; "we have moderate to no confidence in the evidence" [V-abstract].
- **Caution about transfer to everyday talk:** MI is a directional method for helping someone resolve ambivalence about a specific change; complex reflections are used strategically to reinforce "change talk". Itzchakov and Bodie (2026) note paraphrasing in MI can evoke either change talk or sustain talk (secondary). Using MI moves on a friend who has not asked for help to change can itself be a form of steering. A lesson may borrow the fixing-reflex idea and the simple/complex distinction; it should not imply MI's clinical effect sizes apply to conversation between friends.

---

### (a) What this part says the field considers essential

1. Rogers' original idea is about attitude and understanding, not technique: listen for content and feeling together, check your understanding by restating it until the speaker agrees, and withhold judgment, advice and false reassurance while you are trying to understand (Rogers and Farson 1957; Rogers 1957).
2. Being listened to well has real, replicated benefits for the speaker in laboratory experiments (lower anxiety, clearer and less extreme attitudes, less loneliness, less defensiveness in disagreement), and perceived listening correlates strongly with good relationships at work (Itzchakov, Kluger and colleagues; Kluger et al. 2024, r̄ = .39, mostly self-report).
3. Specific "active listening" techniques have modest, outcome-specific effects outside therapy. Paraphrasing tends to make the listener seem likeable and emotionally aware; whether it makes people feel understood varies by study; it does not help solve the problem (Weger 2010, 2014; Bodie et al. 2015). The review by the field's leaders calls paraphrase "a risky intervention" (Itzchakov and Bodie 2026).
4. Understanding another mind is hard and people overrate their success: perspective taking does not improve accuracy, asking does (Eyal et al. 2018); speakers overestimate how transparent they are (Gilovich et al. 1998); friends beat strangers mainly through knowledge (Ickes); self-rated listening and empathy barely track others' ratings or actual accuracy.
5. Supportive responses work best when they acknowledge and legitimise feelings (Burleson's person-centredness; High and Dillard 2012). Advice is not bad in itself; it is judged better when solicited, polite, feasible and placed after emotional support and exploration (MacGeorge, Feng). Help that implies incompetence can backfire (Bolger).
6. Empathy is partly a matter of motivation and can be trained modestly (Teding van Berkhout and Malouff 2016; Weisz et al. 2021), and its emotional form has costs (Bloom 2017).

### (b) Common misconceptions (each named by a source)

1. **"Active listening means a checklist: nod, eye contact, paraphrase."** Rogers and Farson said technique without the attitude is "empty and sterile" [V]; Rogers (1957) said reflecting feelings is "by no means an essential condition" [V]; Itzchakov and Bodie (2026) criticise the "checklist" codification.
2. **"Paraphrasing always makes people feel understood."** Weger et al. (2010): paraphrase did not increase feeling understood; Itzchakov and Bodie (2026), Puzzle 8.
3. **"Happy couples use active listening in their arguments."** Gottman et al. (1998): such exchanges "hardly ever occurred" [V-OCR] in any group.
4. **"Putting yourself in their shoes will tell you what they think."** Eyal, Steffel and Epley (2018): no accuracy gain across 25 experiments; asking works.
5. **"I'm a good listener" / "I can tell how people feel."** Ickes (1993) on lack of metaknowledge; Itzchakov and Bodie (2026) on self-other mismatch; Gilovich et al. (1998) on the speaker's side.
6. **"Never give advice."** Weger et al. (2014): advice was as satisfying as active listening; advice research shows well-timed, requested, feasible advice helps (MacGeorge et al. 2016; Feng 2014). The better rule is "not first, and not unasked" (Feng; Feng and Magen).
7. **"More empathy is always better support."** Bloom (2017, Box 3): a distressed person usually needs "understanding and compassion ... not empathy" [V]; Singer and Klimecki via Bloom on empathic distress.
8. **"Empathy is fixed; some people just have it."** Teding van Berkhout and Malouff (2016); Weisz et al. (2021); Zaki (Edge) on empathy as choice.
9. **"Reassurance ('It'll be fine') is supportive."** Rogers and Farson (1957); Gordon's roadblock 10 (TIP 35); Burleson's hierarchy places non-feeling-centred reassurance in the moderate band, below acknowledgement.

### (c) Contested questions

**C1. Does active listening (paraphrasing) help in ordinary conversation, or only in helping contexts?**
- *Helps broadly:* Itzchakov, Kluger and colleagues: high-quality listening changes speakers' emotions and attitudes across many topics, including everyday opinions and disagreements; Kluger et al. 2024 meta-analysis shows strong associations at work. Best case: many experiments, some preregistered, several outcomes, consistent direction.
- *Helps narrowly:* Weger et al. (2010, 2014) and Bodie et al. (2015): paraphrasing shifts some perceptions (likeability, emotional awareness) with small effects, not satisfaction or problem-solving; Itzchakov and Bodie (2026) call paraphrase risky; Gottman (1998) finds it absent from natural happy talk. Best case: when "active listening" is isolated as a technique, effects shrink.
- *Reconciling note:* the positive programme manipulates the whole listening stance (attention versus distraction), not paraphrase alone; the sceptical studies isolate techniques. Both can be true.
- *What would settle it:* preregistered experiments crossing attention with paraphrase (present/absent) in ordinary conversations between friends, measuring the speaker's felt understanding and later outcomes, rated by speakers and coders.
- *Classification:* contested empirical.

**C2. Does active listening help in conflict?**
- *Gottman et al. (1998):* in natural conflict, paraphrase-and-validate is rare and unpredictive; asking a person to empathise with a complaint aimed at them demands "emotional gymnastics"; teach softened start-up, accepting influence, de-escalation and soothing instead.
- *Stanley, Bradbury and Markman (2000), PREP:* the data are correlational and from an extreme-groups sample; structured listening is a tool for when conflict escalates, not a description of natural talk; absence in happy couples tests nothing about training. Best case: a correlational study cannot overturn an intervention.
- *Convergence:* Gottman's own later Gottman-Rapoport blueprint uses a structured speaker-listener format with restating and validation, plus soothing. The Itzchakov group's disagreement studies (2024) find high-quality listening depolarises; the four-lab 2026 test found an instruction to listen did not change how listened-to partners felt.
- *What would settle it:* RCTs of couples' programmes that dismantle components (listening skill training versus soothing/de-escalation versus both) with long-term outcomes. I did not read such trials; see UNREAD.
- *Classification:* contested empirical.

**C3. Is empathy teachable, and is it good?**
- *Teachable:* meta-analytic g = 0.63 (0.51 after bias correction) for in-person RCTs, mostly short term (Teding van Berkhout and Malouff 2016); motivation interventions raise empathic accuracy for positive emotions (Weisz et al. 2021). Against: digital interventions g = 0.07 after bias correction (Williams et al. 2026); perspective-taking instructions do not raise empathic concern above baseline (McAuliffe et al. 2020) or accuracy (Eyal et al. 2018).
- *Good?* Bloom: emotional empathy is a biased, innumerate spotlight that can burn people out; prefer cognitive understanding plus compassion. Zaki: empathy is a motivated choice, so its biases are not fixed, and it can be chosen, up or down (he says it must sometimes be turned down; see "Gate closures, lesson 07 review"); it remains a social bridge. Bloom accepts that empathy is flexible but says that makes it manipulable.
- *What would settle the empirical part:* long-term RCTs with behavioural outcomes (helping, relationship quality) measured by others, not self-report. The normative part (should compassion or empathy guide moral choice?) is partly a value question.
- *Classification:* teachability is contested empirical (moderate short-term evidence); "is it good" is partly value, partly empirical (burnout and bias findings).

**C4. Should you give advice when someone shares a problem?**
- *Hold back (Rogerian and practitioner tradition):* Rogers and Farson (advice "seldom taken"), Gordon's roadblocks, MI's fixing reflex. Best case: early advice signals you did not listen and can imply incompetence (Goldsmith's face threat; Bolger and Amarel's inefficacy mechanism).
- *Advice helps when done well (communication research):* Weger et al. 2014: advice as satisfying as active listening; ART: feasible, effective, polite advice is used and aids coping; Feng 2014: advice after emotional support and problem inquiry is rated best; MI 4th edition now says offering advice is "not the opposite of MI" [V] and gives Ask-Offer-Ask.
- *What would settle it:* live-interaction experiments varying timing and solicitation of advice with real problems and follow-up outcomes (most sequencing evidence is from vignettes).
- *Classification:* largely established that timing, solicitation and politeness matter; whether to advise at all in a given case is a judgement.

### (d) Gates: claims a drafter must re-check against a primary text before a lesson states them

1. Any effect size for Weger et al. 2010 or 2014 (I have abstracts only); and what the 2014 "active listening" messages contained.
2. Gottman et al. 1998 quotations are OCR; re-check wording ("emotional gymnastics", "hardly ever occurred", "abandon the active listening model") against the page image.
3. The content of Stanley, Bradbury and Markman 2000 beyond its abstract, including the claim (from secondary sources) that speaker-listener is meant for escalated conflict, not to mimic natural talk.
4. Whether Gottman ever explicitly revised his 1998 position on active listening (I found only the later blueprint handout, not a statement).
5. High and Dillard 2012 effect sizes (not obtained).
6. Teding van Berkhout and Malouff 2016: the behavioural-skills-training moderator claim (came via search summary [T]).
7. Which Itzchakov PSPB paper each HBR-described study belongs to, and those papers' effect sizes.
8. Secondary claims inside Itzchakov and Bodie 2026: Bodie et al. 2014 negative self-other correlation; Welker et al. 2023 (71% above average); Huang et al. 2017 on follow-up questions; Ren and Schaumberg 2024 on disagreement; Collins et al. 2024 on undetected distraction; their statement that Rogers 1957 "admitted that therapists interpret and evaluate subconsciously" (I read Rogers 1957 and did not find this; check).
9. Gordon's roadblock wording: the qualifier "prematurely or when unsolicited" on the advice roadblock is SAMHSA's; check Gordon 1970 before quoting it as Gordon's.
10. The Thomas and Maio study on men's empathic accuracy (reported by Zaki only).
11. The origin of the "restate to the speaker's satisfaction" rule (Rogers 1952 HBR / 1951 talk) before dating it.
12. MI 4th edition definitions of simple and complex reflections: I took them from TIP 35 (which cites the 3rd edition, 2013); check the 4th edition wording.
13. Kluger et al. 2024 figures are from the abstract; if a lesson cites the "fewer than 6% negative effects" figure, check it in the paper (it is quoted secondhand in Itzchakov and Bodie 2026).

### (e) Best free resources to link (checked live 2026-09-26 by curl unless noted)

- Rogers and Farson, Active Listening (1987 excerpt): https://wholebeinginstitute.com/wp-content/uploads/Rogers_Farson_Active-Listening.pdf (the founding text, short and readable).
- Rogers 1957, six conditions: https://counselling-tutor.s3.eu-west-2.amazonaws.com/CSR+Documents/The+Necessary+and+Sufficient+Conditions+of+Therapeutic+Personality+Change.pdf (Rogers on why reflecting feelings is not the point).
- Itzchakov and Bodie 2026, Puzzles of interpersonal listening (open access): http://www.grahambodie.com/s/ItzchakovBodie2026.pdf and https://doi.org/10.1111/spc3.70145 (the current state of the field's disagreements; publisher link not curl-checkable, Cloudflare).
- Bodie et al. 2015, active listening in helping conversations: http://www.grahambodie.com/s/The-Role-of-Active-Listening-WJC_PUBLISHED.pdf (a clear student-level experiment with honest limits).
- Itzchakov and Kluger 2018, HBR: https://hbr.org/2018/05/the-power-of-listening-in-helping-people-change (author PDF: https://cdn.prod.website-files.com/63f7b57cd262df24d969df5b/640cb15a2e2b1682051dba3b_Itzchakov&Kluger_2018_HBR.PDF) (plain-language account of the lab and field studies).
- Eyal, Steffel and Epley 2018, Perspective mistaking (author-hosted): https://uploads-ssl.webflow.com/5c484e0f4aa6f839dc553c45/5c955937e29104f659c11a83_Eyal_Steffel_Epley_2018_Perspective_Mistaking%20(1).pdf (the "ask, don't guess" evidence).
- Gottman et al. 1998 (author site, scanned): https://www.johngottman.net/wp-content/uploads/2011/05/Predicting-Marital-Happiness-and-Stability-from-Newlywed-Interactions.pdf (the source of the conflict critique).
- Gottman-Rapoport Conflict Blueprint: https://www.gottman.com/wp-content/uploads/2016/09/Money-Conflict-Blueprint.pdf (a one-page structured listening exercise for disagreements).
- Kim, Capaldi and Crosby 2007, replication test (PMC): https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC1828692/ (shows how the Gottman models fared in another sample).
- Bolger and Amarel 2007 (author site): http://www.columbia.edu/~nb2229/docs/bolger-amarel-support-experiments-2006.pdf (why help can backfire).
- MacGeorge et al. 2016, advice with friends (eScholarship): https://escholarship.org/content/qt0824p98f/qt0824p98f.pdf; Feng 2014, when to give advice: https://escholarship.org/content/qt5q97d0hj/qt5q97d0hj.pdf
- SAMHSA TIP 35 (public domain; roadblocks, OARS, reflections): https://library.samhsa.gov/sites/default/files/tip-35-pep19-02-01-003.pdf
- Miller and Rollnick, What's new in MI 4th edition: https://motivationalinterviewing.org/sites/default/files/what_new_in_mi-4.pdf (fixing reflex, Ask-Offer-Ask).
- Zaki, "Choosing empathy" with Bloom's reply: https://www.edge.org/conversation/jamil_zaki-choosing-empathy (the debate in the protagonists' own words).
- Ickes 1993, Empathic accuracy (Greater Good copy): https://greatergood.berkeley.edu/images/uploads/Empathic_Accuracy.pdf
- Kluger et al. 2024 meta-analysis (open access, Springer; curl blocked by a bot check, OA status from Unpaywall): https://doi.org/10.1007/s10869-023-09897-5
- Cochrane review of MI for substance use (PMC): https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC10714668/ (listed by OpenAlex/Europe PMC; not curl-checked).
- Do not link: the communicationcache copy of Gilovich 1998 or the Rutgers course copy of Bloom 2017 (third-party uploads); link PubMed 9731312 and https://doi.org/10.1016/j.tics.2016.11.004 instead.

### (f) Not read

Full texts I could not obtain (abstract only): Weger et al. 2010 and 2014; Stanley, Bradbury and Markman 2000; Gottman et al. 2000 reply; Hafen and Crane 2003; Itzchakov, Kluger and Castro 2017; Itzchakov et al. 2018; Kluger and Itzchakov 2022; Kluger et al. 2024; High and Dillard 2012; Teding van Berkhout and Malouff 2016; Lundahl et al. 2010; Zaki 2014; Weisz et al. 2021; Feng and Magen 2016; Castro et al. 2018; Itzchakov et al. 2023 and 2024; DeMarree et al. 2026; Cameron et al. 2019; Williams et al. 2026; Marini et al. 2019. Not read at all: Rogers and Farson 1957 full booklet; Rogers 1952 HBR; Burleson 2003 chapter; Bloom 2016 book; Weisz and Zaki 2018; Batson's primary papers; Miller and Rollnick 4th edition text; Gordon 1970 *P.E.T.*; Stinson and Ickes 1992; Bodie et al. 2014 MTMM; Heyman and Slep 2001; Elliott et al. 2018 empathy meta-analysis; Kluger et al. 2021 social relations model study; Nickerson 1999 curse of knowledge; Savitsky et al. 2011 closeness-communication bias; Zee and Bolger review and Girme et al. on visible support; Feng 2009; any RCT dismantling couples' listening training; Weinstein and Itzchakov others cited secondarily.
