## Part D: Nonverbal communication, body language, lie detection, culture, and the misconceptions

Researcher D covered the evidence on nonverbal communication as it bears on a conversation course: where the "93% nonverbal" figure came from and what its author says about it; what research shows about detecting lies from behaviour (the big meta-analyses, the gaze-aversion stereotype, microexpressions, NLP eye cues, airport behaviour detection); what nonverbal behaviour can reliably tell us (thin slices, the facial-expression universality debate); mimicry and power posing and their replication records; culture and gaze, turn timing, and the high/low-context and Hofstede frameworks; autism, eye contact and the double empathy problem; and the listening myths named by Zenger and Folkman. Read on 2026-09-26. Method: primary texts fetched with curl and converted with pdftotext or pandoc wherever obtainable (PLoS ONE, PMC, author and university repositories, Nature, the Max Planck repository); abstracts through the PubMed E-utilities, Crossref and Semantic Scholar APIs, and ERIC; WebFetch only where curl was blocked (marked [T]). Raw extracts are saved in `cl/D/`. The one scanned chapter (Rossano, Brown and Levinson 2009) was read by OCR (tesseract) of selected pages; quotations from it are marked [V, OCR]. Bond and DePaulo 2006 is also an OCR'd scan with character errors, so figures from it are given as paraphrase, not quotation. The two 1967 Mehrabian papers are paywalled and I could not obtain them; their designs are reported here from secondary sources and flagged as a gate.

### 1. Mehrabian and the "7-38-55" / "93% nonverbal" figure

**1.1 Mehrabian, A., and Wiener, M. (1967). Decoding of inconsistent communications. *Journal of Personality and Social Psychology*, 6(1), 109-114. doi:10.1037/h0024532. PMID 6032751.**
What I read: bibliographic record only (PubMed has no abstract; APA PsycNet, Semantic Scholar and Unpaywall report it closed). Not obtained.
Design as reported by secondary sources (a 1994 practitioner article by C. E. Johnson, reprinted at coachteam.no, which says it read the journal article; the Wikipedia article on Mehrabian): nine single words, three positive ("honey", "thanks", "dear"), three neutral ("maybe", "really", "oh"), three negative ("don't", "brute", "terrible"), each read by two female speakers in a liking, neutral and disliking tone; listeners (reported as 30 University of California undergraduates) judged the speaker's attitude towards an imagined addressee, one group told to attend only to tone, one only to word meaning, one to both. Reported finding: when word and tone conflicted, judgements of attitude followed the tone more than the word. Johnson reports that the authors themselves limited the result to situations where no other information about the speaker-addressee relationship is available. All of this is second-hand. Gate.

**1.2 Mehrabian, A., and Ferris, S. R. (1967). Inference of attitudes from nonverbal communication in two channels. *Journal of Consulting Psychology*, 31(3), 248-252. doi:10.1037/h0024648. PMID 6046577.**
What I read: bibliographic record only; paywalled. Design as reported secondarily (Johnson 1994; Trustmakers research summary of Lapakko): one word, "maybe", recorded by three female speakers in liking, neutral and disliking tones; black-and-white photographs of three female models posing liking, neutral and disliking faces; listeners (reported as 17 women) rated the attitude of the pictured person towards an imagined other while seeing a photo and hearing a recording. Reported finding: the face carried about 3/2 the weight of the voice. The 7/38/55 coefficients come from combining this 3:2 face-to-voice ratio with the Mehrabian and Wiener voice-over-words result. Several secondary sources say the combination of two separate studies is itself a methodological weakness (Wikipedia's "Criticism" section lists: single recorded words, figures combined from two studies, only positive/negative feeling, female participants only, no posture or gesture). Gate: the exact N, the sex of participants in each study, and the arithmetic must be checked in the 1967 papers or in *Silent Messages* pp. 75-80 before a lesson states them.

**1.3 Mehrabian's own statement of the limits (his website).** URL: http://www.kaaj.com/psych/smorder.html (fetched with curl today; live, "Copyright 1995-2016").
What I read: the full page. It gives the formula [V]: "Total Liking = 7% Verbal Liking + 38% Vocal Liking + 55% Facial Liking", and then [V]: "Unless a communicator is talking about their feelings or attitudes, these equations are not applicable." It says the equations "were derived from experiments dealing with communications of feelings and attitudes (i.e., like-dislike)" [V] and points to pp. 75-80 of *Silent Messages* (1981) and its references 286 and 305 as the original sources. A lesson may quote these two short sentences and cite the page.
*Silent Messages* (1971; 2nd ed. 1981): not read. The Internet Archive copy (silentmessagesim00mehr) is lending-restricted and its text returned 401. Johnson (1994) reports a phone conversation with Mehrabian in March 1994 in which he said the findings were not meant for normal communication, and paraphrases p. 134 of the book on the limits of actions for expressing complex ideas. Second-hand; do not quote.

**1.4 Lapakko, D. (1997). Three cheers for language: A closer examination of a widely cited study of nonverbal communication. *Communication Education*, 46(1), 63-67. doi:10.1080/03634529709379073.**
What I read: ERIC abstract only (via the ERIC API). The abstract says the essay focuses on applications of the Mehrabian and Ferris 1967 study "that inferred that communication is 7% verbal, 38% vocal, and 55% facial" [V, ERIC abstract] and that these applications "overlook important limitations which do not warrant formulation of a precise numerical formula" [V, ERIC abstract]. A quotation widely attributed through Lapakko 1997 p. 65 to Mehrabian (1995, personal communication) is that it is "absurd" to suggest the verbal portion of all communication is only 7%; I saw it only in the Trustmakers summary, so it must not be quoted until checked in Lapakko.

**1.5 Lapakko, D. (2007). Communication is 93% nonverbal: An urban legend proliferates. *Communication and Theater Association of Minnesota Journal*, 34, 7-19 (pages to be checked).**
What I read: nothing primary. The open copy at cornerstone.lib.mnsu.edu is behind a Cloudflare challenge (curl got the challenge page; WebFetch got 403). A secondary one-page research summary (Trustmakers, trustmakers-training.ca, 2025) reports its method: a January 2007 Google search for "communication 93 percent nonverbal", 79 relevant sites content-analysed, of which only 4 questioned the formula and only 1 correctly limited it to emotional meaning. Treat these numbers as a gate.

**1.6 Other named critiques.** Trimboli and Walker (1987, *Journal of Nonverbal Behavior*, "Nonverbal dominance in the communication of affect: A myth?") and the Oxford Reference entry were not obtained. Wikipedia cites Argyle et al. (1970, *British Journal of Social and Clinical Psychology* 9:222-231) as finding posture and other nonverbal cues outweighing verbal cues for superior/inferior attitudes, and a 1992 study finding the opposite weighting for happy/sad mood; both unread, and they show the relative weight of channels depends on the task.

**What a lesson may say:** the 7-38-55 figures come from two small 1967 laboratory studies of single words and posed photos, about judging liking or disliking when tone or face contradicts the word; the author says the equations do not apply unless someone is talking about feelings or attitudes. **May not say:** exact sample sizes, that all participants were women, or the Lapakko 2007 counts, until the gates are cleared.

### 2. Lie detection from behaviour

**2.1 Bond, C. F., Jr., and DePaulo, B. M. (2006). Accuracy of deception judgments. *Personality and Social Psychology Review*, 10(3), 214-234. doi:10.1207/s15327957pspr1003_2.**
What I read: full text (a scanned copy posted by the ACLU, OCR text with errors; pages 214-234). Design: meta-analysis of 206 documents; 24,483 judges attempting to tell lies from truths told by 4,435 senders, in real time, with no special aids or training. Findings (paraphrased from the OCR text): mean 54% correct; 47.55% of lies classified as deceptive and 61.34% of truths as truthful (a truth bias); mean accuracy d of roughly .40 when scaled by cross-judge standard deviations; the true standard deviation of mean accuracy across studies was only 4.52 percentage points, so results are strikingly consistent. Judgements from video-only (visible) presentations were less accurate than from audio, audiovisual, or transcript presentations; video-only messages also drew a lie bias. Experts: in 19 studies with 20 within-study expert/non-expert comparisons, weighted mean d = -.025 (95% CI -.105 to .055), no evidence that experts (police, judges, psychiatrists, job interviewers, auditors) discriminate better; experts averaged under 55%. The authors propose a double standard: people judge others' lies more harshly than their own.
Replication status: the 54% figure is the consensus benchmark, restated in Vrij et al. 2019 and the GAO 2013 report. **May say:** people average about 54% at telling lies from truths, only a little above the 50% of guessing, and trained professionals do no better on average. **May not say:** "no one can ever detect lies" (the effect is above chance and there are context and questioning methods; see 2.9).

**2.2 Bond, C. F., Jr., and DePaulo, B. M. (2008). Individual differences in judging deception: Accuracy and bias. *Psychological Bulletin*, 134(4), 477-492. doi:10.1037/0033-2909.134.4.477.**
What I read: abstract (PubMed). 247 samples; measurement-corrected standard deviations in judge ability are "less than 1%" of lies detected [V]; "the best judges are no more accurate than a stochastic mechanism would produce" [V]. People differ more in how readily they believe others, and senders differ in how credible they look whether lying or not. Two comments and a reply appeared in the same issue (not read). **May say:** there is little evidence of "human lie detectors"; how believable the speaker looks matters more than who is judging.

**2.3 DePaulo, B. M., Lindsay, J. J., Malone, B. E., Muhlenbruck, L., Charlton, K., and Cooper, H. (2003). Cues to deception. *Psychological Bulletin*, 129(1), 74-118. doi:10.1037/0033-2909.129.1.74.**
What I read: full text (MIT Media Lab copy, 45 pages, text layer good). Design: 1,338 estimates of 158 cues from 120 independent samples (116 reports). Findings: liars were somewhat less forthcoming, told less compelling tales, made a more negative impression and were more tense; but "many behaviors showed no discernible links, or only weak links, to deceit" [V]. Median absolute effect across the 88 cues with enough data was d = 0.10; only two met Cohen's 0.50. Eye contact: 32 estimates, N = 1,491, d = 0.01 (CI -0.06 to 0.08); gaze aversion: 6 estimates, N = 411, d = 0.03 (CI -0.11 to 0.16). Their words: "none of the measures of looking behavior supported the widespread belief that liars do not look their targets in the eye" [V]. Some cues did differ: pupil dilation d = 0.39 (k = 4), pitch d = 0.21, rated nervousness d = 0.27, verbal and vocal immediacy lower in liars. Cues were stronger when liars were motivated, especially for identity-relevant stakes, and when lies were about transgressions; low-stakes lies left almost no cues.
Replication: Luke (2019, *Perspectives on Psychological Science*, "Lessons from Pinocchio", not read) argues even these estimates may be inflated by small samples and publication bias. **May say:** in the largest review, looking away was not a sign of lying; most behaviours differed little or not at all.

**2.4 The Global Deception Research Team (2006). A world of lies. *Journal of Cross-Cultural Psychology*, 37(1), 60-74. doi:10.1177/0022022105282295. PMC2957901.**
What I read: full text (PMC). Study 1: 2,320 lifelong residents of 58 countries (20 men and 20 women per country, mostly students) answered "How can you tell when people are lying?"; 11,157 responses coded into 103 beliefs. 63.66% mentioned gaze aversion, far ahead of nervousness (28.15%), incoherence (25.30%) and body movements (25.04%). Gaze aversion was the top belief in 51 of 58 countries; country explained only 13.81% of variance in it. Study 2 (questionnaire, 63 countries): in 61 of 63, respondents more often said liars decrease eye contact than increase it. Across both studies, 75 countries and 43 languages. Their summary: the belief "was expressed in every one of the 75 countries we studied" [V]. They note gaze aversion "was shown to have little association with lying" in DePaulo et al. 2003 [V]. **May say:** the belief that liars avoid eye contact is found worldwide, and it is wrong.

**2.5 Hartwig, M., and Bond, C. F., Jr. (2011). Why do lie-catchers fail? A lens model meta-analysis of human lie judgments. *Psychological Bulletin*, 137(4), 643-659. doi:10.1037/a0023589.**
What I read: abstract (PubMed and ERIC). Four meta-analyses; 66 cues in 153 samples. Perceived deception is tied to impressions of incompetence (r = .59) and ambivalence (r = .49); eye contact correlates only weakly with lie judgements (r = -.15) despite what people say they use. Cues people use and cues that actually differ covary strongly (r = .59, .72). Conclusion: people "rarely rely on the wrong cues" [V]; accuracy is limited mainly by "weaknesses in behavioral cues to deception" [V]. **May say:** the main problem is not that people watch the wrong signals; there are few good signals to watch. Note the tension with 2.4: people *say* they use gaze, but their actual judgements lean on it less.

**2.6 Aamodt, M. G., and Custer, H. (2006). Who can best catch a liar? A meta-analysis of individual differences in detecting deception. *The Forensic Examiner*, 15(1), 6-11.**
What I read: search snippets only (Semantic Scholar, OJP abstract page failed to load). Reported: 108 studies, 16,537 participants; professional lie catchers 55.51% versus students and citizens 54.22%; confidence, age, experience, education and sex unrelated to accuracy. Snippet only; gate before use.

**2.7 Vrij, A., Hartwig, M., and Granhag, P. A. (2019). Reading lies: Nonverbal communication and deception. *Annual Review of Psychology*, 70, 295-317. doi:10.1146/annurev-psych-010418-103135.**
What I read: full text (PDF of the published version hosted at gwern.net; the University of Portsmouth portal lists it). Abstract: "the nonverbal cues to deceit discovered to date are faint and unreliable and that people are mediocre lie catchers when they pay attention to behavior" [V]. Of 50 cues with at least six measurements, 35 were at least partly nonverbal and 25 of those 35 (71%) showed no relation to deception; verbal cues did better (average d = 0.30; 8 of 18 related). Microexpressions: Porter and ten Brinke (2008) is described as "the first and, to date, only published experiment" [V] on microexpressions and deception; microexpressions appeared in 2% of fragments and six of the 14 were shown by truth tellers. NLP eye movements: no support (Mann et al. 2012; Wiseman et al. 2012); the NLP founders never claimed eye movements reveal lies. Objective multi-cue computer classification in Hartwig and Bond (2014) reached only 67.68%. Training on vocal and visual cues gives small gains; content-based training gives medium gains (citing Hauch et al.). Airport screening: they know of no peer-reviewed research on what officers should look for, and cite GAO 2017 that the TSA lacked valid evidence for most of its behavioural indicators. Summary points include that stereotypical views "are hard to debunk" [V]. **May say** all of the above with this citation.

**2.8 Wiseman, R., Watt, C., ten Brinke, L., Porter, S., Couper, S.-L., and Rankin, C. (2012). The eyes don't have it: Lie detection and neuro-linguistic programming. *PLoS ONE*, 7(7), e40259. doi:10.1371/journal.pone.0040259.**
What I read: full text. Three studies testing the NLP-derived claim that looking up to the right signals lying and up to the left signals truth (for right-handers). Study 1: 32 right-handed participants (12 men, mean age 22.3) each lied and told the truth in a filmed interview about hiding or placing a phone; two coders (ICC 0.89) counted up-right and up-left gazes and glances; no significant differences on any of four measures. Study 2: 50 participants (21 told the NLP pattern, 29 controls) judged 32 clips; accuracy 16.33 vs 16.59 correct out of 32, confidence 4.65 vs 4.58; no differences. Study 3: 52 real televised appeals for missing relatives (26 later shown to be lying); no up-right or up-left gazes at all, and glances did not differ. Conclusion: "it would seem irresponsible for such practitioners to continue to encourage people to make important decisions on the basis of such claims" [V]. Replicated in direction by Mann et al. 2012 (not read). Samples are modest. **May say:** the NLP eye-direction lie cue was tested three ways and found nothing.

**2.9 Porter, S., and ten Brinke, L. (2008). Reading between the lies: Identifying concealed and falsified emotions in universal facial expressions. *Psychological Science*, 19(5), 508-514. doi:10.1111/j.1467-9280.2008.02116.x.**
What I read: abstract (PubMed). Participants responded to emotional images with genuine or deceptive (simulated, neutralised, masked) expressions; 104,550 frames in 697 expressions coded. Inconsistent emotional leakage occurred in every participant at least once but lasted longer than the definition of a microexpression; "Microexpressions were exhibited by 21.95% of participants in 2% of all expressions, and in the upper or lower face only" [V]. Untrained observers were only slightly above chance. **May say:** fleeting "microexpressions" are rare and appear in truthful expressions too.

**2.10 Jordan, S., Brimbal, L., Wallace, D. B., Kassin, S. M., Hartwig, M., and Street, C. N. H. (2019). A test of the micro-expressions training tool: Does it improve lie detection? *Journal of Investigative Psychology and Offender Profiling*, 16(3), 222-235. doi:10.1002/jip.1532.**
What I read: abstract (Semantic Scholar API); author list, volume and pages confirmed via Crossref. N = 90, randomly assigned to Ekman's METT, a bogus training, or none; each judged five videos. Bayesian analyses: METT did not outperform the other groups; "overall accuracy was slightly below chance" [V]. The accepted manuscript at pure.hud.ac.uk is behind Cloudflare. Small N and few judgements per person; one study.

**2.11 US Government Accountability Office (2013). *Aviation Security: TSA Should Limit Future Funding for Behavior Detection Activities* (GAO-14-159), published 8 November 2013.**
What I read: the GAO product page via WebFetch [T] (curl got "Access Denied"). Reported: GAO reviewed four meta-analyses covering over 400 studies from 60 years and concluded that the human ability to identify deception from behavioural indicators is "the same as or slightly better than chance" [T]; the DHS 2011 validation study of SPOT did not demonstrate effectiveness; GAO recommended Congress consider "the absence of scientifically validated evidence" [T] when funding behaviour detection. Denault et al. (2020, full text read) add that GAO in 2017 found the TSA lacked valid evidence for most of its revised indicators, that SPOT indicators included "avoiding eye contact" and "looking down", and that SPOT cost an estimated $1.5 billion from 2007 to 2015 (citing the DHS Office of Inspector General 2016). Gate: re-get the GAO text verbatim before quoting.

**2.12 Hauch, V., Sporer, S. L., Michael, S. W., and Meissner, C. A. (2016; online 2014). Does training improve the detection of deception? A meta-analysis. *Communication Research*, 43(2), 283-343. doi:10.1177/0093650214534974.**
What I read: abstract (Crossref). 30 studies; small-to-medium training effect on accuracy (g = 0.331); "larger training effects if the training was based on verbal content cues" [V]; nonverbal and paraverbal training had only small effects.

**2.13 Luke, T. J., Mac Giolla, E., Memon, A., Landström, S., Granhag, P. A., and Kassin, S. (2023). What have we learned about cues to deception? A survey of expert opinions. *Psychology, Crime & Law*. doi:10.1080/1068316X.2023.2292504.**
What I read: full text (open access PDF from saulkassin.org). Survey of N = 50 active deception-cue researchers; 80% agreement set as consensus. "The single issue on which more than 80% of experts agreed was that gaze aversion is not generally diagnostic of deception" [V]. Nervousness and detail reached about 70%; most other cues and all moderators lacked consensus. **May say:** experts disagree about most cues but agree that looking away is not a general sign of lying.

**2.14 Levine, T. R. (2014). Truth-default theory (TDT): A theory of human deception and deception detection. *Journal of Language and Social Psychology*, 33(4), 378-392. doi:10.1177/0261927X14535916.**
What I read: abstract (Crossref). TDT "emphasizes contextualized communication content in deception detection over nonverbal behaviors" [V]; people default to believing others and this is adaptive. Relevant to the contested question: Levine's programme reports that accuracy rises well above 54% when judges use context, evidence and good questioning (claims not read here; see UNREAD).

**2.15 Denault, V., Plusquellec, P., Jupe, L. M., St-Yves, M., et al. (2020). The analysis of nonverbal communication: The dangers of pseudoscience in security and justice contexts. *Anuario de Psicología Jurídica*, 30(1), 1-12. doi:10.5093/apj2019a9.**
What I read: full text (Redalyc HTML). Examines SPOT, the Behavior Analysis Interview (BAI, first step of the Reid technique) and "synergology". Names the gaze-avoidance belief "a widespread misconception" [V]. Quotes the Reid manual (Inbau et al. 2013, p. 135) that a suspect "who does not make direct eye contact is probably withholding information" and notes research shows lack of eye contact "is not a valid sign of lying" [V]. Quotes Harrigan (2005, p. 139): "Unlike certain facial expressions, there are few, if any, body movements that have invariant meaning within or across cultures" [V, as quoted in Denault et al.]. This is the best scholarly peg for the "crossed arms means defensive" myth, since I found no study testing that specific claim. Lists "the body language never lies" as a popular slogan.

### 3. What nonverbal behaviour can tell you

**3.1 Ambady, N., and Rosenthal, R. (1992). Thin slices of expressive behavior as predictors of interpersonal consequences: A meta-analysis. *Psychological Bulletin*, 111(2), 256-274. doi:10.1037/0033-2909.111.2.256.**
What I read: full text (MIT course copy, text layer). Meta-analysis of predictions of objective outcomes from observations under five minutes; overall r = .39 across 38 results. Longer observation did not predict better: under half a minute did about as well as four to five minutes. Channel (face, speech, body, tone) did not matter; lab versus natural behaviour did not matter; effect sizes were similar for clinical, social, and deception-detection outcomes. **May say:** brief glimpses of behaviour carry real information about some outcomes, at a moderate level of accuracy. **May not say:** that first impressions are reliable judgements of a person; r = .39 leaves most variance unexplained, and the outcomes studied were specific (for example, teacher ratings, not honesty in general).

**3.2 Ambady, N., and Rosenthal, R. (1993). Half a minute: Predicting teacher evaluations from thin slices of nonverbal behavior and physical attractiveness. *JPSP*, 64(3), 431-441. doi:10.1037/0022-3514.64.3.431.** Not read (no abstract in Crossref; paywalled). Commonly reported: silent clips of college teachers predicted end-of-term student ratings. Not verified.

**3.3 Barrett, L. F., Adolphs, R., Marsella, S., Martinez, A. M., and Pollak, S. D. (2019). Emotional expressions reconsidered: Challenges to inferring emotion from human facial movements. *Psychological Science in the Public Interest*, 20(1), 1-68. doi:10.1177/1529100619832930. PMC6640856.**
What I read: full text (PMC), searched and read in the sections on reliability and specificity. Target: the "common view" that emotions can be read from standard facial configurations. Findings: people do sometimes smile when happy, scowl when angry and so on "more than what would be expected by chance" [V], but with "substantial variation" across cultures, situations and people [V]. They report a meta-analysis (Duran and Fernández-Dols 2018; 89 effect sizes, 47 studies, 3,599 participants): average r = .32 between a configuration and a measure of the emotion, and the expected configuration appeared on average in about 19% of emotional events. Hence "a scowling facial configuration is an expression of anger, but not the expression of anger" [V]. They agree facial movements carry social information.
**3.4 Keltner, D., Sauter, D., Tracy, J., and Cowen, A. (2019). Emotional expression: Advances in basic emotion theory. *Journal of Nonverbal Behavior*, 43, 133-160. doi:10.1007/s10919-019-00293-3.** Read: abstract and search of full text (University of Amsterdam repository). The basic-emotion side: "upwards of 20 emotions are signaled in multimodal and dynamic patterns of expressive behavior" [V], with recognition shaped by context.
**3.5 Supporting studies (abstracts, PubMed):** Elfenbein and Ambady (2002, *Psych Bull* 128:203-235): emotions recognised "at better-than-chance levels" across cultures, with an in-group advantage that shrinks with exposure [V]. Gendron et al. (2014, *Emotion* 14:251-262): Himba participants sorting faces without emotion words did not show the "universal" pattern [V abstract paraphrase]. Crivelli et al. (2016, *PNAS* 113:12403-12407): Trobriand adolescents read the Western "fear" gasping face as anger and threat. Cowen et al. (2021, *Nature* 589:251-257): machine learning on 6 million videos from 144 countries found 16 expression types with context associations "70% preserved across 12 world regions" [V]. Ekman and Friesen (1971) "Constants across cultures in the face and emotion": record only.
**May say:** faces carry emotional information that others read better than chance, including across cultures, but a given expression does not reliably mean one emotion, and context and culture matter. The strength of universality is actively disputed (see (c)).

### 4. Mimicry and power posing

**4.1 Chartrand, T. L., and Bargh, J. A. (1999). The chameleon effect: The perception-behavior link and social interaction. *JPSP*, 76(6), 893-910. doi:10.1037/0022-3514.76.6.893.**
What I read: abstract (PubMed). Experiment 1: people unintentionally matched strangers' motor behaviour (face rubbing, foot shaking). Experiment 2: confederates who mimicked participants' posture and movements produced smoother interactions and more liking. Experiment 3: more empathic people mimicked more. Sample sizes not in the abstract.
**4.2 Hale, J., and Hamilton, A. F. de C. (2016). Testing the relationship between mimicry, trust and rapport in virtual reality conversations. *Scientific Reports*, 6, 35295. doi:10.1038/srep35295. PMC5064448.**
What I read: full text downloaded (PMC); relied on abstract for the summary. Study 1 (N = 50): rapport higher towards avatars that mimicked head and torso movements. Study 2, preregistered (N = 40, European and East Asian participants): no effect of mimicry on rapport, trust or implicit trust behaviour. "We conclude that being mimicked does not always increase rapport or trust" [V].
**4.3 Hale, J., and Hamilton, A. F. de C. (2016). Cognitive mechanisms for responding to mimicry from others. *Neuroscience and Biobehavioral Reviews*, 63, 106-123. doi:10.1016/j.neubiorev.2016.02.006.** Abstract: an in-depth review suggests "the link from mimicry to liking and other positive outcomes may be fragile" [V]; positive responses can break down when mimicry is unexpected.
Other replication evidence: a TEAP 2023 conference abstract reporting a replication of Chartrand and Bargh Study 1 exists (search result only). No large multi-lab replication of Study 2 was found in the time available. **May say:** people often unconsciously copy each other, and being copied sometimes increases liking in lab studies, but the effect is fragile, depends on the situation, and can backfire if noticed. **May not say:** "mirror someone's body language to make them like you" as reliable advice.

**4.4 Carney, D. R., Cuddy, A. J. C., and Yap, A. J. (2010). Power posing: Brief nonverbal displays affect neuroendocrine levels and risk tolerance. *Psychological Science*, 21(10), 1363-1368. doi:10.1177/0956797610383437.** Abstract (PubMed): high-power poses raised testosterone, lowered cortisol, increased felt power and risk-taking; "two simple 1-min poses" [V]. N = 42 (per Ranehill and Simmons).
**4.5 Ranehill, E., Dreber, A., Johannesson, M., Leiberg, S., Sul, S., and Weber, R. A. (2015). Assessing the robustness of power posing: No effect on hormones and risk tolerance in a large sample of men and women. *Psychological Science*, 26(5), 653-656. doi:10.1177/0956797614553946.** Full text (Carney's website). N = 200, experimenter blind, over 95% power: significant effect on self-reported feelings of power, "no significant effect of power posing on hormonal levels or in any of the three behavioral tasks" [V]. Carney, Cuddy and Yap's 2015 reply noted procedural differences (e.g., six-minute poses) (not read).
**4.6 Carney, D. R. (2016). "My position on 'Power Poses'".** Full text (her Berkeley page, live). "I do not believe that “power pose” effects are real." [V] She lists that the sample was tiny, the effects "small and barely there", data were collected in chunks with checks along the way, the self-report measure was "p-hacked", and experimenters knew the hypothesis [V fragments].
**4.7 Simmons, J. P., and Simonsohn, U. (2017). Power posing: P-curving the evidence. *Psychological Science*, 28(5), 687-693.** Full text (Simonsohn's site). The 33 studies cited by Carney et al. 2015 produced a p-value distribution "indistinguishable from what would be expected if (a) the average effect size were zero" [V] and selective reporting explained the significant results.
**4.8 Cuddy, A. J. C., Schultz, S. J., and Fosse, N. E. (2018). P-curving a more comprehensive body of research on postural feedback reveals clear evidential value for power-posing effects. *Psychological Science*, 29(4), 656-666.** Not read beyond title and a secondary account (BPS *The Psychologist*, "A decade of power posing: where do we stand?", read in full): Cuddy et al. added 21 studies and argued for evidential value, especially for felt power. Credé (2019, *Meta-Psychology*) objected that most studies lacked a neutral control.
**4.9 Jonas et al. (2017), CRSP special issue (seven preregistered studies)** and **Gronau et al. (2017)** Bayesian meta-analysis (N = 1,071): as reported by the BPS article, "virtually zero effect" on behaviour and hormones but "it is clear that an effect on felt power was observed" [both quoted from Jonas et al. 2017 via the BPS article; gate].
**4.10 Elkjær, E., Mikkelsen, M. B., Michalak, J., Mennin, D. S., and O'Toole, M. S. (2022; online 2020). Expansive and contractive postures and movement: A systematic review and meta-analysis. *Perspectives on Psychological Science*, 17(1), 276-304. doi:10.1177/1745691620919358.** Abstract (Crossref): 73 studies; effects driven by contractive versus neutral (g = 0.45), not expansive versus neutral (g = 0.06): "it is the absence of contractive displays rather than the presence of expansive displays that alters affective and behavioral responding" [V]. Körner, Röseler and Schütz (2022) commented critically (not read).
**May say:** the hormone and risk-taking claims did not replicate and the first author disowned them; a small effect on how powerful people say they feel has held up; slumping may make people feel worse more than "power poses" make them feel better. **May not say:** that posture changes hormones or that two minutes of posing will change how you perform.

### 5. Culture, gaze and conversational norms

**5.1 Akechi, H., Senju, A., Uibo, H., Kikuchi, Y., Hasegawa, T., and Hietanen, J. K. (2013). Attention to eye contact in the West and East: Autonomic responses and evaluative ratings. *PLoS ONE*, 8(3), e59312. doi:10.1371/journal.pone.0059312.**
What I read: full text. 20 Finnish and 20 Japanese university students (10 women each), groups matched on social anxiety. A live female model of the participant's own culture appeared behind an electronic shutter with direct gaze, averted gaze or closed eyes. Both groups showed heart-rate deceleration to eye contact and rated it more arousing; the physiology was broadly similar. Ratings differed: Japanese participants rated direct-gaze faces as angrier (F(1,38) = 4.19, p = .048, ηp² = .10 for the interaction), less approachable and less pleasant than Finns did. Authors' reading: individuals from an East Asian culture "perceive another’s face as being angrier, unapproachable, and unpleasant when making eye contact" [V], and differences reflect "display rules and cultural norms" [V] rather than physiology. Small samples, one pair of national groups, university students, static live faces, not conversation. **May say:** in one well-designed lab study, Japanese students read the same direct gaze as less friendly than Finnish students did.

**5.2 Hessels, R. S. (2020). How does gaze to faces support face-to-face interaction? A review and perspective. *Psychonomic Bulletin and Review*, 27(5), 856-881. doi:10.3758/s13423-020-01715-w. PMC7547045.**
What I read: full text sections on cross-cultural differences and the framework. East Asian observers looked more at the nose and less at the eyes than Western observers in some face tasks (Blais et al. 2008), but Japanese children looked more at the eyes than British children in another (Senju et al. 2013), and one study found the East Asian difference only when the filmed face gazed directly at the viewer (Gobel et al. 2017). Where one looks on a face depends on task, what the face is doing, and context. Lesson: cultural gaze differences are real but not a simple rule.

**5.3 Rossano, F., Brown, P., and Levinson, S. C. (2009). Gaze, questioning and culture. In J. Sidnell (Ed.), *Conversation Analysis: Comparative Perspectives* (pp. 187-249). Cambridge University Press.**
What I read: author copy from pure.mpg.de; scanned, so I OCR'd pp. 187-191 and the conclusions pp. 236-241. Design: about 300 question-answer pairs from each of three unrelated speech communities (northern Italian; Yélî Dnye, Rossel Island, Papua New Guinea; Tzeltal, Mexico), roughly ten dyads per community, informal natural conversation. Findings: in all three, the *questioner* was more likely to be looking than the recipient, contrary to the standard claim that listeners look more; gaze was mostly fixed through the turn, so it did not act as a turn-transition cue. Recipient gaze differed most: Tzeltal recipients looked in 42.3% of questions (speakers 65.7%), and Tzeltal uses verbal repetition responses to show attention instead. The data "strongly challenges the view that looking at the speaker’s face would always be the default gaze behavior for a recipient" [V, OCR]. **May say:** whether a listener shows attention by looking varies between cultures; in some communities listeners show it in words rather than with their eyes.

**5.4 Stivers, T., et al. (2009). Universals and cultural variation in turn-taking in conversation. *PNAS*, 106(26), 10587-10592. doi:10.1073/pnas.0903616106. PMC2705608.**
What I read: full text (PMC), searched. Ten languages; all avoid overlap and minimise silence; mean response offset +208 ms; Danish slowest (+469 ms), Japanese fastest (+7 ms), language means within about 250 ms of the overall mean. Italian, reputedly overlap-tolerant, averaged +310 ms with only 17% overlapping responses. Questions asked with the questioner's gaze on the recipient got faster answers, significant in 5 of 10 languages; Danish trended the other way. Authors: "local variations are quantitative only" [V], and small tempo differences may explain why people perceive "dramatic" differences in style. (Part A may also cover this paper.) **May say:** cultures differ in turn timing by fractions of a second, which is enough to feel rude or slow to an outsider.

**5.5 Hall, E. T. (1976). *Beyond Culture*.** Not read. **Kittler, M. G., Rygl, D., and Mackinnon, A. (2011). Beyond culture or beyond control? Reviewing the use of Hall's high-/low-context concept. *International Journal of Cross Cultural Management*, 11(1), 63-82. doi:10.1177/1470595811398797.** Abstract (Crossref): systematic review; most research using high/low-context country classifications "is based on seemingly less-than-adequate evidence" [V]; conventional classifications "are flawed or, at best, very limited" [V]. **Cardon, P. W. (2008). A critique of Hall's contexting model: A meta-analysis of literature on intercultural business and technical communication. *Journal of Business and Technical Communication*, 22(4), 399-428. doi:10.1177/1050651908320361.** Abstract: 224 articles, 1990-2006; contexting is the most cited framework, few propositions tested, and tests "have failed to support many contexting propositions, particularly those related to directness" [V]; an "unsubstantiated and underdeveloped communication theory" [V].
**5.6 Hofstede and its critics.** McSweeney, B. (2002). Hofstede's model of national cultural differences and their consequences: A triumph of faith, a failure of analysis. *Human Relations*, 55(1), 89-118. doi:10.1177/0018726702551004. Abstract only: questions "the plausibility of systematically causal national cultures" [V]. Fischer, R., and Schwartz, S. H. (2011). Whence differences in value priorities? Individual, cultural, or artifactual sources. *Journal of Cross-Cultural Psychology*, 42(7), 1127-1144. doi:10.1177/0022022110381429. Abstract: three datasets (N = 41,968; 42,359; 84,887); "more consensus than disagreement on value priorities across countries, refuting strong claims that culture determines values" [V]; only conformity values looked like shared national meaning. Hofstede's own work not read.
**5.7 Gudykunst's anxiety/uncertainty management (AUM) theory.** Read: Griffin, E., *A First Look at Communication Theory*, archived chapter 30 on AUM (afirstlook.com, full text). AUM holds that effective communication with "strangers" needs anxiety and uncertainty kept between minimum and maximum thresholds, managed by mindfulness; 47 axioms. Griffin's critique: there is no procedure yet to measure mindfulness or the thresholds; he quotes Gudykunst: "A method for assessing our minimum and maximum thresholds for anxiety and uncertainty has not been developed" [V, as quoted by Griffin]. Ting-Toomey questioned whether uncertainty reduction reflects a Western bias. Gudykunst's primary books not read. Griffin also records Gudykunst's warning that strangers "tend to overestimate the effect of cultural identity" on others' behaviour (Griffin's paraphrase) which fits Fischer and Schwartz.
**May say:** cultures differ in conversational habits (gaze, pace, directness), but country labels like "high-context" or Hofstede scores are weak guides to the individual in front of you; ask and observe rather than predict from nationality.

### 6. Autism, eye contact and the double empathy problem

**6.1 Milton, D. E. M. (2012). On the ontological status of autism: The 'double empathy problem'. *Disability and Society*, 27(6), 883-887. doi:10.1080/09687599.2012.710008.**
What I read: full text (Kent Academic Repository, publisher PDF). A conceptual paper, not an empirical study. Defines the problem as "a disjuncture in reciprocity between two differently disposed social actors" [V]; it is "a 'double problem' as both people experience it, and so it is not a singular problem located in any one person" [V]. Argues against the "lack of theory of mind" framing.
**6.2 Crompton, C. J., Ropar, D., Evans-Williams, C. V. M., Flynn, E. G., and Fletcher-Watson, S. (2020). Autistic peer-to-peer information transfer is highly effective. *Autism*, 24(7), 1704-1712. doi:10.1177/1362361320919286. PMC7545656.**
What I read: full text (PMC). N = 72 adults in nine eight-person diffusion chains (three all-autistic, three all-non-autistic, three mixed and alternating, each mixed chain starting with a non-autistic person); a 30-point story passed along the chain. Autistic and non-autistic chains recalled similar numbers of details (12.96 vs 12.40); mixed chains fewer (8.92); rapport lower in mixed chains. "Information transfer selectively degrades more quickly in mixed pairs, in parallel with a reduction in rapport" [V]. Limitations named by the authors: modest sample, participants knew chain type, mixed chains always began with a non-autistic person, rapport Bayes factors modest; "replications are warranted" [V].
**6.3 Crompton, C. J., Foster, S. J., Wilks, C. E. H., Dodd, M., Efthimiou, T. N., Ropar, D., Sasson, N. J., Lages, M., and Fletcher-Watson, S. (2025). Information transfer within and between autistic and non-autistic people. *Nature Human Behaviour*, 9, 1488-1500. doi:10.1038/s41562-025-02163-z.**
What I read: abstract and opening pages of the full text (Nature PDF). Registered Report, N = 311, replicating and extending 6.2. "We found no difference in information transfer between single-neurotype and mixed-neurotype chains" [V]; non-autistic chains reported higher rapport, and disclosing diagnosis improved rapport; the authors note this "contrasts with prior findings" [V]. **Important:** a lesson must not cite the 2020 study alone as settled; the larger preregistered replication found autistic people shared information as well as others but did not reproduce the mixed-chain drop.
**6.4 Trevisan, D. A., Roberts, N., Lin, C., and Birmingham, E. (2017). How do adults and teens with self-declared autism spectrum disorder experience eye contact? A qualitative analysis of first-hand accounts. *PLoS ONE*, 12(11), e0188446. doi:10.1371/journal.pone.0188446. PMC5705114.**
What I read: full text sections (PMC). 698 "meaning units" from 364 people (10 YouTube videos; 623 units from 354 WrongPlanet forum users), self-declared diagnosis. Themes: adverse emotional and physiological reactions, feeling invaded, sensory overload, and in conversation "an inability to listen to another person while making eye contact at the same time" (authors' summary of posts) [V]. **May say:** many autistic people report that making eye contact makes it harder to listen, so looking away is not a sign of inattention; "good listeners make eye contact" is a cultural and neurotypical expectation, not a test of listening.

### 7. Listening myths in the business literature

**7.1 Zenger, J., and Folkman, J. (2016, 14 July). What great listeners actually do. *Harvard Business Review* (online).**
What I read: full text from the Internet Archive capture of 20 July 2016 (hbr.org itself is paywalled). Data: 360-degree assessments of 3,492 participants in a programme to help managers become better coaches; the top 5% rated as listeners were compared with everyone else on the 20 items with the largest differences. Findings: "Good listening is much more than being silent while the other person talks" [V]; the best listeners ask questions that promote insight, build the other's self-esteem, keep the exchange cooperative rather than competitive, and make suggestions. Metaphor: a trampoline, not a sponge. They name as the common but incomplete view: not interrupting, showing you're listening with facial expressions and "mm-hmm", and repeating back "practically word-for-word" [V].
Weaknesses: not peer-reviewed; the method is described in one paragraph; ratings are colleagues' perceptions of managers in a coaching programme, not measures of understanding; comparing top 5% with the rest on items already selected for large differences invites circularity; no effect sizes. And the article itself repeats a myth: "It is estimated that 80% of what we communicate comes from these signals" [V] (nonverbal cues, with no source), and lists "perspiration, respiration rates" as cues to observe. **May say:** a large set of workplace ratings suggests people experience good listeners as active and engaged, not silent. **May not say** it "proves" what good listening is.

### (a) What this part says the field considers essential

1. Nonverbal signals matter chiefly for feeling and relationship, not for content. Mehrabian's own limit (feelings and attitudes only) is the key fact about the 93% claim, and experts in nonverbal communication and deception research treat "93% nonverbal" as a misreading.
2. Lie detection from demeanour is barely above chance (about 54%), experts are no better, and individual "wizards" are not supported by the individual-differences meta-analysis. The fault is mainly weak cues, not bad observers. Gaze aversion is the world's most common lie belief and one of the best-established non-cues.
3. The field's positive advice for detecting deception has shifted from watching bodies to listening to content: verbal cues and information-gathering questioning outperform nonverbal cue reading (Vrij et al. 2019; Hauch et al.; Levine's TDT).
4. Faces and bodies do carry information (thin slices r ≈ .39; emotion recognition above chance across cultures), but no gesture has a fixed meaning (Harrigan via Denault), and context changes what a face means.
5. Culture shapes gaze and pace, but the variation is quantitative and within-country variation is large. Frameworks that sort nations into types (high/low context, Hofstede scores) have weak evidence as guides to individuals.
6. Headline effects in popular body-language advice (power posing, mimicry-for-liking) have had serious replication trouble; the surviving effects are smaller and narrower.
7. Communication difficulty can belong to the pairing, not one person (double empathy), and eye contact is not a valid test of listening.

### (b) Common misconceptions (each named by a source)

- **"93% (or 80%) of communication is nonverbal" / "7% words, 38% tone, 55% body".** Named by Mehrabian himself (kaaj.com: equations "not applicable" unless feelings or attitudes are the topic); Lapakko 1997 (ERIC abstract) and Lapakko 2007 (title: "an urban legend"). Zenger and Folkman 2016 illustrate the myth ("80%").
- **"Liars avoid eye contact" / "you can tell a liar by their eyes."** Named by the Global Deception Research Team 2006 (pan-cultural stereotype, 75 countries); DePaulo et al. 2003 (d = 0.01 for eye contact); Denault et al. 2020 ("a widespread misconception"); Luke et al. 2023 (the one consensus item); Vrij et al. 2019 (stereotypes hard to debunk).
- **"Looking up and to the right means lying" (NLP eye-accessing cues).** Named and tested by Wiseman et al. 2012; Vrij et al. 2019.
- **"Trained professionals, police or 'body language experts' can spot liars."** Bond and DePaulo 2006 (experts d = -.025 vs lay); Bond and DePaulo 2008 (ability differences minute); GAO 2013 on SPOT; Jordan et al. 2019 (METT training no better than bogus training); Denault et al. 2020 (BAI, synergology).
- **"Microexpressions reveal hidden lies."** Vrij et al. 2019; Porter and ten Brinke 2008 (rare, 2% of expressions, also in truthful ones).
- **"Body language never lies" / "crossed arms means defensive."** Denault et al. 2020 names the first slogan; the Harrigan (2005) statement quoted there (no body movement has invariant meaning) is the scholarly answer to the second. I found no study that specifically tests crossed arms; a lesson should say "no gesture has a fixed meaning" rather than cite a crossed-arms study.
- **"A person's face shows exactly what they feel."** Barrett et al. 2019 (the "common view").
- **"Mirror their body language and they'll like you."** Hale and Hamilton 2016 (preregistered null; review calls the link "fragile").
- **"Power poses boost your hormones and confidence."** Carney 2016 statement; Ranehill et al. 2015; Simmons and Simonsohn 2017.
- **"Good listeners stay quiet, nod and repeat back."** Zenger and Folkman 2016 name this as the common view and argue against it (weak data; see 7.1). "Active listening = repeating back" was not investigated by me beyond this; Weger et al. (2010, 2014) studied paraphrasing (unread; likely Part B).
- **"Good listeners make eye contact."** Contradicted for autistic listeners by Trevisan et al. 2017's first-hand accounts and for Tzeltal recipients by Rossano et al. 2009; Akechi et al. 2013 show direct gaze reads as less friendly to Japanese viewers. No source I read names this exact slogan as a myth; say it as an inference.
- **"People from high-context cultures are indirect" (national typing).** Cardon 2008; Kittler et al. 2011.

### (c) Contested questions

**C1. How much can nonverbal behaviour, especially the face, tell us about feelings? (universality of facial expressions)**
- *Basic emotion theory* (Ekman; Keltner, Sauter, Tracy, Cowen 2019): a set of emotions (now "upwards of 20") have distinctive, cross-culturally recognised multimodal expressions rooted in evolution. Best case: above-chance recognition across cultures (Elfenbein and Ambady 2002); Cowen et al. 2021's 6 million videos from 144 countries showing expressions tied to similar contexts, "70% preserved".
- *Constructionist / behavioural ecology view* (Barrett et al. 2019; Russell; Fernández-Dols; Crivelli): facial movements are variable, context-dependent social signals; the "common view" that one configuration reliably signals one emotion is not supported. Best case: expected configurations appear in only about 19% of emotional events and correlate about .32 with emotion measures; remote-culture studies without emotion words (Gendron 2014, Himba; Crivelli 2016, Trobriand) break the "universal" pattern.
- Common ground (stated in Barrett et al. and Keltner et al.): faces carry social information and context matters. The dispute is how much and how specifically.
- What would settle it: large naturalistic studies measuring what people feel and what their faces do at the same moment, across many small-scale societies, with free-response rather than word-matching methods.
- Classification: **contested empirical.**

**C2. Is lie detection from behaviour ever reliable?**
- *Mainstream deception research* (Bond and DePaulo; Vrij, Hartwig, Granhag; Luke): nonverbal cues are "faint and unreliable"; accuracy about 54%; behavioural screening (SPOT) unsupported. Best case: 206 documents, 24,483 judges, tiny cross-study spread; experts no better; expert survey consensus on gaze.
- *Verbal and interview-based approaches* (Vrij; Granhag and Hartwig's Strategic Use of Evidence; Levine's truth-default theory): detection improves when interviewers elicit and check content, use evidence strategically, or rely on context; Levine reports high accuracy with expert questioning (not read). Best case: Hauch et al. training meta-analysis (content-cue training beats nonverbal); Vrij et al.'s verbal-cue average d = 0.30.
- *Practitioner and Ekman-school view* (Ekman; SPOT designers; BAI/Reid; synergology): trained observers can read emotional leakage and microexpressions. Best case offered: Porter and ten Brinke's finding that emotional leakage occurred in every participant at least once. Against: microexpressions rare and in truth tellers too; METT training null; GAO findings.
- What would settle it: preregistered high-stakes field studies with ground truth (like the televised-appeals archive), comparing trained and untrained judges and questioning methods.
- Classification: for demeanour-only detection, close to **established** (poor); for interview-based methods, **contested empirical.**

**C3. How culture-bound are eye contact and turn-taking norms?**
- *Universals view* (Stivers et al. 2009; Rossano et al. 2009 on speaker gaze; Akechi et al. on physiology): the underlying system is shared; differences are quantitative (turn gaps within about 250 ms; questioner gaze similar across cultures; similar physiological orienting to eye contact).
- *Cultural variation view* (ethnographic tradition; Hall; Rossano et al. on recipient gaze; Akechi et al. on ratings): the *meaning* and *expected amount* of gaze differ (Tzeltal recipients rarely look; Japanese viewers read direct gaze as angrier), and small timing differences are felt as large.
- *Critique of national typologies* (Kittler et al.; Cardon; McSweeney; Fischer and Schwartz): even where differences exist, nation-level labels predict individuals poorly.
- What would settle it: comparative corpora of natural conversation across many communities, coded for both behaviour and participants' interpretations, with within-country variance reported.
- Classification: **contested empirical** (degree), with a **value** element in how far a learner should adapt to others' norms.

**C4. Is mirroring helpful?**
- *Pro* (Chartrand and Bargh 1999; Lakin and Chartrand; sales and therapy applications): nonconscious mimicry smooths interaction and increases liking.
- *Sceptical* (Hale and Hamilton 2016 review and preregistered null): the mimicry-liking link is fragile, depends on who and what context, and can backfire when detected.
- What would settle it: multi-lab preregistered replications of Chartrand and Bargh Study 2 with natural interactions and measures of whether mimicry was noticed.
- Classification: **contested empirical.** The practical advice "deliberately mirror people" has no good support.

**C5 (brief). Does changing posture change how you feel? (power posing)** Carney, Ranehill, Simmons and Simonsohn: no hormonal or behavioural effect. Cuddy et al. 2018, Gronau et al. 2017: a real effect on felt power. Elkjær et al. 2022: the effect runs through avoiding a slumped posture rather than adopting an expansive one. **Contested empirical** for felt power; **established null** for hormones.

**C6 (brief). Is "communication difficulty" in autism a deficit of one person or a mismatch between two?** Milton 2012 and Crompton 2020 (mismatch) versus the 2025 registered replication (no mismatch effect on information transfer, rapport lower in some mixed settings) and critics of double empathy noted in the literature (not read). **Contested empirical**, with a **value** dimension about how autism is described.

### (d) Gates: claims a drafter must re-check against a primary text before a lesson states them

1. Every design detail of Mehrabian and Wiener 1967 and Mehrabian and Ferris 1967 (words used, number and sex of speakers and listeners, how 7/38/55 was computed). I did not obtain either paper. Check the papers or *Silent Messages* pp. 75-80.
2. Any quotation attributed to Mehrabian other than the two sentences on kaaj.com (for example "absurd ... 7%").
3. Lapakko 2007's counts (79 sites, 4 questioned, 1 correct) and its page range.
4. Aamodt and Custer 2006 figures (108 studies, 16,537 participants, 55.51% vs 54.22%).
5. GAO-14-159 wording (my text is [T]); and the GAO 2017 report (GAO-17-608R) conclusion as cited by Vrij et al. and Denault et al.
6. Jordan et al. 2019 results beyond the abstract (accuracy figures, Bayes factors).
7. Jonas et al. 2017 and Gronau et al. 2017 quotations (seen only via the BPS article).
8. Cuddy et al. 2018's claims (read only in secondary summary).
9. Bond and DePaulo 2006 exact wording (OCR scan): quote only after checking the publisher PDF.
10. Ambady and Rosenthal 1993 "half a minute" teacher study details.
11. Chartrand and Bargh 1999 sample sizes and effect sizes (abstract only).
12. The claim that Porter and ten Brinke 2008 is the only experiment on microexpressions and deception was true as of Vrij et al. 2019; check for later studies before repeating it.
13. Harrigan (2005) quotation, taken from Denault et al.; check the original (Harrigan, Rosenthal and Scherer, *The New Handbook of Methods in Nonverbal Behavior Research*, p. 139).
14. The status of the double empathy findings after the 2025 registered replication; check any 2025-2026 follow-ups.

### (e) Best free resources to link (all checked live today, HTTP 200 unless noted)

- http://www.kaaj.com/psych/smorder.html : Mehrabian's own statement that 7-38-55 applies only to feelings and attitudes.
- https://journals.plos.org/plosone/article?id=10.1371/journal.pone.0040259 : Wiseman et al. 2012, short readable test of the NLP eye-movement lie cue.
- https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC2957901/ : "A world of lies", the 58- and 75-country survey of lie beliefs.
- https://researchportal.port.ac.uk/en/publications/reading-lies-nonverbal-communication-and-deception : Vrij, Hartwig and Granhag 2019 review record (full text was read from a mirror; the Annual Reviews version may be paywalled).
- https://journals.copmadrid.org/apj/art/apj2019a9 : Denault et al. 2020, open-access account of SPOT, BAI and synergology.
- https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC6640856/ : Barrett et al. 2019, the long review on reading emotion from faces.
- https://faculty.haas.berkeley.edu/dana_carney/pdf_My%20position%20on%20power%20poses.pdf : Carney's statement, a model of a scientist changing her mind.
- https://www.bps.org.uk/psychologist/decade-power-posing-where-do-we-stand : balanced magazine history of the power-posing debate.
- https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC5064448/ : Hale and Hamilton 2016, preregistered mimicry test.
- https://journals.plos.org/plosone/article?id=10.1371/journal.pone.0059312 : Akechi et al. 2013, Japanese and Finnish responses to eye contact.
- https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC2705608/ : Stivers et al. 2009, turn timing across ten languages.
- https://kar.kent.ac.uk/62639/1/Double%20empathy%20problem.pdf : Milton 2012, the double empathy problem, five pages.
- https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC7545656/ and https://www.nature.com/articles/s41562-025-02163-z : Crompton 2020 and its 2025 registered replication, best read together.
- https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC5705114/ : Trevisan et al. 2017, autistic people's own accounts of eye contact.
- (403 to curl, open access in a browser) https://www.tandfonline.com/doi/full/10.1080/1068316X.2023.2292504 : Luke et al. 2023 expert survey on deception cues.

### (f) Not read

Paywalled or blocked: the two Mehrabian 1967 papers; *Silent Messages* (Internet Archive lending-only); Lapakko 1997 (ERIC abstract only) and 2007 (Cloudflare); Trimboli and Walker 1987; the Oxford Reference entry; GAO-14-159 and GAO-17-608R PDFs (Akamai "Access Denied"; product page via WebFetch only); Aamodt and Custer 2006; Jordan et al. 2019 full text (Cloudflare); Ambady and Rosenthal 1993; Cuddy et al. 2018; Carney et al. 2015 reply; Körner et al. 2022; Jonas et al. 2017; Gronau et al. 2017; Credé 2019; McSweeney 2002 full text; Hall 1976; Hofstede's books; Gudykunst's primary works; Lilienfeld et al. *50 Great Myths* (only learned its Myth #23 is the polygraph; did not confirm any body-language myth in it); Luke 2019 "Lessons from Pinocchio"; Mann et al. 2012 NLP study; Levine's empirical papers on expert questioning; Weger et al. 2010 and 2014 on paraphrasing; Ekman and Friesen 1971; Chartrand and Bargh 1999 full text; Argyle et al. 1970; critiques of the double empathy account.

UNREAD: Mehrabian and Wiener 1967, Mehrabian and Ferris 1967, Silent Messages, Lapakko 1997 full text, Lapakko 2007, Trimboli and Walker 1987, Oxford Reference Mehrabian entry, GAO-14-159 full report, GAO-17-608R, Aamodt and Custer 2006, Jordan et al. 2019 full text, Ambady and Rosenthal 1993, Cuddy Schultz Fosse 2018, Carney Cuddy Yap 2015 reply, Korner Roseler Schutz 2022, Jonas et al. 2017 CRSP, Gronau et al. 2017, Crede 2019, McSweeney 2002 full text, Hall Beyond Culture 1976, Hofstede Culture's Consequences, Gudykunst primary works, 50 Great Myths of Popular Psychology, Luke 2019 Lessons from Pinocchio, Mann et al. 2012 NLP, Levine expert questioning studies, Weger et al. 2010, Weger et al. 2014, Ekman and Friesen 1971, Chartrand and Bargh 1999 full text, Argyle et al. 1970, Harrigan 2005, Hartwig and Bond 2011 full text, Bond and DePaulo 2008 full text, Porter and ten Brinke 2008 full text, Elkjaer et al. 2022 full text, Elfenbein and Ambady 2002 full text, Gendron et al. 2014 full text, Crivelli et al. 2016 full text, Cowen et al. 2021 full text, Hauch et al. 2016 full text, Levine 2014 TDT full text, Kittler Rygl Mackinnon 2011 full text, Cardon 2008 full text, Fischer and Schwartz 2011 full text, Carney Cuddy Yap 2010 full text, Hale and Hamilton 2016 review full text
