## Part E: What practitioners say matters, safety-relevant guidance, and talking across difference

This part records what people who listen for a living teach, in their own published words: crisis
and suicide-prevention services (Samaritans, 988 Lifeline and #BeThe1To, R U OK?, Mental Health
First Aid, NHS), clinical communication (Calgary-Cambridge, the agenda-setting and interruption
studies, Heritage's "some/any" experiment, VitalTalk, AHRQ teach-back), motivational interviewing
practice (MINT, the MITI coding manual), negotiators and mediators (FBI Behavioral Change Stairway
Model via Vecchi et al. 2005, Harvard Program on Negotiation, Acas), interviewers (Oral History
Association, Smithsonian Institution Archives, Poynter, StoryCorps), dialogue across difference
(Braver Angels and its randomised evaluation, Essential Partners, conversational receptiveness, deep
canvassing and the LaCour retraction), talk with older adults (elderspeak), people with hearing
loss or a stammer, talk through an interpreter, and a short note on listening at work (Project
Aristotle, psychological safety, the collective intelligence dispute). All read on 2026-09-26.
Method: pages and PDFs fetched with curl (generic research User-Agent) and converted with pandoc or
pdftotext; abstracts pulled from PubMed E-utilities, Europe PMC and Crossref; full texts read where
free (PMC, publisher open pages, authors' copies). Raw extracts are in `cl/E/`. Quotations marked
[V] were copied from text I extracted myself; nothing in this part is marked [T], because the one
WebFetch attempt (Google re:Work) failed and I re-got that page with curl. Search-snippet-only
material is paraphrased and labelled.

---

### 1. Crisis and suicide-prevention guidance for lay helpers

These are the organisations a Foundation course will send a learner to. Their advice to ordinary
people is strikingly consistent, and the consistency is itself a teachable finding.

**1.1 Samaritans (UK and Ireland), "How to support someone you're worried about"**
URL: https://www.samaritans.org/how-we-can-help/if-youre-worried-about-someone-else/how-support-someone-youre-worried-about/what-do-if-you-think-someone-struggling/ (read in full, section 2 of 6; sections 1 and 4 also read).
- Frames listening as withholding three things: advice, matching their experience with your own,
  and problem-solving. Paraphrase of: listening "could mean not offering advice, not trying to
  identify what they're going through with your own experiences and not trying to solve their
  problems." [V] (22 words, verbatim.)
- **SHUSH** is the acronym, five headings [V]: "Show you care", "Have patience", "Use open
  questions", "Say it back", "Have courage".
  - Show you care: attention, eye contact, "put away your phone" [V]; suggests a week-long
    "listening diary" recording one thing learned about the other person each time.
  - Have patience: if the person pauses, "wait a moment" [V].
  - Use open questions: gives a worked contrast. Instead of "have you had a bad week?" try "what's
    been going on for you this week?"; instead of "are you OK?" try "how are you feeling today?"
    and follow with "tell me more..." [V]. Warns against questions that lead to a particular answer.
  - Say it back: "Check you've understood, but don't interrupt or offer a solution." [V]
  - Have courage: "remember, you don't have to fill every silence." [V]
- On suicide: "If you're worried someone is suicidal, it's okay to ask them directly." [V] The page
  says research shows asking helps because it gives permission to talk and shows they are not a
  burden. The companion page (supporting-someone-suicidal-thoughts) says "Evidence shows asking
  someone if they're suicidal can protect them." [V] Note for drafters: "can protect" is a stronger
  claim than the reviews in 1.6 support (they show no harm and small reductions in ideation in
  research settings, not protection against suicide). Quote Samaritans as Samaritans, and state the
  evidence separately.
- "When you should let someone else help" (section 4): immediate danger, call 999; GP; specialist
  services; Samaritans free on **116 123**, "open 24/7, 365 days a year" [V]; Samaritans will call
  an ambulance on your behalf or call the person for you.
- Access: the "Tips for having a difficult conversation" URL I tried returned 404; not read.

**1.2 988 Suicide & Crisis Lifeline (US), "Help Someone Else"**
URL: https://988lifeline.org/help-someone-else/ (read in full).
- Do's and don'ts list, verbatim items [V]: "Be direct. Talk openly and matter-of-factly about
  suicide." "Be willing to listen. Allow expressions of feelings. Accept the feelings." "Don't
  debate whether suicide is right or wrong, or whether feelings are good or bad. Don't lecture on
  the value of life." "Don't act shocked. This will put distance between you." "Don't be sworn to
  secrecy. Seek support." "Offer hope that alternatives are available but do not offer glib
  reassurance." "Take action. Remove means, like weapons or pills."
- "Never keep it a secret if a friend tells you about a plan to hurt themselves." [V]
- Five Action Steps, as listed on this page [V]: "Ask. Be there. Help keep them safe. Help them
  connect. Follow up." (The brief's wording "Be there / Keep them safe" should be corrected to the
  service's own: "Be there" and "Help keep them safe" are separate steps.)
- "Practice Active Listening" block with four headings [V]: "Acknowledge the Speaker" (a nod or
  "Uh huh"), "Respond Verbally" ("Be sure to let the speaker finish talking before asking any
  questions"), "Summarize What You Hear" (suggested phrases "what I'm hearing is..." and "sounds
  like you're saying...."), "Look the Part" (eye contact, posture, focus).
- Numbers as the service states them: "Call, text, or chat with a 988 Lifeline counselor" [V],
  "available 24/7/365" [V] (from https://988lifeline.org/).

**1.3 #BeThe1To, "How and Why the 5 Steps Can Help"**
URL: https://www.bethe1to.com/bethe1to-steps-evidence/ (read in full). Run by Vibrant, the 988
administrator.
- Ask: the suggested wording is "Are you thinking about suicide?" [V]; also "How do you hurt?" and
  "How can I help?" [V]. "Do not ever promise to keep their thoughts of suicide a secret." [V]
  "The flip side of the 'Ask' step is to 'Listen.'" [V] Advises listening for reasons to live and
  not imposing your own reasons on them.
- Why ask: "Studies show that asking at-risk individuals if they are suicidal does not increase
  suicides or suicidal thoughts." [V] (No citation on the page. The wording that follows closely
  echoes Dazzi et al. 2014's abstract.)
- Be there: "do not commit to anything you are not willing or able to accomplish." [V] Rationale
  cites Joiner's interpersonal theory and Klonsky and May's three-step theory (not read).
- Help keep them safe: asks about prior action, method, plan, timing and access; cites
  means-restriction evidence via Harvard T.H. Chan School and the finding that method substitution
  "frequently does not happen" [V].
- Help them connect: safety plan, professional supports; cites an evaluation of ASIST-trained
  Lifeline counsellors (Gould et al., not read) linking "listening without judgment" [V] with better
  caller outcomes.
- Follow up: caring contacts (e.g. postcards) as evidence-based; cites post-discharge follow-up
  studies (not read).
- For a Foundation course: step 3 (asking about method, timing and access) moves beyond
  conversation skill into risk assessment. A lesson may name the steps and send the learner to the
  service; it should not rehearse the risk-questioning script as if the course trained it.

**1.4 R U OK? (Australia), "How to ask R U OK?"**
URL: https://www.ruok.org.au/how-to-ask (read in full).
- Readiness check before asking, verbatim items [V]: "Am I in a good headspace?" "Am I willing to
  genuinely listen?" "Can I give as much time as needed?" "Do I understand that I can't 'fix'
  someone's problems?" "Do I accept that they might not be ready to talk? Or they might not want to
  talk to me?" Choose somewhere "relatively private and comfy" [V].
- Four steps (ALEC is the page's own heading for them) [V]: "1. ASK R U OK?", "2. LISTEN WITH AN
  OPEN MIND", "3. ENCOURAGE ACTION", "4. CHECK IN".
  - Ask: name specific changes: "You seem less chatty than usual. How are you going?" [V] If they
    don't want to talk, "don't criticise them" and "Avoid a confrontation." [V]
  - Listen: "Take what they say seriously and don't interrupt or rush the conversation." "If they
    need time to think, sit patiently with the silence." Show you've listened "by repeating back
    what you've heard (in your own words) and ask if you have understood them properly." [V]
  - Encourage action: ask what worked before, "How would you like me to support you?" [V]; if
    "really down for more than 2 weeks" [V], encourage a health professional.
  - Check in: a diary reminder in "a couple of weeks" [V], sooner if struggling.
- Crisis line named on the page: Lifeline; "If life is in danger, call 000" [V]. Lifeline's own site
  gives **13 11 14** and a text number, 0477 13 11 14 [V] (https://www.lifeline.org.au/).

**1.5 Mental Health First Aid (US, National Council for Mental Wellbeing), ALGEE**
URL: https://mentalhealthfirstaid.org/news/algee-how-mhfa-helps-you-respond-in-crisis-and-non-crisis-situations/ (dated September 24, 2025; read in full).
- Steps verbatim [V]: "A – Approach, assess for risk of suicide or harm." "L – Listen
  nonjudgmentally." "G – Give reassurance and information." "E – Encourage appropriate
  professional help." "E – Encourage self-help and other support strategies."
- Under L: "let the person share without interrupting them"; "Try to be accepting, even if you don't
  agree with what they are saying." [V]
- The steps "can be used in any order for a non-crisis situation" and "you don't even have to use
  every single step" [V].
- Scope statement worth quoting in a lesson: "your role is not to diagnose someone or solve the
  problem, but instead to provide support and information." [V]
- Wording differs across national MHFA bodies (commercial UK and Australian course pages found in
  search use "Approach, assess and assist with any crisis" and "Give support and information";
  search snippets only). A lesson should cite one named version.
- Evidence (abstract only): Morgan, Ross and Reavley 2018, "Systematic review and meta-analysis of
  Mental Health First Aid training: Effects on knowledge, stigma, and helping behaviour," PLoS One
  13(5):e0197102, doi:10.1371/journal.pone.0197102. 18 trials, 5,936 participants. Knowledge
  d 0.31 to 0.72; confidence d 0.21 to 0.58; amount of help actually provided at follow-up small
  (d = 0.23); "changes in the quality of behaviours offered were unclear" [V]. Authors declare
  collaboration with MHFA's founders. May say: the training reliably raises knowledge and
  confidence; evidence that it improves the quality of real help is thin.

**1.6 Does asking about suicide put the idea in someone's head? The evidence**
- **Dazzi, Gribble, Wessely and Fear 2014.** "Does asking about suicide and related behaviours
  induce suicidal ideation? What is the evidence?" *Psychological Medicine* 44(16):3361 to 3363.
  doi:10.1017/S0033291714001299. Read: full text (open on Cambridge Core). Design: narrative
  literature review (PsycINFO, Ovid Medline) finding 13 papers, 2001 to 2013. Finding: "None found a
  statistically significant increase in suicidal ideation among participants asked about suicidal
  thoughts." [V] Includes Gould et al. 2005 RCT; Biddle et al. 2013 qualitative (57% of 63
  improved on emotional state after interview, 22% worsened, mostly from being reminded of
  difficulties); Reynolds et al. 2006 (borderline personality disorder: 16.4% of sessions showed an
  increase, 17.5% a decrease). Context: the paper's concern is research ethics committees and
  clinical assessment. It is a short review, not a formal systematic review with pooled estimates.
- **Gould et al. 2005.** "Evaluating iatrogenic risk of youth suicide screening programs: a
  randomized controlled trial." *JAMA* 293(13):1635 to 1643. doi:10.1001/jama.293.13.1635.
  Abstract only. 2,342 students, 6 New York high schools, classes randomised. Suicidal ideation
  after survey 4.7% asked vs 3.9% not asked (P = .49); depressed students and prior attempters who
  were asked appeared less distressed (P = .01) and less suicidal (P = .02).
- **Blades, Stritzke, Page and Brown 2018.** "The benefits and risks of asking research
  participants about suicide: A meta-analysis of the impact of exposure to suicide-related content."
  *Clinical Psychology Review* 64:1 to 12. doi:10.1016/j.cpr.2018.07.001. Abstract only. 18
  studies, 2000 to 2017. Exposure led to small reductions in ideation (g = -0.13) and lower odds of
  suicidal behaviour (OR = 0.714); larger reduction in adolescents.
- **DeCou and Schumann 2018.** "On the Iatrogenic Risk of Assessing Suicidality: A Meta-Analysis."
  *Suicide and Life-Threatening Behavior* 48(5):531 to 543. doi:10.1111/sltb.12368. Abstract only.
  13 prospective studies; no significant iatrogenic effect.
- **Polihronis, Cloutier, Kaur, Skinner and Cappelli 2022** (online 2020). "What's the harm in
  asking? A systematic review and meta-analysis on the risks of asking about suicide-related
  behaviors and self-harm with quality appraisal." *Archives of Suicide Research* 26(2):325 to 347.
  doi:10.1080/13811118.2020.1793857. Abstract only. 17 studies rated, 8 meta-analysed; no
  significant effects of asking on suicide-related behaviour, self-injury or distress. Caveat in
  their words: "more RCTs with a low risk of bias are required to firmly conclude that asking
  through self-report and interview methods does not further exacerbate distress" [V].
- **McGuire, Castro-Ramirez and Nock 2026.** "Iatrogenic effect of anonymous suicide assessment
  among adolescents." *Psychiatry Research* 359:117042. doi:10.1016/j.psychres.2026.117042.
  Abstract only. Experiment, 700 US adolescents, anonymous online survey (the one setting where
  harm had been hypothesised, because nobody follows up). Equivalence supported for desire to
  self-injure (d = -0.04), desire to die (d = 0.03) and mood; the same held among those with prior
  suicidal thoughts or behaviours.
- **What a lesson may say:** across randomised trials, cohort studies and at least four reviews or
  meta-analyses, being asked about suicide in research, screening and clinical settings has not been
  found to increase suicidal thinking, and some studies find small reductions. **What it may not
  say:** that the same has been measured in conversations between friends or family (none of these
  studies tested lay conversation; the practice advice extrapolates from them), or that asking
  "prevents suicide". Samaritans' "can protect" and #BeThe1To's "may in fact reduce" should be
  attributed, not restated as fact.

**1.7 NHS Every Mind Matters, "Helping others"**
URL: https://www.nhs.uk/every-mind-matters/supporting-others/helping-others/ (read in full).
- "Ask open questions that start with "how", "what", "where" or "when"." [V] Links to Samaritans'
  listening tips.
- "Do not force someone to talk to you or get help, or go to a GP on their behalf" [V].
- Next steps it names: GP, NHS 111, self-referral to NHS talking therapies.
- The NHS "suicide" condition page URL I tried returned 404; not read.

**1.8 Crisis numbers as the services state them (checked 2026-09-26)**
- US and Canada: **988**. US page: "Call, text, or chat" [V]. Canada (988.ca): "Call 9-8-8",
  "Text 9-8-8", and "If your safety is at risk, call 9-1-1 right away." [V]
- UK and Ireland: Samaritans **116 123**, free from landlines and mobiles [V]; calls go to "more
  than 200 branches and locations across the UK and Ireland" [V].
- Australia: Lifeline **13 11 14**, text 0477 13 11 14; emergency 000 [V].
- Elsewhere: **findahelpline.com**, which describes itself as free, confidential support "from a
  helpline or hotline near you" [V], partnered with the International Association for Suicide
  Prevention and LifeLine International.
- The institute's Mental Fitness course already carries a standard callout with exactly these
  numbers (courses/health-and-body/mental-fitness/lessons/01-where-ordinary-ends.md, line 105).
  This course should reuse that wording rather than write a new one.

**1.9 What a Foundation conversation course should and should not try to teach here**
Drawing only on what the services themselves say:
- Should teach: that asking directly is what the services recommend and why; the listening moves
  they all share (attention, patience with silence, open questions, saying it back, no lectures, no
  quick fixes); that you do not keep a plan to self-harm secret; that the helper's job is to connect
  the person to help, not to fix or assess them (MHFA: not to "diagnose someone or solve the
  problem" [V]; R U OK?: you "can't 'fix' someone's problems" [V]); where the numbers are; that the
  helper looks after themselves (Samaritans section 6, 988 intro).
- Should not teach: risk assessment (method, plan, timing, access), safety-planning, or anything
  framed as counselling. These belong to trained responders; the lesson should point to 988,
  Samaritans, Lifeline, findahelpline and to the institute's Mental Fitness course.

---

### 2. Clinical communication: what medicine has measured about listening

Medicine is the one field that has timed its own listening. The findings are clean and quotable,
and the setting (a stranger with authority and a clock) must be stated each time.

**2.1 The Calgary-Cambridge Guide**
Sources read: the Enhanced Calgary-Cambridge Guide framework sheet citing Kurtz, Silverman, Benson
and Draper 2003, "Marrying Content and Process in Clinical Method Teaching: Enhancing the
Calgary-Cambridge Guides," *Academic Medicine* 78(8):802 to 809 (the sheet, not the article),
https://www.bradfordvts.co.uk/wp-content/onlineresources/communication-skills/teach-communication-skills/calgary-cambridge/01a%20calgary%20cambridge%20framework%20-%20basic.pdf ;
and a 73-item "Calgary-Cambridge Guide Communication Process Skills" list,
https://doctorsspeakup.com/sites/default/files/CCG%20Process.pdf (read in full). The book, Silverman,
Kurtz and Draper, *Skills for Communicating with Patients*, 3rd ed., CRC Press 2013, was not read.
- Structure [V]: Initiating the Session; Gathering Information; Physical Examination; Explanation
  and Planning; Closing the Session; with two running threads, Providing Structure and Building the
  Relationship.
- Gathering-information process skills, as listed on the framework sheet [V]: "patient's
  narrative", "question style: open to closed cone", "attentive listening", "facilitative
  response", "picking up cues", "clarification", "time-framing", "internal summary", "appropriate
  use of language".
- Item-level wording [V]:
  - 5: "Listens attentively to the patient's opening statement, without interrupting or directing
    patient's response"
  - 6: "Confirms list and screens for further problems (e.g. " so that's headaches and tiredness,
    anything else?"" (note: the guide's own example uses "anything", which Heritage et al. 2007
    later tested and found weaker than "something"; see 2.3.)
  - 10: "Listens attentively, allowing patient to complete statements without interruption and
    leaving space for patient to think before answering or go on after pausing" (this is the "wait
    time" skill).
  - 11: facilitates responses "verbally and non-verbally (e.g. use of encouragement, silence
    repletion, paraphrasing, interpretation)" (sic; "repletion" is a typo in this copy, almost
    certainly "repetition").
  - 12: "Picks up verbal and non-verbal cues ... checks out and acknowledges as appropriate"
  - 14: "Periodically summarizes to verify own understanding ... invites patient to correct
    interpretation or provide further information"
  - 36: "avoids giving advice, information or reassurance prematurely"
  - 42: checks understanding "by asking patient to restate in own words" (teach-back by another
    name).
- May say: the most widely used medical communication framework treats listening as a set of
  named, teachable behaviours, and its first rule of gathering information is to let the person
  finish. May not say the guide itself is an evidence-based finding; it is a curriculum built on
  the research below.

**2.2 How soon clinicians interrupt**
- **Beckman and Frankel 1984.** "The effect of physician behavior on the collection of data."
  *Annals of Internal Medicine* 101(5):692 to 696. doi:10.7326/0003-4819-101-5-692. **Abstract only.**
  74 recorded office visits. Patient allowed to complete opening statement in 17 (23%); physician
  interrupted in 51 (69%), and in only 1 of those 51 did the patient get back to finish. **The
  famous "18 seconds" is not in the abstract.** I found it only as a secondary citation: Singh
  Ospina et al. 2019 write that Beckman et al. found "a mean time to interruption of 18 s" [V].
  Gate: re-check against the 1984 full text before a lesson states 18 seconds.
- **Marvel, Epstein, Flowers and Beckman 1999.** "Soliciting the patient's agenda: have we
  improved?" *JAMA* 281(3):283 to 287. doi:10.1001/jama.281.3.283. Abstract only. 264 interviews,
  29 family physicians. Concerns solicited in 75.4%; patients completed their opening statement in
  28.0%; redirected "after a mean of 23.1 seconds" [V]. Patients allowed to finish "used only 6
  seconds more on average" [V]. Late-arising concerns 34.9% when not solicited vs 14.9%.
  Communication-trained physicians let patients finish more often (44% vs 22%).
- **Singh Ospina et al. 2019.** "Eliciting the Patient's Agenda: Secondary Analysis of Recorded
  Clinical Encounters." *J Gen Intern Med* 34(1):36 to 40. doi:10.1007/s11606-018-4540-5.
  PMC6318197. **Full text read.** 112 recorded encounters (Mayo Clinic and Minnesota/Wisconsin
  practices, from shared-decision-making trials). Agenda elicited in 40 of 112 (36%); 49% in
  primary care, 20% in specialty care. Where elicited, interrupted in 27 of 40 (67%) "after a
  median of 11 seconds (interquartile range 7–22; range 3 to 234 s)" [V]. Uninterrupted patients
  took a median of 6 s. Also cites Dyche 2004 (mean 16.5 s, not read). Limits the authors give:
  secondary analysis of trial encounters; completed statements were short partly because some
  patients said they had no concerns. They grant a counter-view: interruptions "may clarify or
  focus the conversation, and thus be beneficial to patients" [V], but judge that unlikely so early.
- **Langewitz et al. 2002.** "Spontaneous talking time at start of consultation in outpatient
  clinic: cohort study." *BMJ* 325(7366):682 to 683. doi:10.1136/bmj.325.7366.682. PMC126654.
  **Full text read.** Basel university hospital internal medicine outpatient clinic; 335 patients,
  14 doctors trained for one hour in "waiting, use of facilitators like "hmm-hmm," nodding, or
  echoing" [V], told not to ask questions in the opening phase, timing with a hidden stopwatch.
  "Mean spontaneous talking time was 92 seconds (SD 105 seconds; median 59 seconds...), and 78%
  (258) of patients had finished their initial statement in two minutes." [V] Seven talked over
  five minutes; the doctors judged all seven were giving important information. Talking time rose
  with age (17 to 29: 77 s; 50 to 87: 108 s). Conclusion: "two minutes of listening should be
  possible and will be sufficient for nearly 80% of patients." [V] Note the paper's intro gives the
  US figure as "22 seconds" citing Marvel; Marvel's abstract says 23.1.
- **What a lesson may say:** in recorded consultations, doctors often cut in within a quarter
  minute (means of 18 to 23 s in older US studies; a median of 11 s in a 2019 sample), and when
  patients are simply allowed to talk, most finish within about two minutes. **May not say**: that
  this describes ordinary conversation between equals (it is a professional encounter with an
  agenda and a clock); that "doctors interrupt after 11 seconds" as a universal (the 11 s is a
  median of 27 interruptions in one sample).

**2.3 One word: "some" versus "any" (Heritage et al. 2007)**
Heritage, Robinson, Elliott, Beckett and Wilkes 2007. "Reducing patients' unmet concerns in primary
care: the difference one word can make." *J Gen Intern Med* 22(10):1429 to 1433.
doi:10.1007/s11606-007-0279-0. PMC2305862. **Full text read.**
- Design: 20 family physicians (Los Angeles County and a Pennsylvania town), 224 acute-care visits
  analysed (204 in primary sample). After 4 normal visits, physicians randomised to ask either "Is
  there anything else you want to address in the visit today?" (ANY) or "Is there something else
  you want to address in the visit today?" (SOME) [V], after a 5-minute training video.
- Results [V]: among patients with more than one previsit concern, affirmative responses "90.3 vs
  53.1%, p = .003" (SOME vs ANY). Unmet concerns: 37% in non-intervention visits; SOME cut the
  covariate-adjusted rate to 9%, "eliminating 78% of all unmet concerns" (OR = .154, p = .001). ANY
  "could not be significantly distinguished from the nonintervention condition (p = .122)". Neither
  question changed visit length (mean 11.4 min).
- Mechanism proposed: "any" has negative polarity and communicates an expectation of "No". The
  authors extend it cautiously: "The relative failure of the ANY intervention may generalize to
  patient responses to other questions framed in similar terms, such as the almost ubiquitous "Do
  you have any questions?"" [V]
- Limits they state: small, underpowered for small differences; two US areas; volunteer physicians.
- Replication: I found no direct replication in this session. Gate.
- May say: in one randomised study, swapping "any" for "some" roughly doubled the rate at which
  patients raised another concern and cut unmet concerns sharply without lengthening visits. May
  not say it is proven for everyday talk ("Any questions?" at the end of a meeting); the authors
  only suggest it "may generalize".

**2.4 VitalTalk: NURSE statements for responding to emotion**
URL: https://www.vitaltalk.org/guides/responding-to-emotion-respecting/ ("Copyright VitalTalk 2023";
read in full). VitalTalk was founded by clinicians including Anthony Back, Robert Arnold and James
Tulsky.
- NURSE with the page's own examples and notes [V]:
  - Naming: "It sounds like you are frustrated". Note: "In general, turn down the intensity a notch
    when you name the emotion"
  - Understanding: "This helps me understand what you are thinking". Note: "stop short of
    suggesting you understand everything (you don't)"
  - Respecting: "I can see you have really been trying to follow our instructions"
  - Supporting: "I will do my best to make sure you have what you need"
  - Exploring: "Could you say more about what you mean when you say that…"
- Three "fundamental skills": "Tell me more" (use it "rather than jump to an assumption" [V]);
  "Ask-tell-ask"; "I wish" statements ("I wish I could say that the chemo always works" [V]).
- Evidence for the training (abstract only): Back et al. 2007, "Efficacy of communication skills
  training for giving bad news and discussing transitions to palliative care," *Arch Intern Med*
  167(5):453 to 460, doi:10.1001/archinte.167.5.453. Pre/post cohort, 115 oncology fellows, 4-day
  residential Oncotalk workshop; standardised-patient encounters coded blind; fellows gained a mean
  5.4 bad-news skills and 4.4 transition skills; coders could tell pre from post in 91% of
  recordings. No control group; outcome is skill use with actors, not patient outcomes.
- For this course: "Understanding: stop short of suggesting you understand everything" is the best
  single corrective to the common "I know exactly how you feel". Transfer to friends is a
  contested question (see (c)).

**2.5 Teach-back (AHRQ Health Literacy Universal Precautions Toolkit, 3rd ed., Tool 5)**
URL: https://www.ahrq.gov/health-literacy/improve/precautions/tool5.html (page last reviewed April
2024; read in full).
- Definition: "a way of checking understanding by asking patients to state in their own words what
  they need to know or do about their health." [V]
- Frames it as checking the explainer, not testing the listener: "you are checking how well you
  explained something, not testing the patient." [V]
- "Do not ask questions that can be answered with a "yes" or "no." "Do you understand?" and "Does
  that make sense?" are NOT teach-back questions. Patients are likely to answer "yes" whether they
  understand or not." [V]
- "Chunk and Check" rather than waiting to the end; reteach and check again; "If they parrot your
  words back to you, they may not have understood." [V]
- Evidence (abstract only): Talevski et al. 2020, "Teach-back: A systematic review of
  implementation and impacts," *PLoS One* 15(4):e0231350, doi:10.1371/journal.pone.0231350.
  20 studies of moderate quality (4 high, 9 moderate, 7 weak); effective in 19 of 20 on outcomes
  from recall to readmissions; heterogeneous. May say it is well supported in health settings; the
  transfer to everyday explanation is reasonable but not measured.
- Note the tension with VitalTalk's "Ask-tell-ask" example "Does that make sense...?" [V]: AHRQ
  says that exact question is not a check of understanding. Worth teaching as a small, concrete
  contrast.

---

### 3. Motivational interviewing: the practitioner technique (evidence is Part C's)

**3.1 MINT, "Understanding Motivational Interviewing"**
URL: https://motivationalinterviewing.org/understanding-motivational-interviewing (read in full).
- Definition quoted from Miller and Rollnick 2013, p. 29: MI is "a collaborative, goal-oriented
  style of communication with particular attention to the language of change." [V]
- Positioning: "a guiding style of communication, that sits between following (good listening) and
  directing (giving information and advice)." [V] Useful for the course because it names three
  listener stances.
- Warnings against technique-mongering: the practitioner must "refrain from unsolicited advice,
  confronting, instructing, directing, or warning. It is not a way to "get people to change" or a
  set of techniques to impose on the conversation." [V]
- OARS, in MINT's words [V]: Open questions "draw out and explore the person's experiences,
  perspectives, and ideas"; Affirmation "of strengths, efforts, and past successes"; Reflections
  "by repeating, rephrasing or offering a deeper guess about what the person is trying to
  communicate" ("a foundational skill of MI and how we express empathy"); Summarizing "ensures
  shared understanding".
- Information exchange as Elicit-Provide-Elicit: explore what they know, ask permission, offer,
  explore their response.
- Four processes: Engaging, Focusing, Evoking, Planning.

**3.2 MITI 4.2.1 coding manual (how practitioners are scored)**
Moyers, Manuel and Ernst, *Motivational Interviewing Treatment Integrity Coding Manual 4.2.1*,
revised June 2015, https://casaa.unm.edu/assets/docs/miti4_2.pdf (relevant sections read; the PDF is
stamped "Draft: Do not cite without permission", so cite the CASAA page, not quote at length).
- Reflection-to-Question ratio: "Fair" 1:1, "Good" 2:1; complex reflections 40% fair, 50% good
  (table read [V]). The thresholds are "based upon expert opinion, and currently lack normative or
  other validity data to support them." [V] The manual adds that the authors "have concluded that
  there is enough empirical evidence to retain the threshold scores for complex reflections and, in
  particular, the ratio of questions and reflections" [V].
- For this course: the one concrete, quotable practitioner norm about how much to ask versus how
  much to reflect back. Present it as MI's professional standard, not as a rule for conversation.

---

### 4. Negotiators and mediators

**4.1 FBI crisis negotiation: the Behavioral Change Stairway Model**
Vecchi, G. M., Van Hasselt, V. B., and Romano, S. J. 2005. "Crisis (hostage) negotiation: current
strategies and issues in high-risk conflict resolution." *Aggression and Violent Behavior*
10(5):533 to 551. doi:10.1016/j.avb.2004.10.001 (DOI confirmed via Crossref). Read: **full text** (copy at
https://joindpp.org/wp-content/uploads/crisis_hostage_negotiation_current_strat.pdf).
- The BCSM, "developed by the FBI's Crisis Negotiation Unit" [V], has five stages: "active
  listening, empathy, rapport, influence, and behavioral change" [V], sequential and cumulative.
- "Most persons in crisis have a desire to be heard and understood." [V]
- Core listening skills: Mirroring, Paraphrasing, Emotional Labeling, Summarizing. Supplemental:
  Effective Pauses, Minimal Encouragers, Open-ended Questions, "I" statements. The authors say the
  skills "are virtually identical to those utilized in counseling" [V].
- Definitions (paraphrased, with short verbatim):
  - Mirroring: "repeating the last few words or gist of the person in crisis" [V].
  - Emotional labeling: even a wrong label shows effort and "tends to defuse emotionality" [V];
    examples "You sound angry", "You seem frustrated" [V].
  - Summarizing: content plus emotion; example "you lost your job for no apparent reason
    (paraphrase) and this makes you angry (labeled emotion)" [V].
  - Effective pauses: deliberate silences before or after a meaningful comment.
  - Minimal encouragers: "uh-huh", "yes", "right", "go on", "okay" [V].
  - Open-ended questions typically begin "What" or "When" or "Tell me more about that"; "Why"
    statements "are usually avoided, as they tend to be perceived as interrogatory." [V] (PDF quote
    glyphs normalised.)
  - "I" messages for self-disclosure or when verbally attacked.
- The authors' own evidence caveat: most writing in the field is "position papers, case studies, or
  anecdotal reports" and "To date, there is a modicum of data regarding the utility of specific
  negotiation approaches themselves." [V] The claim that the model's value "has been consistently
  documented" [V] rests on practitioner reports (Dalfonzo 2002; Flood 2003).
- May say: the FBI's model puts listening first and treats influence as something earned after
  empathy and rapport; its listening toolkit is the counsellor's toolkit. May not say it is
  experimentally validated.

**4.2 Chris Voss, *Never Split the Difference* (with Tahl Raz, Harper Business, 2016)**
Not read. A former FBI hostage negotiator's popular account that repackages mirroring and labelling
as "tactical empathy" for business and personal negotiation. Flag in any lesson as a practitioner's
account with no independent testing of its specific techniques. Its framing is persuasion; this
course's framing is understanding. Useful only as the popular bridge from 4.1.

**4.3 Harvard Program on Negotiation (PON) Daily Blog**
- "Listening Skills for Maximum Success" (adapted from Robert C. Bordone, "Listen Up! Your Talks
  May Depend on It", Negotiation newsletter), https://www.pon.harvard.edu/daily/negotiation-skills-daily/listening-skills-for-maximum-success/
  (read in full). Active listening is three behaviours: paraphrasing, inquiry, acknowledgment.
  "Contrary to popular belief, active listening does not mean sitting patiently while your
  counterpart talks. Nor does it simply involve nodding, maintaining eye contact, or saying, "I
  understand."" [V] "Acknowledgment doesn't mean agreement." [V] Worked paraphrase ending "Have I
  captured your main points?" [V]
- "Silence in Negotiation: Why Saying Nothing Can Be Powerful," https://www.pon.harvard.edu/daily/negotiation-skills-daily/dear-negotiation-coach-when-silence-is-golden-nb/
  (read in full). Anecdote: a banker told to "count to three silently before responding" [V]. Says
  "Research consistently shows that most negotiators don't truly listen" [V] with **no citation**:
  do not repeat it as a finding. Also frames silence partly as a tactic (e.g. after an extreme
  anchor), which differs from the listening use.

**4.4 Acas (UK), *Challenging conversations and how to manage them***
PDF: https://www.acas.org.uk/sites/default/files/2021-04/challenging-conversations-and-how-to-manage-them.pdf
(question and listening sections read). Also read Acas "What mediation is" page
(https://www.acas.org.uk/mediation): mediator is impartial; "not about judging who was right or
wrong in the past" [V].
- A question-type table: open, closed, probing/reflective, leading ("should be avoided" [V]),
  multiple ("should be avoided" [V]). Sequence: "at the start of the discussion it is usually best
  to ask open questions"; then probing; then closed to verify [V]. The open-question example given
  is "Why did you leave?" [V], which crisis negotiators avoid (4.1): a small, real disagreement
  between practitioner traditions about "why".
- Listening dos and don'ts: "Ask questions – but try to avoid interrupting." [V] "Make up your mind
  too quickly without hearing the whole story" is listed as a don't [V].
- Unsourced statistic: the guide says we spend our working day "writing 9%", "reading 16%",
  "speaking 30%", "listening 45%" [V], with no source. These figures resemble a 1920s estimate
  (Rankin) that is widely repeated; I did not trace it. Gate: do not use.
- Mediate.com and CEDR: not read in this session.

---

### 5. Interviewers, oral historians and journalists

All four traditions converge on: prepare, ask open questions one at a time, follow up, let silence
work, do not interrupt, let the speaker take you somewhere you did not plan.

**5.1 Oral History Association, *Principles and Best Practices* (2025 complete PDF)**
https://oralhistory.org/wp-content/uploads/2025/10/2025-OHA-Principles-and-Best-Practices-Complete.pdf
(interviewing and ethics sections read).
- "Along with asking open-ended questions and actively listening to the answers, interviewers
  should ask follow-up questions, seeking additional clarification, elaboration, and reflection."
  [V]
- Interviewers should provide "challenging and perceptive inquiry ... not being satisfied with
  superficial responses" while encouraging narrators "to respond to questions in their own style
  and language" [V]; and "extend the inquiry beyond the specific focus of the project to allow the
  narrator to freely define what is most relevant" [V].
- On difference: oral historians "consider how these differences might impact the way a narrator
  shares memories; and they strive to treat each narrator equitably and do their best to listen
  with empathy." [V] Also the narrator's right to refuse to answer.

**5.2 Smithsonian Institution Archives, *How to Do Oral History* (2020)**
https://siarchives.si.edu/sites/default/files/pdfs/How_to_Do_Oral_History_Guide_2020.pdf (read in
full; the Smithsonian Folklife guide PDF returned only page numbers and the Folklife web page 403'd).
- "Six R's", including RESTRAINT: "asking open-ended questions, listening closely without
  interrupting, following up on details or unexpected avenues of information, challenging
  questionable information in a non-threatening way" [V].
- Question advice [V]: "Allow the interviewee to do the talking." Open questions such as "tell me
  about, describe"; if you get yes or no, "ask how, why, when, where, who"; "Do not ask leading
  questions"; "Ask one question at a time"; "Don't worry about silences. Let the interviewee think
  and take time before they answer."; "Allow the interviewee to drift off to topics not on your
  outline. These can be the best part of your interview."; politely question doubtful information
  ("Oh, I'm confused, I thought that Mrs. X was involved in that.").
- Contrast worth teaching: this guide tells interviewers to avoid "too many verbal responses ...
  such as "Really!" or "Uh-huh, uh-huh."" [V] and use nods instead, because they record over the
  speaker. The 988 Lifeline and FBI model recommend "Uh huh" as a minimal encourager. Same goal
  (show you are listening), different channel constraints (a recording versus a phone line).

**5.3 StoryCorps, "Great Questions"**
https://storycorps.org/participate/great-questions/ (read in full). A list of open prompts by
category (grandparents, love, work, serious illness, remembering a loved one...). Examples [V]:
"Who has been the biggest influence on your life? What lessons did that person or those people
teach you?" "How has your life been different than what you'd imagined?" "Is there anything that
you've never told me but want to tell me now?" Useful as a practice bank for the intergenerational
lesson. Note several items are phrased as yes/no ("Do you have any regrets?") yet work as openers
in context: a caution against teaching the open/closed distinction too rigidly.

**5.4 Poynter: Roy Peter Clark, "12 basics of interviewing, listening and note-taking" (2015)**
https://www.poynter.org/reporting-editing/2015/12-basics-of-interviewing-listening-and-note-taking/
(read in full). "Listen, listen and listen. Let the subject know you are listening by maintaining
eye contact, nodding your head, leaning forward and taking notes." [V] Bonus tips [V]: "Ask one
question at a time. This isn't multiple choice." "Ask open-ended questions, not ones that can be
answered yes or no." "Be patient. Don't break the silence with a new question." Also: prepare, but
"do not be tied to your list of questions" [V]. The 2023 illustrated version (Aguiar) repeats it.

**5.5 Library of Congress Veterans History Project**: interviewing page returned 403, field-kit URL
404. Not read.

---

### 6. Talking across difference

**6.1 Braver Angels (US)**
- "How We Can Reconcile With Each Other When Our Politics Are So Polarized," hosted at
  https://braverangels.org/how-we-can-reconcile-with-each-other-when-our-politics-are-so-polarized/
  (read in full; appears to be a reprinted newspaper feature quoting co-founder William Doherty, a
  marriage and family therapist who designed the workshops "based on techniques used in couples
  therapy" [V]).
  - The **LAPP** technique, verbatim [V]: "Listen to understand, not reload. Acknowledge what you
    heard. Pivot by assessing whether it is OK to offer your views. Offer your perspective (if the
    person has signaled a willingness to hear it)."
  - "Use "I" statements instead of dogmatic ones." [V]
  - Couples-therapy tenets carried over [V]: "Speak for yourself; don't interpret what's going on in
    the other person's mind."
- "What we do" page (https://braverangels.org/what-we-do/, read): Red/Blue workshops of "a small,
  balanced group of Reds and Blues" [V]; "Skills for Bridging the Divide"; "Families & Politics".
  Mission page: "In Braver Angels, neither side is teaching the other or giving feedback on how to
  think or say things differently." [V] Governance rule: Red and Blue leaders equally represented.
  The dedicated skills-workshop URL returned an empty page.
- **Evidence: Baron, Blair, Choi, Gamboa, Gottlieb, Robinson, Rosenzweig, Turnbull and West 2025.**
  "Couples Therapy for a Divided America: Assessing the Effects of Reciprocal Group Reflection on
  Partisan Polarization." *Political Behavior*. doi:10.1007/s11109-024-09993-z. Read: substantial
  parts of the author PDF (https://www.dhdannychoi.com/files/BBCGGRRTW_BraverAngels.pdf).
  Pre-registered (EGAP 20200217AB). Undergraduates at four universities, February to March 2020;
  165 subjects (116 Blue, 49 Red), 59 assigned to a Red/Blue workshop, rest to surveys only;
  midline 1 to 2 weeks, endline about six months later. Findings: reduced affective polarization on
  implicit and explicit measures, "though the effect on the explicit measure just misses the
  conventional 5% threshold for statistical significance" [V]; increased willingness to donate to
  depolarization programmes; explicit and implicit effects "dissipate somewhat over time, but the
  effects on the behavioral measure persist after more than half a year" [V]. Limits: small,
  volunteer, liberal-skewed sample (at two campuses all Red volunteers had to be assigned to
  treatment, so they do not contribute to estimates). May say: one small randomised trial found
  the workshop reduced hostility toward the other party in the short term. May not say it changes
  policy views (not its aim) or that effects are durable in general.

**6.2 Essential Partners and Reflective Structured Dialogue (RSD)**
- "Our Method," https://whatisessential.org/our-method (read in full). Founded 1989 (as the Public
  Conversations Project) by "behavioral health researchers and practitioners" [V] applying family
  therapy practice to polarised public issues. "We never ask people to change their views." [V]
- *Fostering Dialogue Across Divides: A Nuts and Bolts Guide from Essential Partners* (2018),
  https://whatisessential.org/sites/default/files/inline-images/FDAD/FDAD-EssentialPartners-2018.pdf
  (agreements, go-round and pause sections read; the guide is about 65,000 words).
  - Sample agreements [V]: "We will speak for ourselves, and allow others to speak for themselves";
    "We will not criticize the views of others or attempt to persuade them"; "We will listen with
    resilience, "hanging in" when something is hard for us to hear"; replace attributions ("You
    just believe that because…") with a question ("What leads you to that belief?"); "We will share
    airtime"; "We will not interrupt except to indicate that we cannot or did not hear a speaker";
    "We may "pass" or "pass for now"".
  - The go-round: a question, a pause before anyone answers, the question repeated, each person a
    set time in turn.
  - On pauses: "Most moments of silence are anything but empty." [V] Pauses "promote better
    listening, because most listeners can concentrate better when they know there will be a pause
    during which they can gather their thoughts." [V]
  - Questions: "If you ask a question, be sure it reflects genuine curiosity and is not a challenge
    in disguise." [V]
- Evidence: EP links an archive of research on RSD; not read. Treat as practitioner method.

**6.3 Conversational receptiveness (Yeomans, Minson, Collins, Chen and Gino 2020)**
"Conversational receptiveness: Improving engagement with opposing views." *Organizational Behavior
and Human Decision Processes* 160:131 to 148. doi:10.1016/j.obhdp.2020.03.011. Read: substantial
parts of the published PDF (https://receptiveness.net/assets/papers/Conversational.pdf).
- Defines receptiveness as "the use of language to communicate one's willingness to thoughtfully
  engage with opposing views" [V]. Builds a machine-learning model of it from text (Studies 1A-B).
- Receptive markers [V]: explicit acknowledgement ("I understand," "I see your point"), explicit
  agreement ("I agree," "you're right") even when disagreeing overall, and hedges ("somewhat,"
  "might"). Unreceptive markers: negations ("no," "wrong") and "a focus on explanatory reasoning
  (e.g., "because," "therefore")" [V].
- Study 2: government executives rated more receptive by the algorithm and by partners, "but not
  their own self-evaluations" [V], were seen as better teammates. People are poor judges of their
  own receptiveness.
- Study 3: Wikipedia editors whose opening posts were more receptive were less likely to receive
  personal attacks later.
- Study 4: the "receptiveness recipe" [V]: "positive statements, rather than negations; explicit
  acknowledgement of understanding; finding points of agreement; and hedging to soften claims."
  771 online responders (MTurk); instructed writers were rated more receptive (standardised
  beta = 0.57 by humans, 0.51 by algorithm); the abstract says their messages were seen as more
  persuasive and they as more desirable collaborators. Attrition was slightly higher in the
  intervention arm (17.2% vs 12.6%), which the authors flag.
- Caveats for drafters: text-based, mostly online samples; outcome is how others rate you, not
  attitude change. Co-author Francesca Gino was later found by Harvard to have committed research
  misconduct in other papers; none of the Data Colada-flagged papers is this one as far as I know,
  and the corresponding author is Yeomans. Gate: check before citing.
- A 2024 follow-up (Minson, Yeomans, Collins and Dorison, "Conversational receptiveness transmits
  between parties", seen as a PDF link in search) was not read.

**6.4 Deep canvassing: from a fraud to a smaller, real effect**
- **LaCour and Green 2014.** "When contact changes minds: an experiment on transmission of support
  for gay equality." *Science* 346(6215):1366 to 1369. doi:10.1126/science.1256151. **RETRACTED**
  (Science 348(6239):1100, 5 June 2015, doi:10.1126/science.aac6638). Abstract only. Claimed a
  single conversation with a gay canvasser produced large, persistent, contagious opinion change
  (972 voters). Retraction reasons, from the editorial notice as reproduced by Retraction Watch
  (https://retractionwatch.com/2015/05/28/science-retracts-troubled-gay-canvassing-study-against-lacours-objections/,
  read): "(i) Survey incentives were misrepresented" and "(ii) The statement on sponsorship was
  false" [V], plus "statistical irregularities" noted by independent researchers and no original
  data produced [V]. Co-author Green requested retraction; LaCour did not agree. The
  irregularities were identified by Broockman, Kalla and Aronow (report not read; URL 404).
- **Broockman and Kalla 2016.** "Durably reducing transphobia: A field experiment on door-to-door
  canvassing." *Science* 352(6282):220 to 224. doi:10.1126/science.aad9713. Abstract only. 56
  canvassers, 501 voters, South Florida; about 10-minute conversations "encouraging actively taking
  the perspective of others" [V]; reduced transphobia for at least 3 months; effective with
  transgender and non-transgender canvassers.
- **Kalla and Broockman 2020.** "Reducing Exclusionary Attitudes through Interpersonal
  Conversation: Evidence from Three Field Experiments." *American Political Science Review*
  114(2):410 to 425. doi:10.1017/S0003055419000923. Abstract only (read on Cambridge Core).
  Three pre-registered field experiments; 230 canvassers, 6,869 voters, 7 US locations. Key design
  contrast: arguments-alone conversations "had no effects", while "otherwise identical
  conversations also including the non-judgmental exchange of narratives durably reduced
  exclusionary attitudes for at least four months (d = 0.08)" [V]; two transphobia experiments
  replicate "(ds = 0.08, 0.04)" [V].
- May say: the famous 2014 result was fabricated and retracted; the researchers who exposed it
  then ran real trials and found that listening-heavy conversations with an exchange of personal
  stories produce durable but **small** effects (d around 0.04 to 0.08), while argument alone did
  nothing. May not say "one conversation changes minds" in the 2014 sense.

**6.5 Talking with older adults: accommodation and elderspeak**
- Communication accommodation theory (Giles): the idea that speakers converge toward or diverge
  from a partner's style, and that over-accommodation (slower, simpler, louder speech driven by age
  stereotypes) is heard as patronising. **Not read from a primary source in this session** (only
  search-result summaries); Ryan, Hummert and Boich 1995, "Communication Predicaments of Aging,"
  *Journal of Language and Social Psychology* 14(1-2), doi:10.1177/0261927X95141008, located via
  Crossref, not read. Gate before stating any detail.
- **Williams, Kemper and Hummert 2003.** "Improving nursing home communication: an intervention to
  reduce elderspeak." *The Gerontologist* 43(2):242 to 247. doi:10.1093/geront/43.2.242. Abstract
  only. 20 certified nursing assistants, 5 nursing homes, pre/post recordings. After training,
  less elderspeak ("terms of endearment, inappropriate collective pronouns, and shortened sentence
  length" [V]); tone rated less controlling and more respectful, caring qualities maintained.
- **Williams, Herman, Gajewski and Wilson 2009.** "Elderspeak communication: impact on dementia
  care." *Am J Alzheimers Dis Other Demen* 24(1):11 to 20. doi:10.1177/1533317508318472. Abstract
  only. 80 videotaped care episodes, 20 residents with dementia. Probability of resistiveness to
  care after elderspeak .55 (95% CrI .44 to .66) vs .26 (.12 to .44) after normal talk.
  Observational: association, not proof of cause.
- **Williams, Perkhounkova, Herman and Bossen 2017.** "A Communication Intervention to Reduce
  Resistiveness in Dementia Care: A Cluster Randomized Controlled Trial." *The Gerontologist*
  57(4):707 to 718. doi:10.1093/geront/gnw047. Abstract only. 13 nursing homes randomised; 42
  dyads. Elderspeak fell from 34.6% by 13.6 points after training; resistiveness fell from 35.7% by
  15.3 points; change in resistiveness was predicted by change in elderspeak. Small; single
  research group.
- May say: "elderspeak" (pet names like "honey", "we" for "you", sing-song simplified talk) is
  common in care settings, is linked to more resistance from people with dementia, and can be
  trained down. May not generalise from nursing-home dementia care to talk with healthy older
  relatives without saying so.

**6.6 Hearing loss**
RNID, "How to communicate with someone who is deaf or has hearing loss,"
https://rnid.org.uk/information-and-support/how-to-communicate-with-deaf-people-hearing-loss/ (read in
full). Verbatim tips [V]: "Please face me when you talk to me. Lots of people rely on lipreading to
some degree to understand." "Don't be afraid to repeat or rephrase and try something different."
"Ask someone how they want to be communicated with." "Make sure you are facing the person you are
talking to and speak clearly – avoid shouting, speaking too fast or unnecessarily slow." "You should
always follow the advice of the person with communication needs." "In a noisy place, move to a
quieter area if possible." RNID survey figure: "Only 41% of the general public are confident
communicating with people with hearing loss and just 29% with BSL users." [V] (survey details not
read). HLAA tips: not read.

**6.7 Stammering (stuttering)**
STAMMA (British Stammering Association), "In Conversation With Someone Who Stammers,"
https://stamma.org/about-stammering/conversation-someone-stammers (read in full).
- "Basically, people who stammer just want the time to say what they want to say. Without being
  interrupted, rushed or spoken over." [V]
- "It can be tempting to try and help by finishing someone's sentences. Try not to do this. People
  who stammer often find it intrusive rather than helpful." [V]
- Do not advise "slow down", "relax" or "stop and breathe": "This just shows that you're more
  focused on how they speak, than on what they're saying." [V]
- Keep natural eye contact; "Take as long as you need" is OK if they are really struggling; it is
  fine to ask for a repeat; avoid "You're really brave" and congratulating fluency.
- Stuttering Foundation page: URL 404; not read.
- Teaching use: the clearest real-world case where the "helpful" completion of someone's sentence
  (a common cooperative-overlap habit) is experienced as disrespect.

**6.8 Talking through an interpreter (brief)**
LanguageLine Solutions, "11 Helpful Tips for Working with an Over-the-Phone Interpreter," hosted by
the Minnesota Department of Health,
https://www.health.state.mn.us/docs/people/wic/localagency/program/civilrights/helpfultips.pdf (read
in full). "SPEAK DIRECTLY TO THE CUSTOMER - You and your customer can communicate directly with each
other as if the interpreter were not there." [V] "SPEAK NATURALLY, NOT LOUDER" [V]; speak in "one
sentence or two short ones at a time" [V]; "Whatever the interpreter hears will be interpreted" [V];
avoid jargon and idioms; expect the conversation to take about twice as long. Other guides found in
search (courts, Iowa, Bow Valley) say the same (first person, eye contact with the person, not
"tell her..."); search snippets only. Commercial source: fine for a brief note.

---

### 7. Listening at work (short)

- **Google re:Work, "Understand team effectiveness"**
  (https://rework.withgoogle.com/intl/en/guides/understand-team-effectiveness, read). 180 teams
  (115 engineering project teams, 65 sales pods) [V]. Five dynamics: psychological safety,
  dependability, structure and clarity, meaning, impact. Uses Edmondson's definition of team
  psychological safety: "a shared belief held by members of a team that the team is safe for
  interpersonal risk taking" [V]. **The page does not mention conversational turn-taking.** The
  popular link between Project Aristotle and "equality in distribution of conversational
  turn-taking" comes from press coverage (Duhigg, *New York Times Magazine*, 2016, not read) which
  drew on Woolley et al. Gate.
- **Edmondson 1999.** "Psychological Safety and Learning Behavior in Work Teams." *Administrative
  Science Quarterly* 44(2):350 to 383. doi:10.2307/2666999. Abstract only (via Crossref). 51 work
  teams in one manufacturing company; psychological safety associated with learning behaviour,
  which mediates its link to team performance.
- **Woolley, Chabris, Pentland, Hashmi and Malone 2010.** "Evidence for a collective intelligence
  factor in the performance of human groups." *Science* 330(6004):686 to 688.
  doi:10.1126/science.1193147. Abstract only. 699 people in groups of 2 to 5; a "c factor"
  correlated with average social sensitivity, "the equality in distribution of conversational
  turn-taking, and the proportion of females in the group" [V].
- **Credé and Howardson 2017.** "The structure of group task performance: A second look at
  'collective intelligence': Comment on Woolley et al. (2010)." *Journal of Applied Psychology*
  102(10):1483 to 1492. doi:10.1037/apl0000176. Abstract only. Reanalysed 6 samples: "the empirical
  support for the construct is generally weak" [V]; flags low-effort responding and nested data.
- **Riedl, Kim, Gupta, Malone and Woolley 2021.** "Quantifying collective intelligence in human
  groups." *PNAS* 118(21):e2005737118. doi:10.1073/pnas.2005737118. PMC8166150. Substantial parts
  read. 22 studies, 5,279 individuals, 1,356 groups; supports a CI factor; predicted by proportion
  of women, mediated by social perceptiveness; group process more important than individual skill.
  **It does not test turn-taking equality** (tasks were computer-mediated; process measures were
  skill congruence, strategy, effort). Same research group as the original; not an independent
  replication.
- May say, briefly: a team climate where people can speak up without fear is linked to learning
  in teams; one influential study linked more equal speaking turns to group performance, and
  whether a general "group intelligence" exists is disputed. May not say "Google proved that teams
  where everyone speaks equally perform best."

---

### (a) What this part says the field considers essential

1. **Let them finish.** Every tradition read (Samaritans, 988, R U OK?, MHFA, Calgary-Cambridge,
   FBI, Acas, oral historians, Poynter, Essential Partners, STAMMA) names not interrupting as
   basic. Medicine has measured the cost of doing otherwise (interruption within 11 to 23 s; most
   patients done in about 2 minutes if left alone).
2. **Tolerate silence on purpose.** Samaritans ("you don't have to fill every silence"), R U OK?
   ("sit patiently with the silence"), Calgary-Cambridge ("leaving space for patient to think"),
   FBI ("effective pauses"), Smithsonian ("Don't worry about silences"), Poynter ("Don't break the
   silence with a new question"), Essential Partners (designed pauses).
3. **Open questions, one at a time, then follow up.** Near-universal, with specific wording advice
   (Samaritans' "how are you feeling today?" over "are you OK?"; Smithsonian and Poynter "one
   question at a time"; OHA follow-ups for "clarification, elaboration, and reflection").
4. **Say it back and check.** SHUSH "Say it back"; 988 "Summarize What You Hear"; R U OK? repeat
   back "and ask if you have understood"; Calgary-Cambridge internal summary "invites patient to
   correct"; FBI paraphrase and summarise; MI reflections; PON "Have I captured your main points?";
   AHRQ teach-back in the other direction.
5. **Name the feeling, lightly.** FBI emotional labelling, VitalTalk NURSE ("turn down the
   intensity a notch"; "stop short of suggesting you understand everything").
6. **Hold back advice, fixing and your own story.** Samaritans, MHFA, R U OK?, MINT ("refrain from
   unsolicited advice"), Calgary-Cambridge item 36 (no premature reassurance), Braver Angels LAPP
   (offer your view only if they signal willingness).
7. **Acknowledgement is not agreement.** PON verbatim; Yeomans et al.'s receptiveness recipe;
   Braver Angels' Acknowledge step; MHFA "accepting, even if you don't agree".
8. **Ask directly about suicide, do not keep it secret, and connect to help.** Consistent across
   Samaritans, 988, #BeThe1To, R U OK?, MHFA, NHS; supported by research showing no harm from asking.
9. **Adjust to the person, and ask them how.** RNID ("Ask someone how they want to be communicated
   with"), STAMMA (give time, do not finish), elderspeak research (do not over-accommodate),
   interpreters (speak to the person).

### (b) Common misconceptions (each named by a source)

1. **"Asking about suicide will put the idea in their head."** Named and rejected by Dazzi et al.
   2014 (the "commonly held perception" among psychologists), Blades et al. 2018, Polihronis et al.
   2022, #BeThe1To. Dazzi cites surveys in which 23% of German primary-care physicians would not ask
   elderly depressed patients, and one-third of 103 English GPs believed asking could induce
   self-harm thoughts.
2. **"Active listening means sitting patiently and nodding."** PON: "Contrary to popular belief,
   active listening does not mean sitting patiently ... Nor does it simply involve nodding" [V].
3. **"If I let them talk, they'll go on forever."** Addressed by Langewitz et al. 2002 ("Doctors do
   not risk being swamped" [V]) and Marvel et al. 1999 (letting patients finish cost 6 seconds on
   average).
4. **"'Do you understand?' checks understanding."** AHRQ: those are "NOT teach-back questions" [V].
5. **"'Any other concerns?' invites more."** Heritage et al. 2007 show "any" underperforms "some";
   they note textbooks recommend the "any" form.
6. **"Finishing a stammerer's sentence helps them."** STAMMA: "Try not to do this." [V] So is
   telling them to "slow down" or "relax".
7. **"Speak louder and slower to someone with hearing loss (or to an older person)."** RNID:
   "avoid shouting, speaking too fast or unnecessarily slow" [V]; the interpreter guidance: "SPEAK
   NATURALLY, NOT LOUDER" [V]; elderspeak research on over-accommodation.
8. **"I can tell how receptive I sound."** Yeomans et al. 2020: partner and algorithm ratings
   predicted outcomes, self-ratings did not.
9. **"One conversation can flip someone's views on a divisive issue."** The source of the famous
   version (LaCour and Green 2014) was retracted; real trials find small, durable effects (Kalla
   and Broockman 2020, d about 0.04 to 0.08), and argument alone had no effect.
10. **"MI (or NURSE, or the FBI model) is a set of techniques to get people to do things."** MINT:
    "It is not a way to "get people to change" or a set of techniques to impose on the
    conversation." [V]

### (c) Contested questions

**C1. Should you ask someone directly whether they are thinking about suicide?**
- Position A (ask directly, in plain words): Samaritans ("it's okay to ask them directly"), 988 ("Be
  direct"), #BeThe1To ("Are you thinking about suicide?"), MHFA (Approach, assess for risk of
  suicide), R U OK? (via Lifeline). Best case: consistent evidence from RCTs, cohorts and at least
  four reviews or meta-analyses (Dazzi 2014; Blades 2018; DeCou and Schumann 2018; Polihronis 2022)
  that asking in research and clinical settings does not raise suicidal ideation or distress and
  may reduce it slightly; a 2026 experiment extends this to anonymous online surveys of adolescents.
- Position B (caution): the historic view held by some clinicians and ethics committees (the
  surveys Dazzi cites). Its best remaining case is not that asking plants the idea but that the
  evidence base is about structured questionnaires and clinicians, not frightened friends; that
  Polihronis et al. say more low-risk-of-bias RCTs are needed; that a minority of individual
  participants report short-term distress (Biddle 2013: 22%; Reynolds 2006: increases in 16.4% of
  sessions, alongside decreases in 17.5%).
- What would settle it: trials or careful cohort studies of lay-initiated conversations (e.g.
  gatekeeper-trained vs untrained friends), measuring ideation and help-seeking afterward.
- Classification: **established** that asking in research, screening and clinical settings does not
  increase suicidal ideation; **contested empirical** (untested) for lay conversations, where the
  practice advice is an extrapolation that every major service endorses. A lesson should present
  "ask directly" as the services' unanimous advice, backed by strong evidence of no harm in the
  settings studied.

**C2. Does conversation across political difference change minds or reduce hostility?**
- Position A (yes, structured listening reduces hostility and can shift attitudes a little):
  Braver Angels ("reduce stereotyped thinking"); Essential Partners (interrupts polarised cycles);
  Kalla and Broockman 2020 (non-judgmental exchange of narratives: durable effects, d about 0.04 to
  0.08); Baron et al. 2025 (Braver Angels workshop reduced affective polarization, behavioural
  effect lasting six months). Best case: randomised, pre-registered field evidence, including a
  design contrast in which arguments alone did nothing.
- Position B (effects are small, fragile or about feelings not views): the same papers' own
  numbers (tiny ds; the explicit-measure effect in Baron et al. just missed significance; effects
  dissipate); samples are volunteers inclined to dialogue; the most celebrated result (LaCour and
  Green) was fraudulent. Also, the organisations themselves disclaim changing views (Essential
  Partners: "We never ask people to change their views"; Braver Angels: neither side is teaching the
  other), so "changing minds" may be the wrong outcome to measure.
- What would settle it: larger pre-registered trials with non-volunteer samples, longer follow-up,
  independent replication of Baron et al., and clear separation of outcomes (hostility toward the
  other side vs policy attitudes vs behaviour).
- Classification: **contested empirical** on size and durability; partly a **value** question about
  what such conversations are for (understanding and civility vs persuasion).

**C3. Are practitioner scripts (NURSE, OARS, the FBI stairway) transferable to friends and family?**
- Position A (yes, the skills are the same): Vecchi et al. say the FBI's listening skills are
  "virtually identical" to counselling skills; R U OK?, Samaritans and 988 teach lay people the same
  moves (paraphrase, summarise, open questions, silence); MINT says MI principles "are useful in a
  wide range of conversations" [V] and have "intuitive or "common sense" appeal" [V]; MHFA trains
  lay people and its meta-analysis shows knowledge and confidence gains.
- Position B (not as scripts): the scripts are built for asymmetric, goal-driven roles (clinician
  with a clock, negotiator seeking behaviour change, MI practitioner steering toward change). MINT
  warns MI is not "a set of techniques to impose on the conversation"; VitalTalk's own note warns
  against implying you understand everything; formulaic reflection can sound manipulative among
  equals, and influence-oriented versions (Voss) import a persuasion goal. Evidence on lay transfer
  is thin: MHFA's meta-analysis finds the quality of real helping behaviour "unclear"; Oncotalk and
  the FBI evidence concern trained professionals with actors or case reports. MI's own proficiency
  thresholds are "expert opinion".
- What would settle it: studies that train ordinary people in these specific moves and measure
  how their real partners (not raters) experience the conversations over time.
- Classification: **contested empirical**, with a **value** element (whether it is acceptable to
  use influence techniques on people you love). A lesson can safely teach the shared listening
  moves (let them finish, silence, say it back, name the feeling lightly) and flag the scripts as
  professional tools.

### (d) Gates: claims a drafter must re-check against a primary text before a lesson states them

1. **Beckman and Frankel 1984 "18 seconds".** Only seen as a secondary citation (Singh Ospina et
   al. 2019). Read the Annals full text for the exact figure and how it was measured.
2. **Marvel 1999 "23 seconds"** is 23.1 s (abstract); Langewitz cites it as 22. Use 23.
3. **Heritage 2007 numbers** (90.3% vs 53.1% affirmative; 78% of unmet concerns eliminated; 37% to
   9%) are from the full text I read; check no direct replication exists before calling it
   replicated.
4. **Dazzi et al. 2014 is a short narrative review**, not a formal systematic review with pooling;
   do not call it a meta-analysis. For pooled estimates, cite Blades 2018 (g = -0.13) or Polihronis
   2022, but read their full texts first (abstracts only here).
5. **"Asking can protect" (Samaritans) and "may reduce" (#BeThe1To)**: attribute; the evidence is
   about ideation in research and clinical settings, not suicide deaths and not lay conversations.
6. **988 five steps**: use the service's exact wording (Ask; Be there; Help keep them safe; Help them
   connect; Follow up).
7. **ALGEE wording** differs by country; cite the US page (2025) exactly or name the version.
8. **Vecchi et al. 2005**: the claim that the model's value is "consistently documented" rests on
   practitioner reports the authors cite (Dalfonzo 2002; Flood 2003), not trials; say so.
9. **Acas "listening 45%"** statistic is unsourced; do not use it.
10. **PON "Research consistently shows that most negotiators don't truly listen"** is uncited; do
    not repeat as fact.
11. **Project Aristotle and turn-taking**: Google's own page does not mention turn-taking; the
    link is press coverage of Woolley 2010. Read Duhigg 2016 if the lesson mentions it.
12. **Woolley 2010 turn-taking correlation**: read the full paper for the size of the correlation
    and which study it came from; note Credé and Howardson 2017 and that Riedl 2021 did not test it.
13. **Kalla and Broockman 2020 and Broockman and Kalla 2016**: abstracts only; read full texts
    before describing scripts or subgroup results.
14. **Yeomans et al. 2020**: confirm the paper is not among any retracted or flagged Gino papers
    before citing, and note it is text-based.
15. **Elderspeak papers**: abstracts only; check definitions of elderspeak and effect sizes.
16. **Communication accommodation theory and the communication predicament of aging**: read a
    primary Giles source (e.g. Giles, Coupland and Coupland 1991, or Ryan et al. 1995) before
    stating any detail.
17. **Crisis numbers**: re-check on publication day (done 2026-09-26); reuse the Mental Fitness
    course's callout text.
18. **MITI thresholds**: the manual is stamped "Draft: Do not cite without permission"; cite it
    as the CASAA-published MITI 4.2.1 and keep quotation minimal.

### (e) Best free resources to link (all checked live 2026-09-26, HTTP 200)

- Samaritans, SHUSH listening tips: https://www.samaritans.org/how-we-can-help/if-youre-worried-about-someone-else/how-support-someone-youre-worried-about/what-do-if-you-think-someone-struggling/ (the clearest five-point listening guide for lay people).
- Samaritans, supporting someone with suicidal thoughts: https://www.samaritans.org/how-we-can-help/if-youre-worried-about-someone-else/supporting-someone-suicidal-thoughts/ (asking directly, being there).
- 988 Lifeline, Help Someone Else: https://988lifeline.org/help-someone-else/ (do's and don'ts, five steps, active listening).
- #BeThe1To, the 5 steps and the evidence: https://www.bethe1to.com/bethe1to-steps-evidence/ (reasons given for each step).
- R U OK?, How to ask: https://www.ruok.org.au/how-to-ask (readiness check and four-step conversation, with roleplay videos).
- MHFA ALGEE explainer: https://mentalhealthfirstaid.org/news/algee-how-mhfa-helps-you-respond-in-crisis-and-non-crisis-situations/ (what a lay helper is and is not for).
- NHS Every Mind Matters, Helping others: https://www.nhs.uk/every-mind-matters/supporting-others/helping-others/ (short, UK, plain).
- findahelpline.com: https://findahelpline.com/ (crisis lines by country).
- Dazzi et al. 2014, free full text: https://www.cambridge.org/core/journals/psychological-medicine/article/does-asking-about-suicide-and-related-behaviours-induce-suicidal-ideation-what-is-the-evidence/FCAEE9E5BC840D76CF10AEBECD921AC9 (the short review behind "asking doesn't plant the idea").
- Heritage et al. 2007, free full text: https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC2305862/ (the "some vs any" experiment; readable).
- Langewitz et al. 2002, free full text: https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC126654/ (one page; 92 seconds).
- Singh Ospina et al. 2019, free full text: https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC6318197/ (11-second median; good literature summary).
- VitalTalk NURSE guide: https://www.vitaltalk.org/guides/responding-to-emotion-respecting/ (one-page emotion-response skills).
- AHRQ Teach-Back Tool 5: https://www.ahrq.gov/health-literacy/improve/precautions/tool5.html (checking understanding without "Do you understand?").
- MINT, Understanding MI: https://motivationalinterviewing.org/understanding-motivational-interviewing (OARS and what MI is not).
- PON, Listening Skills for Maximum Success: https://www.pon.harvard.edu/daily/negotiation-skills-daily/listening-skills-for-maximum-success/ (paraphrase, inquiry, acknowledgment).
- Smithsonian Institution Archives, How to Do Oral History: https://siarchives.si.edu/sites/default/files/pdfs/How_to_Do_Oral_History_Guide_2020.pdf (practical question list; silence; follow-ups).
- StoryCorps Great Questions: https://storycorps.org/participate/great-questions/ (question bank for talking with older relatives).
- Poynter, 12 basics of interviewing: https://www.poynter.org/reporting-editing/2015/12-basics-of-interviewing-listening-and-note-taking/ (a journalist's short list).
- Essential Partners, Fostering Dialogue Across Divides: https://whatisessential.org/sites/default/files/inline-images/FDAD/FDAD-EssentialPartners-2018.pdf (communication agreements, go-rounds, pauses).
- Braver Angels, reconciliation article with LAPP: https://braverangels.org/how-we-can-reconcile-with-each-other-when-our-politics-are-so-polarized/ (LAPP in four lines).
- Yeomans et al. 2020, free PDF: https://receptiveness.net/assets/papers/Conversational.pdf (receptiveness recipe).
- STAMMA, In conversation with someone who stammers: https://stamma.org/about-stammering/conversation-someone-stammers (what to do and not do).
- RNID, How to communicate with someone who is deaf or has hearing loss: https://rnid.org.uk/information-and-support/how-to-communicate-with-deaf-people-hearing-loss/ (practical tips).
- Google re:Work, Understand team effectiveness: https://rework.withgoogle.com/intl/en/guides/understand-team-effectiveness (psychological safety, from the source).
